3.1
Tools for Analyzing Graph Matrices

In this section, we will describe some tools that we use to analyze graph matrices.

3.1.1
Separators and Norm Bounds

Unless otherwise specified, paths in this paper include length-0 (degenerate) ones.

Definition 3.10

(Vertex separators)
.

Let
\alpha=(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha})
be a shape. We say that
S\subseteq V(\alpha)
is a vertex separator of
\alpha
if every path from
I
to
J
contains at least one vertex in
S
.

As observed in previous work
[
AMP21
,
GJJ+20
]
, the norms of graph matrices are determined by their minimum weight vertex separator. That said, to give an approximate PSD decomposition
M\approx LQL^{\top}
of our moment matrix, we will need a new kind of minimum vertex separator, namely minimum square separators.

Definition 3.11

(Minimum weight and minimum square separators)
.

Given a shape
\alpha
, we say that a vertex separator
S
is a
minimum weight vertex separator
if
w(S)
is minimized, i.e., there is no vertex separator
S^{\prime}
such that
w(S^{\prime})<w(S)
.

Given a shape
\alpha
such that
I\cup J
only contains square vertices, we say that a vertex separator
S
is a
square separator
if it contains only square vertices, and it is a
minimum square separator
if furthermore
|S|
is minimized, i.e., there is no square separator
S^{\prime}
such that
|S^{\prime}|<|S|
.

By a standard argument, both the set of minimum weight separators and the set of minimum square separators form a lattice. The proof of the following two lemmas is in
Section

7
.

Lemma 3.12

(Menger’s theorem for bipartite graphs)
.

For any shape
\alpha
, the maximum number of square disjoint paths is equal to the size of the minimum square separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
.

Definition 3.13
.

Given a shape
\alpha
and vertex separators
S,T
, we say that
S
is to the left of
T
if
S
separates
U_{\alpha}
and
T
and we say that
S
is to the right of
T
if
S
separates
V_{\alpha}
and
T
.

Lemma 3.14

(Existence of leftmost and rightmost minimum vertex separators)
.

For all shapes
\alpha
, there exist unique leftmost and rightmost minimum weight separators of
\alpha
. Similarly, for all shapes
\alpha
, there exist unique leftmost and rightmost minimum square separators of
\alpha
.

Theorem 3.15

(Norm bounds; see e.g. Lemma A.3 of
[
GJJ+20
]
)
.

There is a universal constant
C
such that with probability
1-o_{n}(1)
,
the following holds simultaneously for all shapes
\alpha
which have no multi-edges and have total size at most
n
.

\lVert M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq\Big[\Big(1+w(E(\alpha))\Big)\cdot|V_{\alpha}|\cdot\log(mn)\Big]^{C\big(\big|V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})\big|+w(E(\alpha))\big)}n^{\frac{w(V_{\alpha})+w(I_{s}(\alpha))-w(S_{min})}{2}},

(13)

where
S_{min}
is any choice of the minimum weight vertex separator of
\alpha
.

3.1.2
Linearizing Ribbons and Shapes with Multi-Edges

For our analysis, we will need to consider ribbons and shapes with multi-edges. To handle ribbons with multi-edges, we reduce each multi-edge to a linear combination of labeled edges.

Definition 3.16
.

Given a ribbon
R
with multi-edges, we obtain its linearization as follows. For a multi-edge
e
with labels
l_{1},\ldots,l_{m}
, we replace it with a linear combination of edges with labels
0,\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{m}{l_{m}}
where the coefficient of the edge with label
j
is the coefficient of
h_{j}
in the product
\prod_{j=1}^{m}{h_{l_{m}}}
. We treat an edge with label 0 as a non-edge.

Example 1
.

If we have a double edge then this becomes a linear combination of an edge with label
2
plus an edge with label
0
(which vanishes) as
x^{2}=(x^{2}-1)+1=h_{2}(x)+h_{0}
.

Example 2
.

If we have a multi-edge consisting of edges with labels
1
,
2
, and
3
then we would replace this multi-edge with a linear combination of four edges with labels
6
,
4
,
2
, and
0
(the edge with label
0
vanishes) and coefficients
1
,
11
,
24
, and
6
respectively as

\displaystyle(x^{3}-3x)(x^{2}-1)x

\displaystyle=x^{6}-4x^{4}+3x^{2}

\displaystyle=(x^{6}-15x^{4}+45x^{2}-15)+11(x^{4}-6x^{2}+3)+24(x^{2}-1)+6

To linearize the multi-edges of a graph matrix
M_{\alpha}
where
\alpha
has multi-edges, we use
Definition

3.16
repeatedly to replace each ribbon with multi-edges by its linearizations. For a ribbon
R
of shape
\alpha
, this creates a DAG-like process where
R
is the top node, and each non-leaf node is a ribbon
R^{\prime}
which still has multi-edges and continues to expand to its children. The edge from node
R^{\prime}
to a child records the coefficient of that child in this step of expanding
R^{\prime}
, and the leafs are ribbons with no multi-edges. The ribbon
R
will be replaced by the sum over all sinks, each with a coefficient equal to the sum over all paths from the root to this sink where each path contributes the product of all coefficients on it.

Note that the final expansion
\sum_{i}{c_{i}}R_{i}
depends only on
R
but not the order in the expansion process. Also, if we sum over all
R
of shape
\alpha
, the result is invariant under vertex permutations. Thus, each shape
\alpha
uniquely expands to a sum of shapes having no multi-edges.

The expansion of
\alpha
can also be described step-wise on the shape level. This requires a bit more care in adjusting the coefficient of the resulting shapes, as the number of ways of realizing a shape can change after a multi-edge-replacement step, due to the change in the size of the automorphism group for the shape. In any case, we have the following.

Proposition 3.17

(Expanding multi-edges, cf. Prop. 5.29–5.31 of
[
GJJ+20
]
)
.

Let
\alpha
be an improper shape, and let
P
be the set of proper shapes that can be obtained by expanding
\alpha
. Then there are coefficients
|c_{\gamma}|\leq C_{\text{Fourier}}\cdot C_{\text{Aut}}
such that

M_{\alpha}=\sum_{\gamma\in P}c_{\gamma}M_{\gamma}

(14)

where
C_{\text{Fourier}}
(“Hermite” in our case) is an upper bound on the largest absolute value of the coefficients of Hermite polynomials in the expansion of a product of any Hermite polynomials in the process described above, and
C_{\text{Aut}}=\max_{\gamma\in P}\frac{|Aut(\gamma)|}{|Aut(\alpha)|}
.

The magnitudes of
C_{\text{Aut}}
and
C_{\text{Fourier}}
can be bounded as follows.

Lemma 3.18

(Bounds on
C_{\text{Aut}}
and
C_{\text{Fourier}}
, cf. Propositions. 5.30 and 5.31 of
[
GJJ+20
]
)
.

1.

If
l_{1}\leq\cdots\leq l_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
l:=l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k}>1
, then in the Hermite expansion of
h_{l_{1}}(z)\cdots h_{l_{k}}(z)
, the maximum magnitude of a coefficient is no more than
(2l)^{l-l_{k}}
.

2.

If
\alpha^{\prime}
is obtained from
\alpha
by adding an edge, deleting an edge, or changing the label of an edge then
\frac{|Aut(\alpha^{\prime})|}{|Aut(\alpha)|}\leq{|V(\alpha)|}^{2}
.

Using
Lemma

3.18
and
Proposition

3.17
, we get the following corollary of
Theorem

3.15
.

Corollary 3.19
.

If the constant
C
in
Theorem

3.15
is chosen to be sufficiently large, then for any improper shape
\alpha
with multi-edges, the same upper bound there also upper bounds
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in P}\lVert c_{\gamma}M_{\gamma}\rVert
from the expansion (
14
). In particular, the norm bound in
Theorem

3.15
also holds for improper shapes with multi-edges.

For convenience, we choose the universal constant
C
to be large enough so that
Corollary

3.19
applies. We denote this constant by
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
in the rest of the paper.

3.1.3
Decomposition of Shapes

In our analysis, we will decompose each shape into three parts based on the leftmost and rightmost minimum square separators.

Definition 3.20

(Left and right shapes)
.

We say that
\sigma=(V(\sigma),E(\sigma),U_{\sigma},V_{\sigma})
is a left shape if
V_{\sigma}
is the unique minimum square separator of
\sigma
and every vertex in
V(\sigma)\setminus V_{\sigma}
is reachable by a path from
U_{\sigma}
without passing through any vertices in
V_{\sigma}
. We say that
\sigma
is a right shape if
\sigma^{\top}
is a left shape.

Definition 3.21

(Middle shapes)
.

We say that
\tau=(V(\tau),E(\tau),U_{\tau},V_{\tau})
is a middle shape if
U_{\tau}
and
V_{\tau}
are minimum square separators of
\tau
. A
proper middle shape
is a middle shape that is also proper.

We define left, right, and middle ribbons according to their shapes.

Definition 3.22

(Square canonical decomposition)
.

Given a shape
\alpha=(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha})
we decompose it into a triple of shapes
(\alpha_{l},\alpha_{m},\alpha_{r})
as follows. Let
S_{l}
,
S_{r}
be the leftmost and rightmost minimum square separators of
\alpha
respectively.

1.

The left shape of
\alpha
,
\alpha_{l}
, is the part of
\alpha
between
U_{\alpha}
and
S_{l}
. That is,
\alpha_{l}=(V(\alpha_{l}),E(\alpha_{l}),U_{\alpha},S_{l})
where
V(\alpha_{l})
and
E(\alpha_{l})
are the vertices and edges of
\alpha
which are reachable from
U_{\alpha}
without passing through a vertex in
S_{l}
. Note that
V(\alpha_{l})
includes the vertices in
S_{l}
.

2.

The right shape of
\alpha
,
\alpha_{r}
, is the part of
\alpha
between
S_{r}
and
V_{\alpha}
. That is,
\alpha_{l}=(V(\alpha_{r}),E(\alpha_{r}),S_{r},V_{\alpha})
where
V(\alpha_{r})
and
E(\alpha_{r})
are the vertices and edges of
\alpha
which are reachable from
V_{\alpha}
without passing through a vertex in
S_{r}
. Note that
V(\alpha_{r})
includes the vertices in
S_{r}
.

3.

The middle shape of
\alpha
,
\alpha_{m}
, is the remaining part of
\alpha
together with the vertices in
S_{l}
and
S_{r}
. That is,
\alpha_{m}=(V(\alpha_{m}),E(\alpha_{m}),S_{l},S_{r})
where
V(\alpha_{m})
and
E(\alpha_{m})
are the vertices and edges of
\alpha
which cannot be reached from
U_{\alpha}
without touching a vertex in
S_{l}
and which cannot be reached from
V_{\alpha}
without touching a vertex in
S_{r}
.

We call
(\alpha_{l},\alpha_{m},\alpha_{r})
the square canonical decomposition of
\alpha
.

Proposition 3.23
.

If
\alpha
is a proper shape then
\alpha_{l}
is a proper left shape,
\alpha_{m}
is a proper middle shape, and
\alpha_{r}
is a proper right shape.

u_{2}
u_{3}
w_{1}
x
w_{2}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}

u_{2}

w_{1}
x_{1}

x_{3}

S_{l}
and
S_{r}
are shown with dotted ovals, and assuming
n\leq m\leq n^{2}
, a minimum weight vertex separator is shown in red.

\approx

This figure shows the approximate decomposition of
S(2,2;0)
using minimum weight vertex separators (assuming that
m\leq n^{2}
). While natural, this decomposition
does not work
for our analysis.

Remark 3.24
.

A natural attempt is to use minimum weight vertex separators for the decomposition of shapes into left, middle, and right parts. However, this decomposition is not the right one for our PSDness analysis. In particular, one property we want for our PSDness analysis is that square terms (i.e., terms which are composed of a left shape and its transpose) have a positive coefficient. However, the coefficient of
S(2,2;0)
(which is a square term with this decomposition) is
l_{4}
which can be negative. For an illustration, see
Figure

4
.

3.1.4
Indices With Parity Labels

One complication for the matrix
M
is the parity condition on the total degree of a square vertex in (
12
).
This means that if we factor
M
as
XY
where
X
,
Y
are linear combinations of graph matrices, then in the multiplication
M_{\alpha}M_{\beta}
where
M_{A}\in X
and
M_{B}\in Y
, we had better match the parities of
\deg_{\alpha}(v)
and
\deg_{\beta}(v)
for all
v\in V_{\alpha}=U_{\beta}
. For this reason, we refine Definition
3.2
with a parity labeling as follows.

Definition 3.25

(Modified graph matrix definitions with parities)
.

1.

(Matrix indices) A matrix index is a pair
(I,a)
where
I\subseteq\mathcal{S}
and
a:I\to\{odd,even\}
is a function we call the parity labeling on
I
.

2.

(Ribbons, shapes) A ribbon
R
is defined as before except the two indices are now
(U_{R},a_{R}),(V_{R},b_{R})
where
a_{R}:U_{R}\to\{odd,even\}
and
b_{R}:V_{R}\to\{odd,even\}
are parity labels such that

(a)

For each vertex
u\in U_{R}\setminus V_{R}
,
a_{R}
maps
u
to the parity of its
total degree
in
R
. Similarly, each vertex
v\in V_{R}\setminus U_{R}
,
b_{R}
maps
v
to the parity of its
total degree
in
R
.

(b)

For each vertex
u\in U_{R}\cap V_{R}
, if the
total degree
of
u
is even then
a_{R}
and
b_{R}
map
u
to the same parity and if the
total degree
of
u
is odd then
a_{R}
and
b_{R}
map
u
to opposite parities.

In other words, for each vertex
u\in U_{R}\cup V_{R}
, the sum of the parity label(s) for
u
is the parity of the
total degree
of
u
.

We modify the definition of a shape similarly.

3.

(Shape of a ribbon) We say a ribbon
R
has shape
\alpha
if there is a bijection
\pi:V(\alpha)\to V(R)
that satisfies the previous conditions plus
a_{\alpha}=a_{R}\circ\pi
and
b_{\alpha}=b_{R}\circ\pi
.

4.

(Ribbon matrices, graph matrices) A ribbon matrix
M_{R}
is defined by (
9
) except now the only nonzero element is at position
\big((I,a),(J,b)\big)=\big((U_{R},a_{R}),(V_{R},b_{R})\big)
.
The graph matrix
M_{\alpha}
is defined to be
\sum\limits_{R:\text{ has shape }\alpha}M_{R}
.
For each shape
\alpha
, we associate the scaled graph matrix
n^{-{w(E(\alpha))\over 2}}M_{\alpha}
to it.

5.

(Canonical decomposition) When decomposing a shape
\alpha
into
(\alpha_{l},\alpha_{m},\alpha_{r})
, the parity labelings on indices of
\alpha_{l}
,
\alpha_{m}
,
\alpha_{r}
are decided by the shapes themselves via
Item

2
. Note the parity labelings on
U_{\alpha}
are the same in
\alpha_{l}
and
\alpha
, and similarly for
V_{\alpha}
in
\alpha_{r}
and
\alpha
.

6.

(Transpose) For
\alpha=\big(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),(U_{\alpha},a_{\alpha}),(V_{\alpha},b_{\alpha})\big)
,
\alpha^{\top}:=\big(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),(V_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}),(U_{\alpha},a_{\alpha})\big)
.

Other concepts about ribbons and shapes (size, weight, middle vertices, isolated vertices, total size, trivial shapes, left/right/middle shapes) are defined in the same way as before.

While adding the parities is important for describing the correct factorization, its impact on the analysis turns out to be superficial. For the remainder of the paper, we use indices with parities but sweep the parities under the rug. That said, we give explanations at necessary places. The first one is the following.

Remark 3.26

(Norm bounds for shapes with parities)
.

The norm bounds in
Theorem

3.15
and
Corollary

3.19
also hold in the setting with parities.
For clarity, in this remark, we distinguish the previous and current objects by the superscript “parity”.
For fixed
\alpha
,
Item

2
implies that ribbons
R
of shape
\alpha
are in 1-1 correspondence to ribbons
R^{\mathrm{parity}}
of shape
\alpha^{\mathrm{parity}}
. The nonzero Fourier characters in
M_{\alpha}
are also in 1-1 correspondence to those in
M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}
, although their relative positions can be different: it can happen that some
R_{1},R_{2}
have the same matrix indices while
R_{1}^{\mathrm{parity}},R_{2}^{\mathrm{parity}}
have different ones. The trace power estimate for proving
Theorem

3.15
applies to
M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}
, since for all
t
, any product
R^{\mathrm{parity}}_{1}\cdots R^{\mathrm{parity}}_{2t}
in
\left(M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}\cdot\big(M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}\big)^{\top}\right)^{t}
is a product in
\left(M_{\alpha}\cdot M_{\alpha}^{\top}\right)^{t}
by “forgetting” the parity, with the same expression in the resulting entry. Note the converse is not true: some products in
\left(M_{\alpha}\cdot M_{\alpha}^{\top}\right)^{t}
may be non-multiplicable in
\left(M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}\cdot\big(M_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{parity}}\big)^{\top}\right)^{t}
because of unmatched matrix indices. Given
Theorem

3.15
,
Corollary

3.19
follows by applying
Proposition

3.17
and
Lemma

3.18
as before.

3.1.5
Approximate Decomposition of
M

Definition 3.27

(Composable)
.

We say that ribbons
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are composable if
U_{R_{2}}=V_{R_{1}}
. More generally, we say that a ribbon sequence
R_{1},\ldots,R_{j}
is composable if
U_{R_{i+1}}=V_{R_{i}}
for all
i\in[j-1]
. We make an analogous definition for shapes. Note that being composable is not a commutative property.

Definition 3.28

(Ribbon composition)
.

We call a composable ribbon sequence
R_{1},\ldots,R_{j}
a
ribbon composition
or
ribbon product
. The
result

R
of the ribbon composition is the graph union, which is viewed as a ribbon that can have multi-edges and has
U_{R}=U_{R_{1}}
,
V_{R}=V_{R_{j}}
.

Definition 3.29

(Properly composable; extra intersections)
.

We say that ribbons
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are properly composable, if they are composable and
V(R_{1})\cap V(R_{2})=V_{R_{1}}=U_{R_{2}}
.
More generally, we say that ribbons
R_{1},\ldots,R_{j}
are properly composable, if they are composable and the
pairwise extra intersections
, which we sometimes also refer to as the
pairwise non-trivial intersections
,
V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{i_{1}},R_{i_{2}}):=\Big(V(R_{i_{1}})\cap V(R_{i_{2}})\Big)\backslash\Big(\mathop{\bigcap}\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[i_{1}+1,i_{2}]}{U_{R_{i^{\prime}}}}\Big)
are empty for all
i_{1}<i_{2}
.

Definition 3.30

(Canonical product)
.

Given shapes
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{j}
, we define the canonical product

[M_{\alpha_{1}},M_{\alpha_{2}},\ldots,M_{\alpha_{j}}]_{\mathrm{can}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(R_{1},\ldots,R_{j}):\\
R_{1},\ldots,R_{j}\text{ are properly composable and have shapes }\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j}\end{subarray}}{M_{R_{1}}M_{R_{2}}\ldots{M_{R_{j}}}}

which is
0
if the shapes are not composable. We extend
[\ldots]_{\mathrm{can}}
to linear combinations of graph matrices by making it a multi-linear operator.

Definition 3.31

(Matrix
L
)
.

We define

L=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma:\\
\sigma\text{ is a proper left shape, all square vertices in $V(\sigma)\setminus V_{\sigma}$ have even total degree;}\\
|U_{\sigma}|\leq D,\ |V_{\sigma}|\leq D,\ |\mathrm{total}(\sigma)|\leq D_{trunc}\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\sigma}(\lambda_{\sigma}M_{\sigma})}.

Definition 3.32

(Matrix
Q_{0}
)
.

We define

Q_{0}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau:\\
\tau\text{ is a proper middle shape},\text{ all square vertices in $V(\tau)\setminus(U_{\tau}\cup V_{\tau})$ have even total degree};\\
|U_{\tau}|\leq D,\ |V_{\tau}|\leq D,\ |\mathrm{total}(\sigma)|\leq D_{trunc}\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\tau}(\lambda_{\tau}M_{\tau})}.

Remark 3.33

(Parity labels for
L
and
Q_{0}
)
.

For left shapes
\sigma
in
L
, we define the parity labeling
a_{\sigma}
on
U_{\sigma}
to be all even; note that this automatically determines
b_{\sigma}
on
V_{\sigma}
.
Similarly, for right shapes
\sigma^{\top}
in
L^{\top}
, we define the parity labeling
b_{\sigma^{\top}}
on
V_{\sigma^{\top}}
to be all even.

For middle shapes
\tau\in Q_{0}
, there are two possibilities for the parity labels of each vertex
u\in U_{\sigma}\cap V_{\sigma}
and we take both of these possibilities. Technically, this gives
2^{|U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}|}
different terms for
\tau
but we group (sum) these terms together and think of them as one term.
The matrix norm bounds
Theorem

3.15
and
Corollary

3.19
apply to this sum as well. To see this, note that both the matrix row set and matrix column set can be partitioned into
2^{|U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}|}
subsets such that each term is supported on a distinct row subset and a distinct column subset, so the norm of the sum is at most the maximum norm of the individual terms.

We observe that with the parity labels,
M=[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}
up to a truncation error.
In particular, the only terms appearing in
[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-M
are terms where
|\mathrm{total}(\alpha_{l})|\leq D_{trunc}
,
|\mathrm{total}(\alpha_{m})|\leq D_{trunc}
, and
|\mathrm{total}(\alpha_{r})|\leq D_{trunc}
but
|\mathrm{total}(\alpha)|>D_{trunc}
. In
Section

7
, we show that
\lVert M-[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\rVert\leq n^{-\Omega(\varepsilon D_{trunc})}
(
Lemma

7.55
).

3.2
Intersection Configurations and Finding
Q

To find a
Q
such that
M=LQL^{\top}
, we use a recursive procedure as in
[
BHK+16
,
Pan21
,
JPR+21
,
JPRX23
,
KPX24
]
.
We start with
M_{target}=M
and
i=0
, then do the following:

Definition 3.34

(Recursive factorization)
.

The recursive factorization of the moment matrix
M
(see
Definition

2.6
) goes as follows.
In round
i=0,1,\dots,2D
,

1.

We choose
Q_{i}
so that
[L,Q_{i},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}=M_{\mathrm{target}}
up to a truncation error;

2.

We update
M_{\mathrm{target}}\leftarrow M_{\mathrm{target}}-L{Q_{i}}L^{\top}
,
i\leftarrow i+1
.

We repeat the procedure and show that it is guaranteed to terminate in
2D
steps i.e.,
M_{\mathrm{target}}
becomes 0. Finally, we take
Q=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{2D}{Q_{i}}
.

Each
Q_{i}
can be understood as follows. For simplicity, we will ignore truncation errors for now by writing “
\approx
” until
Definition

3.41
. First, note that
[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]\approx M
. Then after round
i
,
M_{\mathrm{target}}\approx[L,Q_{i},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQ_{i}L^{\top}
. That is,
M_{\mathrm{target}}
is minus one times the sum of all non-canonical products (with coefficients) in
L{Q_{i}}L^{\top}
up to a truncation error.

To find a
Q_{i}
that fulfills item 1 in
Definition

3.34
, for each non-canonical product
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
in
L{Q_{i-1}}L^{\top}
, we construct a tuple
(R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
such that
M_{R_{1}}M_{R_{2}}M_{R_{3}}=M_{R^{\prime}_{1}}M_{R^{\prime}_{2}}M_{R^{\prime}_{3}}
, the shape of
R^{\prime}_{1}
is a left shape, the shape of
R^{\prime}_{3}
is a right shape, and
R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3}
are properly composable. We find the tuple
(R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
as follows:

Definition 3.35

(Separating decomposition)
.

1.

Let
A^{\prime}
be the leftmost minimum
square
separator of
A_{R_{1}}
and
B_{R_{1}}\cup V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{1},R_{2})\cup V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{1},R_{3})
, where
V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{i},R_{j})
is the set of vertices involved in extra intersections between
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
. Similarly, let
B^{\prime}
be the rightmost minimum
square
separator of
A_{R_{3}}\cup V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{1},R_{3})\cup V_{\mathrm{ext}}(R_{2},R_{3})
and
B_{R_{3}}
in
R_{3}

2.

Let
R^{\prime}_{1}
be the part of
R_{1}
between
A_{R_{1}}
and
A^{\prime}
(cf.
Definition

3.22
), and let
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
be the part of
R_{1}
between
A^{\prime}
and
B_{R_{1}}
. Similarly, let
R^{\prime}_{3}
be the part of
R_{3}
between
B^{\prime}
and
B_{R_{3}}
, and let
R^{\prime\prime}_{3}
be the part of
R_{3}
between
A_{R_{3}}
and
B^{\prime}
.

3.

We define
R^{\prime}_{2}
to be the composition of
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
R_{2}
, and
R^{\prime\prime}_{3}
. Note that
R^{\prime}_{2}
might be improper.

4.

We choose the parities for vertices in
A^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}
in
R^{\prime}_{2}
to match the parities of their total degrees in
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{3}
.

\times
u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
u_{4}
u_{5}
w_{1}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
w_{2}
v
v
w_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
w_{4}
v^{\prime}_{1}
v^{\prime}_{2}
v^{\prime}_{3}
v^{\prime}_{4}
v^{\prime}_{5}

Figure 5:
An intersection configuration for the product
M_{\sigma}M_{\sigma^{\top}}
where
\sigma
is the left shape shown on the left. The orange arch between shape vertices
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
means that the two vertices coincide in the ribbon products being represented. The dotted set of vertices in
\sigma
is the leftmost minimum square separator between
U_{\sigma}
and the union of the set of intersected vertices and
V_{\sigma}
. Similarly the dotted set of vertices in
\sigma^{\top}
is the rightmost minimum vertex separator between the union of
U_{\sigma^{\top}}
and the set of the intersected vertices and
V_{\sigma^{\top}}
.

u_{2}
u_{3}
u_{4}
u_{5}

x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
w_{2}
v
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
w_{4}

v^{\prime}_{2}

v^{\prime}_{4}

Result of
Figure

5
represented by a multi-edged shape. All edges have label 1 and there are two edges between
v,w_{2}
.

We take
Q_{i}
to be the sum of the ribbons
R^{\prime}_{2}
with the appropriate coefficients.

Remark 3.36
.

Note that the ribbon
R^{\prime}_{2}
and its coefficient is independent of
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{3}
, i.e., it only depends on
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
R_{2}
, and
R^{\prime\prime}_{3}
. As in previous SoS lower bounds for average case problems, this property is crucial for the factorization of
M
.

We now write down an explicit expression for
Q_{j}
using intersection configurations. In particular, for all
j\geq 1
we will have that

Q_{j}\approx(-1)^{j}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}:\ \text{an intersection configuration on shapes}\\
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}

where recall that
\approx
means ignoring a truncation error, and:

1.

\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are the shapes of composable ribbons that non-trivially intersect.
Here, each shape has total size at most
D_{trunc}
, each index set has size at most
D
, each square vertex has even total degree, each
\gamma_{i}
is a proper left shape, each
\gamma_{i}^{\prime\top}
is a proper right shape, and
\tau
is a proper middle shape.
We will sometimes abbreviate these conditions as “each
\gamma_{i}
is in
L
,
\tau
is in
Q_{0}
, and each
\gamma^{\prime\top}_{i}
is in
L^{\top}
.”

2.

The
intersection configuration

\mathcal{P}
(
Definition

3.37
) describes the intersections between the shapes and what happens to the resulting multi-edges (if any).
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
is the shape of the composition.

3.

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
is the product of the scaling coefficients of all shapes in
\mathcal{P}
.

4.

\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
is the part of coefficient of
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
coming from the Hermite coefficients of
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and the linearization of the multi-edges.

5.

N(\mathcal{P})
is a combinatorial factor that counts the number of different ways to obtain a ribbon
R
of shape
\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
from all possible ribbon realizations of an intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
.

The following definitions can be quite technical, please see
Figures

5
and
6
for an example.

Definition 3.37

(Intersection configurations)
.

Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
together with proper left shapes
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1}
,
a proper middle shape
\tau
, and proper right shapes
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
such that
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are composable and for each
i\in[j]
,
\gamma_{i}
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
are not both trivial, we define an intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
on
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
to consist of the following data.

First,
\mathcal{P}
specifies intersection configurations
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{j})
such that:

1.

\mathcal{P}_{1}
specifies the matching between the indices in
V_{\gamma_{1}}
and
U_{\tau}
and the matching between the indices in
V_{\tau}
and
U_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1}}
.
\mathcal{P}_{1}
also specifies the non-trivial intersections between
\gamma_{1}
,
\tau
, and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1}
.

2.

For each
i\in[2,j]
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
specifies the matching between the indices in
V_{\gamma_{i}}
and
U_{\gamma_{i-1}}
and the matching between the indices in
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i-1}}
and
U_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}
.
\mathcal{P}_{i}
also specifies the non-trivial intersections between
\gamma_{i}
,
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}
, and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
where
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}
is the result of applying the intersection configurations
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{i-1}
to
\gamma_{i-1},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i-1}
. In other words,
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}
is the shape which results from taking the composition of
\gamma_{i-1},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i-1}
given by the matchings in
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{i-1}
and merging all pairs of vertices which are intersected by
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{i-1}
. Note that
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}
may be an improper shape as it may have multi-edges.

3.

(Intersection-separating condition)
For all
i\in[j]
,
U_{\gamma_{i}}
is the minimum square vertex separator in
\gamma_{i}
between
U_{\gamma_{i}}
and
\{\text{non-trivial intersections specified by }\mathcal{P}_{i}\}\cup V_{\gamma_{i}}
. Similarly,
V_{\gamma_{i}}
is the minimum square vertex separator in
\gamma^{\prime\top}_{i}
between
U_{\gamma_{i}}\cup\{\text{non-trivial intersections specified by }\mathcal{P}_{i}\}
and
V_{\gamma_{i}}
.

Second, letting
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}}
be the shape which results from applying the intersection configurations
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{j}
to
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
, for each multi-edge
e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}})
,
\mathcal{P}
specifies a label
l_{e}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
describing a term which results from linearizing this multi-edge.

We define the shape
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
to be the result when we replace each multi-edge
e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}})
with a single edge with label
l_{e}
. Note that
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
may be an improper shape as some of the labels
l_{e}
may be
0
so
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
may contain isolated vertices.

We call
j
the length of
\mathcal{P}
, and
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\tau,\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}
the shapes in
\mathcal{P}
. The vertex set of
\mathcal{P}
is
V(P):=V(\gamma_{j})\cup\dots\cup V(\tau)\cup\ldots\cup V({\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j})
, where vertices in indexed sets that are matched by
\mathcal{P}
will be treated as the same but there is no other vertex identifications regardless of the non-trivial intersections specified by
\mathcal{P}
. Note that
V(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})
is different from
V(P)
. We view two intersection configurations
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
as the same if there are bijections between their vertices that maps
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, including the edge labels.

For the remainder of the paper, whenever we write
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
, we assume that

1.

\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1}
are proper left shapes that are in
L
.

2.

\tau
, is a proper middle shape that is in
Q_{0}
.

3.

{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are proper right shapes that are in
L^{\top}
.

4.

\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are composable.

5.

For each
i\in[j]
,
\gamma_{i}
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
are not both trivial.

Definition 3.38

(Configuration coefficients)
.

Given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
, we let
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
denote the set of all different intersection configurations on
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
. For
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
,

1.

We define the scaling coefficient
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
to be

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\lambda_{\gamma_{i}}}\right)\lambda_{\tau}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\lambda_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}}\right)
.

2.

We define the Hermite coefficient
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
for
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
to be

\eta_{\mathcal{P}}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{\gamma_{i}}}\right)\eta_{\tau}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}}\right)\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}})}{(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)}.

3.

There may be many different ways to obtain a ribbon realization of an intersection configuration given the resulting ribbon. To handle this, given a ribbon
R
of shape
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
, we define
N(\mathcal{P})
to be the number of ways to specify the following data so that the induced intersection configuration is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}
and the resulting ribbon is
R
.

1.

We have a tuple
(R_{-j},\ldots,R_{-1},R_{0},R_{1},\ldots,R_{j})
having shapes
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
.

2.

Letting
R^{\prime}
be the ribbon of shape
\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}}

such that
\prod_{i=-j}^{j}{M_{R_{i}}}=M_{R^{\prime}}
, we have a map from the multi-edges
e\in E(R^{\prime})
to
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
specifying which term to take when we linearize
e
.

Remark 3.39
.

Computing
N(\mathcal{P})
can be tricky.
We only describe
N(\mathcal{P})
for well-behaved intersection configurations involving simple spiders in
Section

4.1
. For an upper bound on
N(\mathcal{P})
in general, see
Lemma

7.52
.

In order to avoid repeatedly writing out
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
in our sums, we make the following definition.

Definition 3.40
.

We define
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}
to be the set of all intersection configurations of length
j
. Note that when we sum over
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}
, we are implicitly summing over the possible
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
.

With these definitions, up to truncation error, we can describe
Q_{j}

(j\geq 1)
by:

Q_{j}\approx(-1)^{j}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}.

We now describe and handle the truncation errors in a precise manner.
The next two definitions are technical and are used to make sure that (
15
) holds.

Definition 3.41

(
L_{\leq t}
)
.

We define
L_{\leq t}=\sum\limits_{\sigma\text{ in }L:\ \mathrm{total}(\sigma)\leq t}{\eta_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}M_{\sigma}}
.
Note that
L=L_{\leq D_{trunc}}
.

Definition 3.42

(
l_{\mathcal{P}}
,
r_{\mathcal{P}}
)
.

Given an intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
, we let

l_{\mathcal{P}}:=D_{trunc}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}{(\mathrm{total}(\gamma_{i})-|U_{\gamma_{i}}|)}
. Similarly, we let
r_{\mathcal{P}}:=D_{trunc}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}{(\mathrm{total}({\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i})-|V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}|)}
.

With these definitions, we have the following equation.

[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}=L{Q_{0}}L^{\top}+\sum_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}}

(15)

Based on this, we choose:

\displaystyle Q_{j}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}},\ l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|,\ r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|\end{subarray}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}},

(16)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{2D}{Q_{j}}.

(17)

We then have the following expression of the truncation error:

\displaystyle[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}

(18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}},\ l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|,\ r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})\left(L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}-{L}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}L^{\top}\right)}.

Remark 3.43
.

The condition
l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|
is equivalent to
\mathrm{total}(\gamma_{j}\circ\ldots\circ\gamma_{1})\leq D_{trunc}
,
where
\gamma_{j}\circ\ldots\circ\gamma_{1}
denotes the shape of the proper composition of ribbons
(R_{j},\ldots,R_{1})
where
R_{i}
has shape
\gamma_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,j\}
. Similarly, the condition
r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|
is equivalent to
\mathrm{total}({\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1}\circ\ldots\circ{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j})\leq D_{trunc}
.

In
Section

7
, we show that the truncation error (
18
) has norm at most
n^{-\Omega(\varepsilon D_{trunc})}
; see
Lemma

7.56
.

4
Well-Behaved Intersection Configurations and Spider Products

In this section, we analyze the terms in the matrix
Q
from the recursive factorization. In
Section

4.1
, we describe which terms of
Q
are the most important for our analysis, namely disjoint unions of simple spiders coming from well-behaved intersection configurations. In
Section

4.2
, we analyze products of simple spiders and disjoint unions of simple spiders. In
Section

4.3
, we derive a formula determining
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
. Finally, in
Section

4.4
, we give direct analyses for degree 4 and degree 6 SoS which are interesting in their own right and illustrate the challenges which we overcome.

4.1
Well-Behaved Intersection Configurations

It turns out that the dominant terms of
Q
in (
17
) are the terms coming from intersection configurations satisfying certain properties which we call well-behaved intersection configurations.

Definition 4.1

(Well-behaved intersection configuration)
.

Given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
, we say that
\mathcal{P}
is well-behaved if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are all simple spider disjoint unions (SSD).

2.

\mathcal{P}
has no non-trivial intersections between square vertices.

3.

Whenever there is a square vertex
v
which is not in
U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}\cup V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, there is an intersection between the two neighbors of
v
which results in a double edge in
E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}})
. For each such double edge
e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}_{j}})
, we have that
l_{e}=0
(i.e., the double edge vanishes).

Remark 4.2

(Results of well-behaved configurations)
.

Using the definition it can be checked that every well-behaved configuration results in an SSD shape together with some additional isolated vertices. For a formal proof, see item
(1)
in the proof of
Theorem

7.43
.

In
Section

7
, we show that the contribution to
Q
from intersection configurations which are not well-behaved is small. In particular, we show that

\sum_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\ {\mathcal{P}}\text{ is not well-behaved, $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}

(19)

is small (
Lemma

7.58
).

For each well-behaved intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
, we obtain a cleaner expression by deleting the isolated vertices from
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
and taking the leading order term of the resulting coefficient.

Definition 4.3

(Reduced coefficient)
.

Given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and well-behaved intersection configuration
{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
, we define the reduced shape
\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
to be the shape obtained by deleting all isolated middle vertices
I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})
from
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
. We set
\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}=n^{-\frac{|E(\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|}{2}}=n^{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
.

We observe that
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}=(1\pm\O(n^{-1}))\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}
. That is,

Proposition 4.4
.

Given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and a well-behaved
{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
,

\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}=\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\left(\frac{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|-1}{(n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|-i)}}{n^{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|}}\right)\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}.

Proof.

Notice that
I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})
consists of square vertices since
\mathcal{P}
is well-behaved.
The proposition then follows immediately from the facts that
\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}=n^{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|}\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
and
M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}=\binom{n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|}M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}
.
∎

Based on this, we define
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
to consist of all the terms in
Q
coming from well-behaved intersection configurations where
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
is replaced by
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}
.

Definition 4.5

(
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
)
.

We define

[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2D}{(-1)^{j}\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\textrm{ $\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved, $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}}}

(20)

In
Section

7
, we confirm that

\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{2D}{(-1)^{j}\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\text{ $\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved, $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|$}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\left({\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}\right)}}\rVert

(21)

is small (see Lemma
7.58
). Combining the two statements (
19
), (
21
), we have that
\lVert Q-[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\rVert
is small.

Finally, we can use the following remark to get rid of the condition on
(l_{\mathcal{P}},r_{\mathcal{P}})
in
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
.

Remark 4.6

(No truncation error from well-behaved configurations in
Q
)
.

For any well-behaved
\mathcal{P}
, each shape in
\mathcal{P}
is an SSD and thus has total size at most
6D
. Since at most
2D
of
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
can be non-trivial (as each non-trivial
\gamma_{i}
increases the size of
U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
by at least
1
and each non-trivial
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
increases the size of
V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
by at least
1
),
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq(2D+1)6D
. Therefore, as long as
D_{trunc}\geq 20D^{2}
, the conditions
l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|,\ r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}|
in the summation (
20
) can be dropped since they automatically hold for all well-behaved
P
there.

Corollary 4.7

(Simplified expression of
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
)
.

Let

[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}:=(-1)^{j}\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\textrm{ $\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}}\ \ \text{ for }j=0,\ldots,2D,

(22)

then we have
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2D}{[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}}
.

4.2
Spider Products

In order to analyze
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
, it is crucial to understand products of spiders and disjoint unions of simple spiders.

We start by considering the products of simple spiders, where we only take the products in which the circle vertices intersect and there are no non-trivial intersections for square vertices.

Lemma 4.8
.

For
u\leq\min\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\}
, if we take the terms from the scaled graph matrix product
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\cdot S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
where the circle vertices intersect and there are no non-trivial intersections between the square vertices, the result is approximately

\sum_{i=\max\{0,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i)

Proof.

The terms of the product
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\cdot S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
where the circle vertices intersect and there are no non-trivial intersections between the square vertices are given by pairs of ribbons
(R_{1},R_{2})
of shapes
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)
and
S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
such that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
have the same circle vertex
w
and the only square vertices which
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
have in common are
B_{R_{1}}=A_{R_{2}}
. For each such pair of ribbons
(R_{1},R_{2})
, letting
i=|A_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{2}}|
,

1.

There are
u-i
square vertices in
(A_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{1}})\setminus B_{R_{2}}
and
k_{1}-u
vertices in
A_{R_{1}}\setminus B_{R_{1}}
. These vertices are adjacent to
w
in
R_{2}
but not
R_{1}
.

2.

There are
v-i
vertices in
(A_{R_{2}}\cap B_{R_{2}})\setminus A_{R_{1}}
and
k_{3}-v
vertices in
B_{R_{2}}\setminus A_{R_{2}}
. These vertices are adjacent to
w
in
R_{1}
but not
R_{2}
.

3.

There are
k_{2}-(u-i)-(v-i)=k_{2}+i-u-v
vertices in
B_{R_{1}}\setminus(A_{R_{1}}\cup B_{R_{2}})
. These vertices are adjacent to
w
in both
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.

Observe that
M_{R_{1}}M_{R_{2}}=M_{R}
where
R
is the ribbon such that

1.

R
has the circle vertex
w
.

2.

A_{R}\cap B_{R}=A_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{2}}
. Note that
|A_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{2}}|=i
and none of these vertices are adjacent to
w
.

3.

A_{R}\setminus B_{R}=(A_{R_{1}}\setminus B_{R_{1}})\cup(A_{R_{1}}\cap B_{R_{1}}\setminus B_{R_{2}})
. Note that
|A_{R}\setminus B_{R}|=k_{1}-i
and all vertices in
A_{R}\setminus B_{R}
are adjacent to
w
.

4.

B_{R}\setminus A_{R}=(B_{R_{2}}\setminus A_{R_{2}})\cup(A_{R_{2}}\cap B_{R_{2}}\setminus A_{R_{1}})
. Note that
|B_{R}\setminus A_{R}|=k_{3}-i
and all vertices in
A_{R}\setminus B_{R}
are adjacent to
w_{3}
.

5.

V(R)\setminus(A_{R}\cup B_{R})=B_{R_{1}}\setminus(A_{R_{1}}\cup B_{R_{2}})
. Note that
|V(R)\setminus(A_{R}\cup B_{R})|=k_{2}+i-u-v
and each of the square vertices in
V(R)\setminus(A_{R}\cup B_{R})
has a double edge to
w
.

For each such ribbon
R
, we observe that there are
\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}
pairs of ribbons
(R_{1},R_{2})
which result in the ribbon
R
as there are
\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}
ways to choose which of the
k_{1}-i
indices in
A_{R}\setminus B_{R}
are in
A_{R_{1}}\setminus B_{R_{1}}
and there are
\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-u}
ways to choose which of the
k_{3}-i
indices in
B_{R}\setminus A_{R}
are in
B_{R_{2}}\setminus A_{R_{2}}
.

Finally, we note that following similar logic as the proof of Proposition
4.4
, deleting the
k_{2}+i-u-v
vertices in
V(R)\setminus(A_{R}\cup B_{R})
together with the double edges between these vertices and
w
and then shifting to the corresponding shape
S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i)
gives a factor of roughly
\frac{1}{|V(R)\setminus(A_{R}\cup B_{R})|!}=\frac{1}{(k_{2}+i-u-v)!}
.
∎

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
i
c_{1}
b_{2}
i
c_{2}
c_{3}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
i
b_{2}
c_{1}
c_{2}
c_{3}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
i
b_{1}
b_{2}
i
c_{1}
c_{2}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
i
b_{1}
b_{2}
c_{1}
c_{2}

Figure 7:
Dominant terms of the product
S(2,2;1)\cdot S(2,2;1)
.

Based on this, we define the following algebra.

Definition 4.9

(Simple Spider Algebra of degree
D
,
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
)
.

For fixed
D
, the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
has its underlying
\mathbb{R}
-vector space spanned by a symbol set
\{S(i,j;u)\mid\max\{i+u,j+u\}\leq D\}
, and its product, denoted by
\star
, is defined as

\displaystyle S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\star S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)

(23)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max\{0,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i),

extended linearly to all elements.

Remark 4.10

(Parity labels for simple spiders)
.

For the simple spider
\alpha
with
i
legs to the left,
j
legs to the right, and
u
vertices in
U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
, there are
2^{u}
possible parity labels for the vertices in
U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
. Technically, this gives
2^{u}
separate terms (where each term also incorporates the scaling coefficient
\lambda_{\alpha}
) but we group these terms together and use
S(i,j,u)
to denote the sum of these terms. It is not hard to check that this does not affect the argument in
Lemma

4.8
.

We now define a product
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
(“well-behaved products”) on disjoint unions of simple spiders. This product generalizes the
\star
-product, and is analogous to
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
in the sense that it only takes well-behaved product configurations (
Definition

4.13
) and then cleans up the coefficients.

In the following, we will abbreviate simple spider disjoint unions as SSD at places for spatial reasons.

Definition 4.11

(Product configuration)
.

Given composable shapes
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
which are disjoint unions of simple spiders, a product configuration
\mathcal{P}
on
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
consists of the following data.

1.

\mathcal{P}
specifies the matching between the indices in
V_{\alpha_{1}}
and
U_{\alpha_{2}}
.

2.

\mathcal{P}
specifies the non-trivial intersections between
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.

3.

Letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be the shape which results from composing
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
according to this matching and then merging the vertices which are intersected, for each multi-edge
e\in E(\tau_{{\mathcal{P}_{j}}})
,
\mathcal{P}
specifies a label
l_{e}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
describing a term which results from linearizing this multi-edge.

We define the shape
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
to be the result when we replace each multi-edge
e\in E(\alpha^{\prime})
with a single edge with label
l_{e}
.

We say that two product configurations
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are equivalent if there are permutations of
V(\alpha_{1})
and
V(\alpha_{2})
which map
\mathcal{P}
to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.12

(Product configuration coefficients)
.

Given composable
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
which are disjoint unions of simple spiders, we define
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
to be the set of non-equivalent possible product configurations on
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.

Given
{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
, we define coefficients for
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
as follows:

1.

The scaling coefficient
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
for
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
is
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}=\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}
.

2.

We define the Hermite coefficient
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
for
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
to be

\eta_{\mathcal{P}}=\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})}{(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)}.

3.

There can be many different ways to obtain a ribbon
R
of shape
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
from a product configuration
\mathcal{P}
.
For this reason, we define
N(\mathcal{P})
to be the number of ways to specify the following data so that the induced product configuration is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}
and the resulting ribbon is
R
.

(1)

We have a tuple of
(R_{1},R_{2})
of ribbons with shapes
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
.

(2)

Letting
R^{\prime}
be the ribbon of shape
\alpha^{\prime}
such that
M_{R_{1}}M_{R_{2}}=M_{R^{\prime}}
, we have a map from the multi-edges
e\in E(R^{\prime})
to
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
specifying which term to take when we linearize
e
.

Now we define well-behaved product configurations. Note that they refer to products of simple spider disjoint unions only.

Definition 4.13

(Well-behaved product configurations)
.

Given composable shapes
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
which are disjoint unions of simple spiders and a product configuration
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
, we say that
\mathcal{P}
is well-behaved if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\mathcal{P}
has no non-trivial intersections between square vertices.

2.

Whenever there is a square vertex
v
outside of
U_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}\cup V_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}
, there is an intersection between the two neighbors of
v
which results in a double edge in
E(\alpha^{\prime})
. For each such double edge
e\in E(\alpha)
, we have that
l_{e}=0
(i.e., the double edge vanishes).

Different vertices in a shape never get identified in a product, so the conditions in
Definition

4.13
imply that when two circles
u_{1}\in\alpha_{1},u_{2}\in\alpha_{2}
nontrivially intersect, all edges from
u_{1}
to
V_{\alpha_{1}}\backslash(U_{\alpha_{1}}\cup V_{\alpha_{2}})=U_{\alpha_{2}}\backslash(U_{\alpha_{1}}\cup V_{\alpha_{2}})
match all edges from
u_{2}
to the same set. We define the reduced shape
\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
to be
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
after removing these edges and the isolated vertices, with a coefficient
\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
that is the leading order term, as follows.

Definition 4.14

(Reduced shapes and coefficients)
.

For each well-behaved product configuration
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
, we define the reduced shape
\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
to be the shape obtained by deleting all isolated vertices from
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
. We set
\lambda_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}=n^{-\frac{|E(\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|}{2}}=n^{|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|}\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

As before, we observe that the dominant term of
{\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}
is
{\lambda_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|!}
. Based on this, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.15

(Well-behaved products)
.

Given composable
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
which are disjoint unions of simple spiders, we define

(\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}M_{\alpha_{1}})*_{\mathrm{wb}}(\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}M_{\alpha_{2}}):=\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}:\ \mathcal{P}\text{ is well-behaved}}{{\lambda_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|!}}

We extend this product multi-linearly to all linear combinations of disjoint unions of simple spiders.

Remark 4.16
.

As noted earlier, instead of always writing
\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
for well-behaved products, we will sometimes just write
\alpha
for convenience.

In
Section

7
, we show that if all non-trivial shaped simple spiders in
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are good, i.e., they have at least
\frac{k}{2}
legs to the left and right (see
Definition

5.3
),
then

\lVert\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}M_{\alpha_{1}}M_{\alpha_{2}}-(\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}M_{\alpha_{1}})*_{\mathrm{wb}}(\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}M_{\alpha_{2}})\rVert

is small.

Remark 4.17

(Range of approximation)
.

Note that in general,
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
does not always give the dominant terms. For example,
S(3,1,0)*_{\mathrm{wb}}S(1,3,0)=S(3,3,0)
but there are other shapes in
S(3,1,0)S(1,3,0)
with norm
\widetilde{O}(\frac{m}{n})\gg 1
, where we assume
k=4
,
\varepsilon
is small, and
m=n^{(1-\varepsilon)k/2}
.

Also, it is possible that the well-behaved product of two simple spiders
\alpha*_{\mathrm{wb}}\beta
contains more shapes than
\alpha\star\beta
. For example,
S(4,0;0)*_{\mathrm{wb}}S(0,4;4)
contains their canonical product which is an SSD but not a simple spider.

As a sanity check, we observe that when we restrict our attention to simple spiders,
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
reduces to the product
\star
for the simple spider algebra.

Lemma 4.18
.

\displaystyle{[S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)*_{\mathrm{wb}}S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)]}_{\text{SS}}=S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\star S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max\{0,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i)

Proof sketch.

Assume
\mathcal{P}
is a product configuration on
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)
and
S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
where there is an intersection between the circle vertices,
i
of the vertices in
U_{S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)}\cap V_{S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)}
are matched to vertices in
U_{S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)}\cap V_{S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)}
, and all double edges vanish.
We have that
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
is
S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i)
plus
k_{2}+i-u-v
isolated vertices, and
\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}=S(k_{1}-i,k_{3}-i;i)
. By the same logic as in
Lemma

4.8
, we have that
N(\mathcal{P})=\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}
so

\frac{N(\mathcal{P})}{|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|!}=\frac{\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}}{(k_{2}+i-u-v)!}
.
∎

In fact,
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
is also associative.

Lemma 4.19
.

The product
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
is associative.

Proof sketch.

When we compute
(\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2})*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3}
, we consider the products of ribbons
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
whose product configurations are well-behaved and then take the leading order terms. Since ribbon products are associative, one would expect
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
to be associative as well.

That said, we need to check that restricting to well-behaved product configurations and taking the leading order terms does not affect the associativity of ribbon products. To do this, we observe that when we have a sequence of ribbons whose product configuration is well-behaved, we perform the following types of operations in some order.

1.

Multiply ribbons together by composing them and merging intersected vertices.

2.

Delete a doubled edge incident to a square vertex, making this square vertex isolated.

3.

Delete isolated vertices and adjust the coefficients accordingly.

It is not hard to see that for a given sequence of ribbons, the order in which we delete doubled edges does not matter. The following lemma implies that we can delay deleting isolated vertices until the end and the result will stay the same.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
be composable shapes which are disjoint unions of simple spiders. Let
\alpha^{\prime}_{1}
be
\alpha_{1}
together with
x
isolated vertices and let
\alpha^{\prime}_{2}
be
\alpha_{2}
together with
y
isolated vertices. We take
\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}=\frac{1}{n^{x}}\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}
and
\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}}=\frac{1}{n^{y}}\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}

If we extend the definition of
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
to simple spider disjoint unions together with isolated square vertices (with no non-trivial intersection among the isolated vertices), then we have that

x!{\alpha}^{\prime}_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}y!{\alpha}^{\prime}_{2}=\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2}

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a well-behaved product configuration for
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
and let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the corresponding well-behaved product configuration for
\alpha^{\prime}_{1}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{2}
.

We claim that
N(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\frac{(x+y+|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|)!}{x!y!|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|!}N(\mathcal{P})
. To see this, observe that given a ribbon
R^{\prime}
of shape
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
, we can find ribbons
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
which have shapes
\alpha^{\prime}_{1}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{2}
, have product configuration
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, and result in
R^{\prime}
as follows.

1.

Choose
x
out of the
(x+y+|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|)!
isolated vertices in
R^{\prime}
to be in
R^{\prime}_{1}
.

2.

Choose
y
out of the remaining
(y+|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|)!
vertices

3.

After removing these isolated vertices, we are left with a ribbon
R
of shape
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
. There are now
N(\mathcal{P})
ways to choose
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
to have product configuration
\mathcal{P}
and result in
R
. Adding the previously chosen isolated vertices to
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
gives
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
.

We further observe that
\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}=n^{|I_{s}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})|}\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}=\frac{n^{|I_{s}(\mathcal{P})|+x+y}}{n^{x}n^{y}}\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}=\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}
. Thus, we have that

x!y!\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\frac{M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})|!}=\lambda_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\alpha_{P,{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(P)|!}.\qed

Since we can delay deleting isolated vertices until the end and the result will stay the same, we can compute the terms of
(\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2})*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3}
and
\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}(\alpha_{2}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3})
as follows:

1.

Multiply the ribbons together by composing them and merging intersected vertices.

2.

For each square vertex incident to a doubled edge, delete this doubled edge, making this square vertex isolated.

3.

Delete the isolated vertices and adjust the coefficients accordingly.

Here, the operations are done in the given order. The first step gives the same results for
(\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2})*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3}
and
\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}(\alpha_{2}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3})
so we have that
(\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2})*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}(\alpha_{2}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{3})
, as needed.
∎

From now on, by “well-behaved products” we always refer to well-behaved products, which includes well-behaved SS products by definition.

To conclude this sub-section, we summarize some relations of the definitions as follows. The shapes below are all simple spider disjoint unions.

1.

\alpha_{1}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha_{2}
is the sum of the resulting term of all well-behaved product configurations in
(\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}M_{\alpha_{1}})(\lambda_{\alpha_{2}}M_{\alpha_{2}})
.

2.

When we restrict our attention to simple spiders,
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
reduces to
\star
.

3.

To obtain the results of the well-behaved intersection configurations for
(\lambda_{\gamma}M_{\gamma})(\lambda_{\tau}M_{\tau})(\lambda_{\gamma^{\prime\top}}M_{\gamma^{\prime\top}})
, we take the terms from
\gamma*_{\mathrm{wb}}\tau*_{\mathrm{wb}}{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}
where every non-trivial simple spider in
\gamma
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}
has a non-trivial intersection.

In
Section

7
, we will show that the dominant terms in
Q_{i}
are disjoint unions of simple spiders. For now and the upcoming sections, we focus on these shapes in
Q
. We end this subsection with a general notation.

Definition 4.21

(Restrictions)
.

Except when
X=Q
or
X=Q_{i}
,
i=0,\ldots,2D
(see equations (
16
) and (
17
)), if
X=\sum_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
is a linear combination of graph matrices and
G
is a set of shapes, we use
X_{G}
to denote
\sum_{\alpha\in G}\lambda_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
. In particular, except for
X=Q
and
X=Q_{i}
,
{X}_{\text{SS}}
will denote the sum of the simple spider terms from
X
and
{X}_{\text{SSD}}
will denote the sum of the simple spider disjoint unions terms from
X
.

For
X=Q
or
X=Q_{i}
, we first take the well-behaved part of
X
—i.e., the terms from well-behaved intersection configurations in (
16
), (
17
)—and then apply this restriction. See also
Definition

4.22
.

4.3
A Closed Formula for
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}

In this section, we derive closed-form formulas that characterize
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
and its simple spider part.

We first set up some notation. By
Corollary

4.7
,

\displaystyle[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}

\displaystyle={(-1)^{j}\sum_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\textrm{ $\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}}},

\displaystyle[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{2D}[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}.

Recall that each
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
contains only simple spider disjoint union shapes since we have removed all isolated vertices from each
\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}
.
Based on this, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.22

(
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
and
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
)
.

Viewing
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
as a linear combination of graph matrices, we define
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
to be the simple spider part of
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
and
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
to be the simple spider disjoint union part of
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
. Note that
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}=[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
.

Similarly, for
j=0,\ldots,D
, we define
{(Q_{j})}_{\text{SS}}
and
{(Q_{j})}_{\text{SSD}}
to be the simple spider part and the simple spider disjoint union part of
[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
, respectively. Note that
{(Q_{j})}_{\text{SSD}}=[Q_{j}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
.

Lemma 4.23

(Closed formula for
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
)
.

L_{SS}\star Q_{SS}\star L_{SS}^{\top}=L_{SS}+{(Q_{0})}_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id}.

Proof.

We make the following observations:

1.

(Q_{1})_{SS}=(L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id})-L_{SS}\star{(Q_{0})}_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}
as the terms in
L_{SS}\star{(Q_{0})}_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}
which do not give a well-behaved intersection configuration are
(L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id})
.

2.

For all
i>1
,
(Q_{i})_{SS}=(Q_{i-1})_{SS}-L_{SS}\star{(Q_{i-1})}_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}
as the terms in
L_{SS}\star{(Q_{i-1})}_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}
which do not give a well-behaved intersection configuration are
(Q_{i-1})_{SS}
.

Using these observations, we can write

\displaystyle L_{SS}\star Q_{SS}\star L_{SS}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{2D}{L_{SS}\star(Q_{j})_{SS}\star L_{SS}^{\top}}

\displaystyle=(L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id})-(Q_{1})_{SS}+\sum_{j=1}^{2D}{((Q_{j})_{SS}-(Q_{j+1})_{SS})}

\displaystyle=L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id}.\qed

There is a similar equation for
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
but it is more intricate.

Definition 4.24
.

1.

We define
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
to be the part of
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
where there are no intersections between
L_{SSD}
,
{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}
, and
L^{\top}_{SSD}
.

2.

We define
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}
to be the part of
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
where there is at least one intersection between
L_{SSD}
,
{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}
, and
L^{\top}_{SSD}
.

Lemma 4.25
.

L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{Q}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}=[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}.

(24)

Proof.

We make the following observations:

1.

For all
i\in[2D]\cup\{0\}
,

\displaystyle L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}

\displaystyle=[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}

\displaystyle+[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}

2.

For all
i\in[2D]
,

[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}=-[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}

3.

[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{2D})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}=0

The first statement follows from the definitions. The third statement follows from the second statement and the fact that
Q_{2D+1}=0
(i.e., the recursive factorization terminates after
2D
steps). For the second statement, we observe that the terms of
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[Q_{i}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
which involve at least one intersection exactly cancel out the terms of
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[Q_{i+1}]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
which do not involve any intersections. More precisely, we prove statement
2
by the following bijection between ribbons in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}
and ribbons in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
.

Given a triple of ribbons
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
, and
R_{3}
contributing to
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}
, we obtain a triple of ribbons
R^{\prime}_{1}
,
R^{\prime}_{2}
, and
R^{\prime}_{3}
contributing to
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
as follows. For this argument, we do not delete double edges from the ribbons so
R_{2}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
may have double edges.

We split the ribbon
R_{1}
into ribbons
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
as follows.

(1)

Let
C_{1,\text{int}}
be the set of circle vertices of
R_{1}
which also appear in
R_{2}
or
R_{3}
(i.e., the set of circle vertices in
R_{1}
which are intersected with another circle vertex). Let
A
be the set of vertices in
V(R_{1})
which are either in
C_{1,\text{int}}
or are adjacent to a vertex in
C_{1,\text{int}}
. Let
B=V(R_{1})\setminus A
.

(2)

We take
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
to be the ribbon such that
V(R^{\prime\prime}_{1})=A\cup(B\cap V_{R_{1}})
,
U_{R^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=(A\cap U_{R_{1}})\cup(B\cap V_{R_{1}})
,
V_{R^{\prime\prime}_{1}}=(A\cap V_{R_{1}})\cup(B\cap V_{R_{1}})
, and
E(R^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\{\{u,v\}\in E(R_{1}):u,v\in A\}
. Intuitively,
R^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is the part of
R_{1}
which is involved in the intersection(s).

(3)

Similarly, we take
R^{\prime}_{1}
to be the ribbon such that
V(R^{\prime}_{1})=B\cup(A\cap U_{R_{1}})
,
U_{R^{\prime}_{1}}=(B\cap U_{R_{1}})\cup(A\cap U_{R_{1}})
,
V_{R^{\prime}_{1}}=(B\cap V_{R_{1}})\cup(A\cap U_{R_{1}})
, and
E(R^{\prime}_{1})=\{\{u,v\}\in E(R_{1}):u,v\in B\}
. Intuitively,
R^{\prime}_{1}
is the part of
R_{1}
which is not involved in the intersection(s).

Observe that
M_{R_{1}}=M_{R^{\prime}_{1}}M_{R^{\prime\prime}_{1}}
. Similarly, we split
R_{3}
into
R^{\prime\prime}_{3}
and
R^{\prime}_{3}
so that
M_{R_{3}}=M_{R^{\prime\prime}_{3}}M_{R^{\prime}_{3}}
, and we let
R^{\prime}_{2}
be the improper ribbon such that
M_{R^{\prime}_{2}}=M_{R^{\prime\prime}_{1}}M_{R_{2}}M_{R^{\prime\prime}_{3}}
. Then the triple
(R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
contributes to
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
,
M_{R_{1}}M_{R_{2}}M_{R_{3}}=M_{R^{\prime}_{1}}M_{R^{\prime}_{2}}M_{R^{\prime}_{3}}
, and the product of coefficients of
M_{R_{1}},M_{R_{2}},M_{R_{3}}
in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}
is
-1
times the product of the coefficients of
M_{R^{\prime}_{1}},M_{R^{\prime}_{2}},M_{R^{\prime}_{3}}
in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
. Thus, these terms cancel.

It is not hard to check that the map
(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\to(R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{2},R^{\prime}_{3})
is invertible, and consequently, it induces a bijection between ribbons in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}
and ribbons in
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
. Using these observations, we have that
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{Q}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2D}{[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}}+\sum_{i=0}^{2D}{[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2D-1}{\left([L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}+[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{i+1})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}\right)}

\displaystyle+[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{2D})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\geq 1\text{ intersections}}+[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}.\qed

It is not hard to see that
[L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}]_{\text{no intersections}}
is equal to the SSD part of their canonical product, i.e.,
{\left([{L}_{\text{SSD}},{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SSD}},{L}_{\text{SSD}}^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\right)}_{\text{SSD}}
. The latter is equal to
{\left([L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\right)}_{\text{SSD}}
, which is further equal to
{M}_{\text{SSD}}
as no well-behaved configuration of length 1 contributes to
[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-M
when
D_{trunc}\geq 18D
(Cf.
Remark

4.6
).
Combining this with
Lemma

4.25
, we get:

Corollary 4.26

(Closed formula for
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
)
.

If
D_{trunc}\geq 20D^{2}
, then

L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}{Q}_{\text{SSD}}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}={M}_{\text{SSD}}.

4.4
Alternative Analyses for Degree 4 and Degree 6 SoS

Before settling on our final approach for analyzing
Q
, we found two alternative approaches which work well for low degrees. We present these approaches because they are interesting in their own right and because they demonstrate the technical and conceptual challenges which we overcome. For simplicity, we assume that
A
is an even distribution (i.e.,
m_{A}(I)=m_{A}(-I)
for all intervals
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
). These approaches are as follows:

1.

Write
[Q]_{well-behaved}=Z*_{\mathrm{wb}}Z^{\top}
for some matrix
Z
which is a linear combination of disjoint unions of simple spiders, find equations for the coefficients of
Z
, and show that these equations are solvable for all distributions
A
.

2.

Show that we can write
[Q]_{well-behaved}=\E_{a\sim A}[Z(a)*_{\mathrm{wb}}Z(a)^{\top}]
.

We demonstrate how the two approaches can be used to obtain degree
4
and degree
6
SoS lower bounds when the distribution
A
is even. The dominating part of
Q
in these small degrees consists of simple spiders only. For simplicity, we take this for granted and ignore other terms. Thus, we can write
Q_{SS}
rather than
[Q]_{well-behaved}
.

For degree 4, the final
Q_{SS}
we obtain is
Q_{SS}=\mathrm{Id}+{l_{4}}S(2,2;0)
.

For the first approach, taking
Z=\mathrm{Id}+xS(2,2;0)
and recalling that
S(2,2;0)\star S(2,2;0)=\frac{1}{2}S(2,2;0)
, we have

Z\star Z^{\top}=\mathrm{Id}+(2x+\frac{x^{2}}{2})S(2,2;0).

To solve this, we need
l_{4}=\E_{a\sim A}[h_{4}(a)]=2x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}
. Since
\frac{x^{2}}{2}+2x=\frac{1}{2}(x+2)^{2}-2
, it can take values in the range
[2,\infty)
, so there is a solution as long as
l_{4}\geq-2
. But this is true for any distribution
A
with
\E_{a\sim A}[a^{2}]=1
, as by Cauchy-Schwarz,
1=\left(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{2}\cdot 1]\right)^{2}\leq\E_{a\sim A}[(a^{2})^{2}]\E_{a\sim A}[1]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]
so

l_{4}=\E_{a\sim A}[h_{4}(a)]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}-6a^{2}+3]\geq-2.

For the second approach, taking
Z=\mathrm{Id}+x(a)S(2,2;0)
where
x
is an undetermined function in the sample
a
of
A
, we want:

l_{4}=\E_{a\sim A}[h_{4}(a)]=\E_{a\sim A}\left[2x(a)+\frac{x(a)^{2}}{2}\right].

We let
x(a):={b_{2}}(a^{2}-1)+{b_{1}}a+b_{0}
for constants
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2}
and observe that since
\E_{a\sim A}[a^{2}]=1
,

E_{a\sim A}\left[(a^{2}-1)^{2}\right]=E\left[a^{4}-2a^{2}+1\right]=E\left[(a^{4}-6a^{2}+3)+4(a^{2}-1)+2\right]=l_{4}+2.

This means

E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{x(a)^{2}}{2}+2x(a)\right]=\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}(l_{4}+2)+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{2}+2b_{0}.

In order for this to equal
l_{4}
, we need that
\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}=1
and
b_{2}^{2}+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{2}+2b_{0}=0
. The real solutions are
b_{2}=\pm{\sqrt{2}}
,
b_{1}=0
, and
b_{0}=-2
. Thus, we can take
Z=\mathrm{Id}+(\sqrt{2}(a^{2}-1)-2)S(2,2;0)
.

For degree
6
, the final
Q_{SS}
is

Q_{SS}=\mathrm{Id}+{l_{4}}S(2,2;1)+(l_{6}-l_{4}^{2})S(3,3;0).

For the first approach, we take
Z=\mathrm{Id}+xS(2,2;1)+yS(3,3;0)
and expand
Z\star Z^{\top}
using the following facts which are not hard to check:

1.

S(3,3;0)\star S(3,3;0)=\frac{1}{6}S(3,3;0)

2.

S(3,3;0)\star S(2,2;1)=S(2,2;1)\star S(3,3;0)=\frac{3}{2}S(3,3;0)

3.

S(2,2;1)\star S(2,2;1)=\frac{1}{2}S(2,2;1)+9S(3,3;0)

Letting
Z\star Z^{\top}=Q_{SS}
, we need to determine the values of
x,y
so that two equations hold:

1.

\frac{x^{2}}{2}+2x=l_{4}

2.

\frac{y^{2}}{6}+9x^{2}+3xy+2y=l_{6}-l_{4}

For a given
x
, the minimum value of
\frac{y^{2}}{6}+9x^{2}+3xy+2y
is
-\frac{9}{2}x^{2}-18x-6
since

\frac{y^{2}}{6}+9x^{2}+3xy+2y=\frac{1}{6}(y+9x+6)^{2}-\frac{9}{2}x^{2}-18x-6.

By the first equation,
-\frac{9}{2}x^{2}-18x-36=-9l_{4}-6
so the two equations are feasible as long as
l_{4}\geq-2
and
l_{6}-l_{4}^{2}\geq-9l_{4}-6
. To show that these two inequalities hold for any even distribution
A
with
\E_{a\sim A}[a^{2}]=1
, we make the following observations:

1.

l_{4}=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}-6a^{2}+3]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]-3

2.

l_{6}=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{6}-15a^{4}+45a^{2}-15]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{6}]-15E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]+30

3.

l_{4}^{2}=\left(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}-6a^{2}+3]\right)^{2}=(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]-3)^{2}=(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}])^{2}-6E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]+9

4.

By Cauchy-Schwarz,
\left(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]\right)^{2}=\left(\E_{a\sim A}[a^{3}\cdot a]\right)^{2}\leq\E_{a\sim A}[a^{6}]E_{a\sim A}[a^{2}]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{6}]
.

Putting these observations together,

l_{6}-l_{4}^{2}\geq-9E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}]+21=-9(l_{4}+3)+21=-9l_{4}-6,

so we can find
x,y
in the first approach so that
Z\star Z^{\top}=Q_{SS}
.

For the second approach, we take
Z=\mathrm{Id}+x(a)S(2,2;1)+y(a)S(3,3;0)
. Similar to before, we need to have that

1.

\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{x(a)^{2}}{2}+2x(a)\right]=l_{4}
,

2.

\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{2}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]=l_{6}-l_{4}^{2}
.

As before, we will take
x(a)=\sqrt{2}(a^{2}-1)-2
. As for
y(a)
, we let

y(a)={b_{3}}(a^{3}-3a)+{b_{2}}(a^{2}-1)+({b^{\prime}_{1}}l_{4}+b_{1})a+({b^{\prime}_{0}}l_{4}+b_{0})

and determine the values of
b_{3},b_{2},b_{1},b^{\prime}_{0},b_{0}
. The following computations are useful:

1.

As we computed before,
\E_{a\sim A}[(a^{2}-1)(a^{2}-1)]=l_{4}+2
.

2.

\E_{a\sim A}[(a^{3}-a)a]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{4}-3a^{2}]=\E_{a\sim A}[(a^{4}-6a^{2}+3)+3(a^{2}-1)]=l_{4}

3.
