\E_{a\sim A}[(a^{3}-3a)^{2}]=\E_{a\sim A}[a^{6}-6a^{4}+9a^{2}]=l_{6}+9l_{4}+6

We now compute the expected values of the products involving
x
and/or
y
:

1.

\E_{a\sim A}[y(a)^{2}]={b_{3}^{2}}(l_{6}+9l_{4}+6)+{b_{2}^{2}}(l_{4}+2)+2b_{3}({b^{\prime}_{1}}l_{4}+b_{1})l_{4}+({b^{\prime}_{1}}l_{4}+b_{1})^{2}+({b^{\prime}_{0}}l_{4}+b_{0})^{2}

2.

\E_{a\sim A}[x(a)y(a)]=\sqrt{2}b_{2}(l_{4}+2)-2({b^{\prime}_{0}}l_{4}+b_{0})

3.

\E_{a\sim A}[x(a)^{2}]=2(l_{4}+2)+4=2l_{4}+8

4.

\E_{a\sim A}[y(a)]=({b^{\prime}_{0}}l_{4}+b_{0})

Recall that we want
\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{6}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]=l_{6}-l_{4}^{2}
. For this we observe:

1.

The coefficient of
l_{6}
in
\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{6}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]
is
\frac{b_{3}^{2}}{6}
, so we let
b_{3}=\pm{\sqrt{6}}
;

2.

The coefficient of
l_{4}^{2}
in
\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{6}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]
is
\frac{{b^{\prime}_{1}}^{2}}{6}+\frac{{b_{3}}{b^{\prime}_{1}}}{3}+\frac{{b^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}}{6}=\frac{1}{6}(b^{\prime}_{1}+b_{3})^{2}+\frac{{b^{\prime}_{0}}^{2}}{6}-1
, which can only be
-1
if
b^{\prime}_{1}=-\sqrt{6}
and
b^{\prime}_{0}=0
;

3.

The constant coefficient in
\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{6}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]
is

\displaystyle b_{3}^{2}+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{3}+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{6}+\frac{b_{0}^{2}}{6}+72+6\sqrt{2}b_{2}-6b_{0}+2b_{0}

\displaystyle=6+\frac{1}{3}(b_{2}+9\sqrt{2})^{2}-54+\frac{1}{6}(b_{0}-12)^{2}-24+72+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(b_{2}+9\sqrt{2})^{2}+\frac{1}{6}(b_{0}-12)^{2}+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{6},

which is only
0
if
b_{2}=-9\sqrt{2}
,
b_{0}=12
, and
b_{1}=0
.

Thus, we let

y(a)={\pm}\sqrt{6}(a^{3}-3a)-9\sqrt{2}(a^{2}-1)-\sqrt{6}{l_{4}}a+12.

With this
x(a)
and
y(a)
, the coefficient of
l_{4}
in
\E_{a\sim A}\left[\frac{y(a)^{2}}{6}+9x(a)^{2}+3x(a)y(a)+2y(a)\right]
is

9+27-54+18=0,

as needed.

Remark 4.27
.

In both approaches mentioned above, the “solvability” of the target object relies on the commutativity of products such as
S(3,3;0)\star S(2,2;1)=S(2,2;1)\star S(3,3;0)
, which holds for simple spiders having an equal number of left and right legs.
For general simple spiders, multiplication is non-commutative.

Important reminder.
In the rest of the lower bound proof, namely from
Section

5
to
Section

7
, we always assume that the samples
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies the event in
Theorem

3.15
, which happens with probability
1-o_{n}(1)
.
The remaining arguments involve no more probability.

5
PSDness via Representation

The main result of this section is the following lemma.
Recall the matrix
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
from
Definition

4.22
and the quantities
C_{U},C_{L}
from
Definition

2.11
.

Lemma 5.1

(Positive-definiteness of
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
in
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
)
.

Within the algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
,

{Q}_{\text{SS}}\succeq(6C_{U})^{-2D}C_{L}^{-D}\mathrm{Id}.

(25)

Lemma

5.22
is a more precise statement containing more technical conclusions needed later.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Lemma

5.22
, i.e., under mild conditions on the distribution
A
, the matrix
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
is positive-definite in the simple spider algebra. In
Section

5.1
we give the high-level plan of the proof. In
Section

5.3
through
Section

5.5
, we define an algebra representation
\rho
for the simple spider algebra and show the PSDness of
\rho({Q}_{\text{SS}})
. Finally in
Section

5.6
, we prove the positive-definiteness of
\rho({Q}_{\text{SS}})
.

5.1
Proof Overview for showing
M\succeq 0

The overall analysis of the PSDness of
M
proceeds as follows. We approximate
M
by
L{[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}}L^{T}
and show that the error terms are small. More precisely, we have the following equality

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left(M-[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\right)+\left([L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle+L\left(Q-[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\right)L^{\top}+L[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}L^{\top}

To prove our lower bound, we need to show the following:

1.

[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
is positive-definite. More precisely,

[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\succeq{\delta}\mathrm{Id}
for some
\delta>0
(which will depend on
A
and
D
but not on
n
).

2.

\lVert Q-[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\rVert
is
n^{-\Omega(\varepsilon/D)}
,

3.

\lVert M-[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\rVert
and
\lVert[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}\rVert
are both
n^{-\Omega(\varepsilon D_{trunc})}
.

4.

L
is somewhat well-conditioned. More precisely, the smallest singular value of
L
is at least
(5n)^{-D}\mathrm{Id}
(
Lemma

8.1
).

In this section we show that in the simple spider algebra,
Q_{SS}
is positive-definite. We show this as follows:

1.

We use the fact that
L_{SS}\star Q_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}=L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id}
(
Lemma

4.23
).

2.

Using a representation of the simple spider algebra, we show that
L_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id}
is positive-definite in the simple spider algebra.

In the next section, we use additional ideas to deal with disjoint unions of simple spiders in order to show that
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
is positive-definite.

5.2
Useful Notions in the Study of Simple Spiders

Recall that
S(i,j;u)
denotes the scaled simple spider shape
\alpha
with
i
weight-1 edges to the left,
j
weight-1 edges to the right, and
u
intersections.

Definition 5.2

(Default expansion)
.

Given
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
, we use
c_{\alpha}(i,j;u)
to denote the coefficient of
S(i,j;u)
in
\alpha
. We will sometimes write
\alpha\!=\!\sum\limits_{S}c_{\alpha}(S)S
or simply
\sum\limits_{i}c_{i}S_{i}
when there is no confusion.

Definition 5.3

(Good simple spiders and disjoint unions)
.

We call
S(i,j;u)

good
if it either has a trivial shape, i.e.,
i=j=0
, or its unique circle vertex has at least
\lceil k/2\rceil
edges to both sides, i.e.,
i,j\geq\lceil k/2\rceil
.
We call a simple spider disjoint union good if all of its components are good simple spiders. An SS- or SSD-linear combination is good if all nonzero summands are good.

Definition 5.4

(Consistent simple spiders)
.

A simple spider linear combination
\alpha=\sum_{i}c_{i}S_{i}
in
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is
consistent
if
c_{i}=c_{j}
whenever
S_{i},S_{j}
differ only by their intersection sizes and the coefficient of
S(0,0;0)
is 1.

Note that if
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is consistent, then every trivial shape with at most
D
vertices has coefficient 1 in
\alpha
.

Definition 5.5

(
\left|{\cdot}\right|_{\infty}
-norm on
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
and SSD combinations)
.

For
\beta=\sum_{S}c_{\beta}(S)S\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
, we let
\left|{\beta}\right|_{\infty}:=\max_{i}\{|c_{i}|\}
. Similarly, if
\beta=\sum_{S}c_{\beta}(S)S
where each
S
is a scaled SSD shape (i.e.,
S=\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
for some SSD shape
\alpha
), we let
\left|{\beta}\right|_{\infty}:=\max_{i}\{|c_{i}|\}
.

When viewing an element of
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
as a linear combination of scaled SS graph matrices, the
\left|{\cdot}\right|_{\infty}
norm corresponds to the maximal coefficient of the scaled SS shapes.

Remark 5.6

(Algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
and its dimension)
.

Recall that the
\star
-product is associative by
Lemma

4.19
, and there is a unit element
\sum_{i=0}^{D}S(i,i;0)
in
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
, so
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is indeed an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. (Another way to see this is via the representation
Lemma

5.9
below.) The dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{SA}_{D})
is the number of different simple spiders with indices size at most
D
. To count them, note that for fixed
|U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}|=i\in[0,D]
, both the left and right degree of the circle vertex in a simple spider can range in
[0,D-i]
, where if both degrees are
0
it means that the shape is trivial. Thus, there are
\sum_{i=0}^{D}(D-i+1)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{D+1}i^{2}
many simple spiders.

Remark 5.7
.

(Good simple spiders)
The algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
approximates the simple spider part in the result of matrix multiplication of
good
simple spiders. This follows from
Lemma

4.8
together with
Lemma

7.57
and
Lemma

7.59
which we will show later.

Observe that in the canonical decomposition of
M
, both
{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SS}}
and
{L}_{\text{SS}}
are consistent. Moreover,
{(Q_{0})}_{\text{SS}}
is left, right, and good. The goodness comes from the assumptions that
\E_{A}[h_{i}]=0
for
i=1,\dots,(k-1)
and that all shapes in
Q_{0}
are middle shapes. Note that
{L}_{\text{SS}}
is left and
{L}_{\text{SS}}^{\top}
is right, but they are not good.

5.3
Representation of the Simple Spider Algebra

In this subsection, we construct our representation
\rho
of the simple spider algebra. First, we define a linear map
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
from
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)
to
\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}(i+1)\right)
-by-
\left(\sum_{i=0}^{D}(i+1)\right)
real matrices and show that it gives an algebra isomorphism.

\text{\bf Notation: we write }\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)\text{\bf for }\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\big(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\big).

(26)

This notation is convenient for us since the image of
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(-)
are block matrices and the block
(k_{1},k_{2})
is where
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u))
is supported. The subscript means ‘preparatory’, for reasons that will be clear soon.

Definition 5.8

(Representation
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
)
.

For
k_{1},k_{2}\in[0,D]
and
0\leq u\leq\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
, we define
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)=\rho(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u))
to be a matrix on blocks
\{B(a,b)|0\leq a,b\leq D\}
where
B(a,b)
has dimension
[0,a]\times[0,b]
. The matrix
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)
is 0 outside
B(k_{1},k_{2})
, and within
B(k_{1},k_{2})
it is as follows. For each
(i,j)\in[0,k_{1}]\times[0,k_{2}]
,

\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)\upharpoonright_{B(k_{1},k_{2})}(i,j):=\begin{cases}0&if $j-i\neq k_{2}-k_{1}$ or $i<k_{1}-u$;\\
\binom{i}{k_{1}-u}/(k_{2}-u)!&o.w.\end{cases}

(27)

In other words,
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)
is a matrix supported on block
B(k_{1},k_{2})
with all the nonzero entries placed ‘diagonally up from the bottom-right’. We let
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
be defined over
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
by linear extension.

The key point is that
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
gives an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism, as shown in
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any fixed
D
,
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}:\ \mathrm{SA}_{D}\to\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathrm{SA}_{D})
is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism.

Note that in general,
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S^{\top})\neq\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S)^{\top}
. They in fact differ by a conjugation by a diagonal matrix, which we denote by
C
:

Definition 5.10

(Rescaling matrix)
.

Let the diagonal matrix
C
be

C\upharpoonright_{B(i,i)}(a,a)=a!\ \text{if $0\leq a\leq i\leq D$ and $0$ otherwise.}

(28)

Let
\sqrt{C}
be the diagonal matrix that takes the positive square roots of
C
’s entries.

Proposition 5.11
.

\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S)^{\top}=C^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S^{\top})C

Proof.

Suppose
S=S(i,j;u)
. By definition (
27
),
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S)\upharpoonright_{B(i,j)}(a,a+j-i)=\binom{a}{i}/j!
so

\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S)^{\top}\upharpoonright_{B(j,i)}(a+j-i,a)=\binom{a}{i}/j!.

On the other hand,
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S^{\top})\upharpoonright_{B(j,i)}(a+j-i,a)=\binom{a+j-i}{j}/i!
and
C
is diagonal, so

C^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S^{\top})C\upharpoonright_{B(j,i)}(a+j-i,a)=1/(a+j-i)!\cdot\binom{a+j-i}{j}/i!\cdot a!=\binom{a}{i}j!,

assuming the binomials are defined (otherwise they are zero). This proves the proposition.
∎

We now give our actual representation of the simple spider algebra, which is
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
composed with a conjugation of
\sqrt{C}
.

Definition 5.12

(Representation
\rho
)
.

The
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism
\rho:\mathrm{SA}_{D}\to\text{Im}(\rho)\subseteq\mathbb{M}_{D(D+1)/2}(\mathbb{R})
is the composition of
\sqrt{C}
-conjugation
and
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
, i.e.,
\rho(X)=\sqrt{C}^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)\sqrt{C}
. Entry-wise, we again denote
\rho\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right):=\rho\left(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\right)
, then

\rho\Big(k_{1},k_{2};u\Big)\upharpoonright_{B(k_{1},k_{2})}(i,j):=\begin{cases}0&if $j-i\neq k_{2}-k_{1}$ or $i<k_{1}-u$;\\
{\sqrt{i!j!}\over(k_{1}-u)!(k_{2}-u)!\big(u-(k_{1}-i)\big)!}&o.w.\end{cases}

(29)

By
Proposition

5.11
we have that
\rho
preserves the transpose:

\rho(S)^{\top}=\rho(S^{\top})\quad\forall S\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}.

(30)

In other words, we have:

Lemma 5.13

(
\rho
preserves sum-of-squares)
.

If
\rho(Y)=\sum_{i}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}
such that
Y_{i}\in\text{Im}(\rho)
for each
i
, then we have
Y=\sum_{i}Z_{i}\star Z_{i}^{\top}
where
Z_{i}:=\rho^{-1}(Y_{i})
.

Therefore, we can translate sums-of-squares expressions in
\text{Im}(\rho)
to ones in
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
.
For this reason, we will be working with
\rho
and only call
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
to assist with computations.

5.4
Structure of the Representation
\rho

In this subsection, we give an explicit decomposition of the algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}\simeq\rho(\mathrm{SA}_{D})
into matrix algebras which will be very useful. Recall that
\mathbb{M}_{i}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the algebra of all
i
-by-
i
real matrices.

Lemma 5.14

(Structure of
\text{Im}(\rho)
)
.

We have an algebra isomorphism

\rho(\mathrm{SA}_{D})\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{D+1}\mathbb{M}_{i}(\mathbb{R})

(31)

constructed as follows.
Recall that
\rho(\mathrm{SA}_{D})
consists of block matrices on blocks
\{B(i,j)\mid 0\leq i,j\leq D\}
where
B(i,j)
has dimension
(i+1)\times(j+1)
. Let

e_{i}\ (i=0,1,\dots,D)

(32)

be the diagonal
\{0,1\}
-matrix with 1s on
\{(b-i,b-i)\mid b\geq i\}
in
B(b,b)
for all
b
in
[0,D]
. Then

e_{i}(-)e_{i}:\ \text{Im}(\rho)\to\mathbb{M}_{D-i+1}(\mathbb{R})

(33)

is the projector to the subalgebra
\mathbb{M}_{D-i+1}(\mathbb{R})
in (
31
).

Proof.

Given
X\in\text{Im}(\rho)
, note that the operator
e_{i}(-)e_{i}
takes the submatrix supported on

\supp_{i}:=\{(a-i,b-i)\text{ of }B(a,b)\mid a,b\in[i,D]\}

(34)

and pads 0s elsewhere. Thus
e_{i}(-)e_{i}
is idempotent and linear, and it follows by inspection that
e_{i}(X\cdot Y)e_{i}=e_{i}(X)e_{i}\cdot e_{i}(Y)e_{i}
for all
X,Y\in\text{Im}(\rho)
. In other words,
e_{i}(-)e_{i}
an algebra projection. For
i\neq j
,
\supp_{i}
and
\supp_{j}
are disjoint so
e_{j}e_{i}(-)e_{i}e_{j}=0
, so these projectors are orthogonal to each other. Now if
X\in\text{Im}(\rho)
, by (
29
),

\displaystyle\supp(X)

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \{(i,j)\text{ of block }B(a,b)\mid 0\leq i\leq a\leq D,0\leq j\leq b\leq D,j-i=b-a\}

(35)

\displaystyle=\coprod_{i=0}^{D}\{(a-i,b-i)\text{ of }B(a,b)\mid a,b\in[i,D]\}=\coprod_{i=0}^{D}\supp_{i},

so
X=e_{0}Xe_{0}+\dots+e_{D}Xe_{D}
, i.e.,
\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}e_{i}(-)e_{i}
is the identity map.
Together, this means that

\text{Im}(\rho)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{D}e_{i}(\text{Im}(\rho))e_{i}\ \text{as $\mathbb{R}$-algebras.}

(36)

Finally, counting the dimension,

\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\left(e_{i}(\text{Im}(\rho))e_{i}^{\top}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:iso}}}}{{=}}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{SA}_{D})=1^{2}+\dots+(D+1)^{2}

which equals
\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{M}_{i}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, each
e_{i}(\text{Im}(\rho))e_{i}
is the full matrix algebra
\mathbb{M}_{D-i+1}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Remark 5.15

(Why call it a representation)
.

The decomposition (
36
) can be read as “
\rho
contains the information of all irreducible representations of the algebra”, for the following reason. First, each
\pi_{i}(-):=e_{i}\rho(-)e_{i}
is an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
as the image is
\mathbb{M}_{i}(\mathbb{R})
. Second, given (
36
), it is not hard to show that every finite-dimensional representation
\psi
of
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
can be decomposed into
\psi_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\psi_{t}
, where each
\psi_{i}:=\psi\circ\left(\rho^{-1}(e_{i})\star(-)\star\rho^{-1}(e_{i})\right)
is either the 0 map or a direct sum of representations that are isomorphic to
\pi_{i}
.

Remark 5.16
.

By the Wedderburn-Artin theorem, every finite-dimensional semisimple algebra is isomorphic to a direct sum of matrix algebras.
8
8

8

Strictly speaking, the semisimplicity of
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is not immediately clear a priori, and the theorem does not completely specify the dimensions of the matrix subalgebras nor their underlying division algebras (
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
, or the quaternions).
Here, however, we emphasize that we need more than an abstract or ‘purely computational’ existence of such a map.
We need a concrete construction that helps analyze whether special elements in the algebra like
\hat{Q}:=L_{SS}+L^{\top}_{SS}+(Q_{0})_{SS}-2\mathrm{Id}
are sums of squares.

Definition 5.17

(Components of
\rho
)
.

For
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
, we use
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
to denote the
e_{i}(\rho(\alpha))e_{i}
component in the direct sum (
36
), and we express the direct-sum decomposition of
\rho
as

\rho=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{D}\rho_{i}.

(37)

In other words,
\rho_{i}(\alpha)(a,b)
is entry
\rho(\alpha)(a,b)
in block
(a+i,b+i)
. We will view each
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
as a
(D-i+1)
-by-
(D-i+1)
matrix. Similarly, we view the rescaling matrix
C
as a
(D-i+1)\times(D-i+1)
matrix with entry
a!
at position
(a,a)
(
0\leq a\leq D-i
) when we multiply it with matrices in
\text{Im}(\rho_{i})
.

We record some more properties of the representation
\rho
.

Proposition 5.18
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
has expression
\alpha=\sum_{S(a,b;u)}c_{\alpha}(a,b;u)S(a,b;u)
.

1.

(Expression of
\rho_{i}
)

For any
i\in[0,D]
, if we view
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
as a
(D-i+1)\times(D-i+1)
matrix on
\supp_{i}
(
34
), then

\rho_{i}(\alpha)(a,b):=\sum_{u=0}^{\min\{a,b\}}\binom{a}{u}\binom{b}{u}\frac{u!}{\sqrt{a!b!}}c_{\alpha}(a-u,b-u;u+i).

(38)

2.

We say that
\rho(\alpha)
satisfies the
leading principal submatrix condition (lpc)
if
its top-left element is 1 and for all
i
,
\rho_{i+1}(\alpha)
is a leading principal submatrix of

\rho_{i}(\alpha)
.
Then
\rho(\alpha)
satisfies lpc if and only if
\alpha
is consistent (
Definition

5.4
).

3.

(Inverse of
\rho
)
Suppose
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is consistent.
For each
t\in[0,D]
, if we denote

v_{t}(i):=\rho_{0}(\alpha)(i+t,i),\ \ w_{t}(i):=c_{\alpha}(i+t,i;0)

which are vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{D-t+1}
, then
v_{t}=H_{t}w_{t}
where
H_{t}
is
[0,D-t]\times[0,D-t]
lower-triangular:

H_{t}(i,j)=\begin{cases}0,&if $i<j$;\\
\frac{\sqrt{i!(i+t)!}}{(i-j)!j!(j+t)!}&otherwise.\end{cases}

(39)

Inversely,
w_{t}=H_{t}^{-1}v_{t}
, where
\left|H_{t}^{-1}(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i-j}
.

Proof.

By definition
\rho(-)=\sqrt{C}^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(-)\sqrt{C}
,
where for every
k_{1},k_{2}\in[0,D]
and
i
in an appropriate range, we have:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(\alpha)\upharpoonright_{B(k_{1},k_{2})}(i,i+k_{2}-k_{1}){=}

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}c_{\alpha}(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(k_{1},k_{2};u)\upharpoonright_{B(k_{1},k_{2})}(i,i+k_{2}-k_{1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rhopre}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u=k_{1}-i}^{\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}c_{\alpha}(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\cdot\binom{i}{k_{1}-u}/(k_{2}-u)!.

So by the definition of
C
(
28
),

\rho(\alpha)\upharpoonright_{B(k_{1},k_{2})}(i,i+k_{2}-k_{1})=\sum_{u=k_{1}-i}^{\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}c_{\alpha}(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\cdot\binom{i}{k_{1}-u}\frac{\sqrt{(i+k_{2}-k_{1})!}}{\sqrt{i!}(k_{2}-u)!}.

(40)

Now
\rho_{i}(\alpha)(a,b)
is by definition the
(a,b)
th entry of block
B(a+i,b+i)
in
\rho(\alpha)
, so

\displaystyle\rho_{i}(\alpha)(a,b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rhocalc}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u=i}^{\min\{a+i,b+i\}}c_{\alpha}(a+i-u,b+i-u;u)\cdot\binom{a}{a+i-u}\frac{\sqrt{b!}}{\sqrt{a!}(b+i-u)!}

(41)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(u^{\prime}:=u-i)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{u^{\prime}=0}^{\min\{a,b\}}c_{\alpha}(a-u^{\prime},b-u^{\prime};u^{\prime}+i)\binom{a}{u^{\prime}}\binom{b}{u^{\prime}}\frac{u^{\prime}!}{\sqrt{a!b!}}=\eqref{eq:rhoialpha}.

This proves
Item

1
.

For
Item

2
, assume
\alpha
is consistent. Then the coefficient of
S(0,0;0)
in
\alpha
is 1, so by (
29
), the
(0,0)
th element in the
(0,0)
th block of
\rho(\alpha)
is 1. Also, since
c_{\alpha}(a,b;x)
depends only on
(a,b)
, each
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
in (
38
) is a leading principal submatrix of
\rho_{0}(\alpha)
. This shows that
\rho(\alpha)
satisfies lpc.

For the reverse direction, suppose
\rho(\alpha)
satisfies lpc. Then the top-left entry is 1, so by (
29
), the coefficient of
S(0,0;0)
in
\alpha
has to be 1. Next, for the entries
(a,0)
and
(0,b)
where
0\leq a,b\leq D
, by comparing
\rho_{0}(\alpha)
and
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
on these entries for all possible
i
, we see that for indices in
\{(a,b;x)\mid\text{one of $a$, $b$ is 0}\}
,
c_{\alpha}(a,b;x)
depends only on
(a,b)
. This allows us to use induction to show the following property
P_{t}
:

(P_{t}):\ \text{restricted to index set $\{(a,b;x)\mid\text{$a\leq t$ or $b\leq t$}\}$, $c_{\alpha}(a,b;x)$ depends only on $(a,b)$.}

We’ve shown
P_{0}
holds. Assuming
P_{t-1}
, using the lpc on
\rho(\alpha)
we can get that on
(a,b;x)
, where
a=t
or
b=t
,
c_{\alpha}(-)
is independent of
x
. Thus,
P_{t}
holds. This means
P_{t}
holds for all
t\in[0,D]
, i.e.,
c_{\alpha}(a,b;x)
depends only on
(a,b)
.
Together, we get that
\alpha
is consistent.

For
Item

3
, the identity
v_{t}=H_{t}w_{t}
follows by expanding
Item

1
, where we substitute all
c_{\alpha}(i-u,i+t-u;u)
by
c_{\alpha}(i-u,i+t-u;0)
(we can do so because
\alpha
is consistent) and use the running variable
u^{\prime}:=i-u
in the sum. We next estimate the entries in
H_{t}^{-1}
. There is a general formula for the inverse of a lower-triangular invertible matrix
H
of dimension
d
:
9
9

9

To see this, write
H=(\mathrm{Id}+H^{\prime})H_{\mathrm{diag}}
where
H^{\prime}
is nilpotent, then
H^{-1}=H_{\mathrm{diag}}^{-1}(\mathrm{Id}+H^{\prime}+\ldots+(H^{\prime})^{d-1})
.

H^{-1}(a,b)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{d-1}{(-1)^{k}\over H(a,a)}\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k+1}):\\
a=i_{1}>\ldots>i_{k+1}=b\end{subarray}}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}\frac{H(i_{l},i_{l+1})}{H(i_{l+1},i_{l+1})}

(42)

where we let the empty product (for
k=0
) be 1 if
a=b
and 0 otherwise. Applying this formula to
H_{t}
, since
\frac{1}{H_{t}(a,a)}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}\frac{H_{t}(i_{l},i_{l+1})}{H_{t}(i_{l+1},i_{l+1})}=\frac{1}{(a-i_{2})!(i_{2}-i_{3})!\ldots(i_{k}-b)!\sqrt{b!(b+t)!}}\leq{1\over b!}
by (
39
),

\left|H_{t}^{-1}(a,b)\right|\leq{1\over b!}\sum_{k=0}^{D-t+1}\#\big(\text{strictly decreasing sequence $(i_{1}=a,\dots,i_{k+1}=b)$}\big)={1\over b!}\binom{a-b-1}{k-1}\leq 2^{a-b}.

Here, we let
\binom{n}{-1}
be
1
if
n=-1
and
0
otherwise.
∎

5.5
PSDness of
{(Q_{0}+L+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})}_{\text{SS}}
Under
\rho

In this subsection, we show that
\rho\left({(Q_{0}+L+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})}_{\text{SS}}\right)
is PSD. To simplify notation, in the rest of this section we denote

l(t):=\E_{A}[h_{t}(x)],\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{N}.

Definition 5.19

(
\hat{Q}
)
.

We denote
{(Q_{0}+L+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})}_{\text{SS}}
by
\hat{Q}
, i.e.,

\hat{Q}=\sum\limits_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in[0,D]\times[0,D]}\sum\limits_{u=0}^{\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}{l(k_{1}+k_{2}-2u)\cdot S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)}.

(43)

Recall that in the notation of
Proposition

5.18
,

\rho(\hat{Q})=\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})+\dots+\rho_{D}(\hat{Q}).

(44)

By
Item

1
of
Proposition

5.18
applied to
\hat{Q}
, we immediately get the following.

Proposition 5.20
.

For each
i=0,\dots,D
,
\rho_{i}(\hat{Q})
is a symmetric matrix supported on
\supp_{i}
(
34
), each
\rho_{i+1}(\hat{Q})
is a leading principal submatrix of
\rho_{i}(\hat{Q})
, and

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(a,b)=\sum_{u=0}^{\min\{a,b\}}\binom{a}{u}\binom{b}{u}\frac{u!}{\sqrt{a!b!}}\cdot l(a+b-2u).

(45)

where we recall that
l(n)=\E\limits_{A}[h_{n}(x)]
.

In particular, to find a sum-of-squares expression of
\rho(\hat{Q})
, we only need to do so for
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
.

Lemma 5.21
.

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
in (
55
) is a sum-of-squares—more precisely, an expectation of squares.

Proof.

Recall that
l(t):=\E_{A}[h_{t}(x)]
. By (
55
),

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})=\E_{x\sim A}[\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(x)]

(46)

where
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(x)
is the matrix

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(x)(i,j):=\sum_{u=0}^{\min\{i,j\}}\binom{i}{u}\binom{j}{u}\frac{u!}{\sqrt{i!j!}}\cdot h_{i+j-2u}(x).

(47)

The product formula of the physicists’ Hermite polynomials
\{H_{i}(-)\}
says (see e.g.
[
Car57
]
)

H_{i}(x)H_{j}(x)=\sum_{u=0}^{\min\{i,j\}}\binom{i}{u}\binom{j}{u}u!2^{u}H_{i+j-2u}(x),

(48)

and there is a relation
H_{n}(x)=h_{n}(\sqrt{2}x)2^{n/2}
between
H_{n}
and
h_{n}
, which together imply that

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(x)(i,j)=\frac{h_{i}(x)}{\sqrt{i!}}\frac{h_{j}(x)}{\sqrt{j!}}.

Viewing
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
as having row and column indices
[0,D]
, we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(x)=v_{0}(x)v_{0}(x)^{\top}

(49)

where

v_{0}(x)=(\frac{h_{0}(x)}{\sqrt{0!}},\dots,\frac{h_{D}(x)}{\sqrt{D!}})^{\top}.

(50)

So
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})=\E_{A}[v_{0}v_{0}^{\top}]
which is an expectation of a PSD matrix.
∎

5.6
Positive-Definiteness of
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
as an Element in the Simple Spider Algebra

In this subsection, we prove the positive-definiteness of
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
in the algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
.
More specifically, we show that
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
consists of only good simple spiders, and it is a sum-of-squares of good simple spiders under the
\star
-product. This is
Lemma

5.22
below.

To provide motivation, note that
\rho(\hat{Q})
is PSD and we have the equation
L_{SS}\star Q_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}=\hat{Q}
. Given this, it is natural to attempt to transfer the PSDness of
\hat{Q}
to
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
by multiplying
{L}_{\text{SS}}^{-1}
,
({L}_{\text{SS}}^{-1})^{\top}
from both sides. A subtlety is that we need to ensure
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
contains only good simple spiders for the approximation algebra to work well (cf.
Remark

4.17
), and to control the coefficients in its square root (important for
Section

6
). For this reason, we prove the following quantitative result.

Lemma 5.22

(Positive-definiteness of
{Q}_{\text{SS}}
as an element in
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
)
.

Suppose
X\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
satisfies:

{L}_{\text{SS}}\star X\star{L}_{\text{SS}}^{\top}=\hat{Q}.

(51)

Then:

1.

X
is consistent and good;

2.

\rho(X)\succeq(6C_{U})^{-2D}C_{L}^{-D}\mathrm{Id}
, and
X=\alpha_{0}\star\alpha_{0}^{\top}
for some
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
that is left, good and consistent;

3.

For all
0\leq j\leq i\leq D
, we have bounds

\displaystyle\left|c_{\alpha_{0}}(i,j;0)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{i(i+4)}\cdot{C_{U}}^{2i^{2}+i},

(52)

\displaystyle\left|c_{\alpha_{0}^{\star\text{-inv}}}(i,j;0)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(8C_{U}^{2}\sqrt{C_{L}}\right)^{i}.

(53)

Proof.

The equation that
X
satisfies can be rewritten as

X={L}_{\text{SS}}^{\star\text{-inv}}\star\hat{Q}\star\left({L}_{\text{SS}}^{\star\text{-inv}}\right)^{\top}

(54)

where
(-)^{\star\text{-inv}}
means the inverse in algebra
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
. We analyze the RHS of (
54
) in the following steps, where we recall that
\rho_{i}(\hat{Q})
denotes a component of
\rho(\hat{Q})
as in
Lemma

5.21
.

1. Proof of
Item

1
.

Recall that
A
matches the first
k-1
moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, so

\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&B^{\top}\\
B&F\end{pmatrix}

(55)

for some real matrices
B
and
F
. Here, the “
\mathrm{Id}
” is supported on
[0,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor]\times[0,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor]
. As for
\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})
, it is lower-triangular, its off-diagonal part equals the lower half of
\rho(\hat{Q})
and moreover, all its diagonal entries are all 1 by the computation (
38
) where we recall that the only simple spiders in
{L}_{\text{SS}}
having equally many left and right legs are the trivial shapes
\{S(0,0;u)\mid 0\leq u\leq D\}
. Therefore,

\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
B&\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}}\end{pmatrix}\ \text{where $F_{\text{low}}$ means the strict lower-triangular part of $F$.}

(56)

We then have

\displaystyle\rho(X)

\displaystyle=\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\rho(\hat{Q})\left(\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\right)^{\top}

(57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{D}\underbrace{\rho_{i}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\cdot\rho_{i}(\hat{Q})\cdot\left(\rho_{i}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\right)^{\top}}_{=\rho_{i}(X)}

(58)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
-(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1}B&(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

(59)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&0\\
0&(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1}\cdot\left(F-BB^{\top}\right)\cdot(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}}^{\top})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(60)

To see that
X
is consistent and good: since
\hat{Q}
and
{L}_{\text{SS}}
are consistent, by item 2 of
Proposition

5.18
, both
\rho(\hat{Q})
and
\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})
satisfy the leading principal submatrix condition (lpc). Then
\rho(X)
satisfies it too by the following easy fact applied to (
58
) twice, so
X
is consistent.

Fact 5.23
.

If
A
,
B
are square matrices such that
A
is lower-triangular or
B
is upper-triangular,
then the leading principal submatrices of
AB
are products of the leading principal ones of
A
and
B
.

By (
60
) and consistency,
\rho(X)
is supported on the images of good simple spiders, so
X
is good.

2. Proof of
Item

2
.

We start by lower bounding the eigenvalues of the symmetric matrix in (
60
),

(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1}\cdot\left(F-BB^{\top}\right)\cdot(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}}^{\top})^{-1}.

(61)

Here,
(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})
is a
(D+1)
-by-
(D+1)
submatrix of
\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})
, so by the definition of
\rho
(
38
), its entries can be upper bounded in absolute value by
(3C_{U})^{D}
. Thus, its Frobenius norm is at most
(3C_{U})^{D}\cdot(D+1)\leq(6C_{U})^{D}
. We now use the following fact.

Fact 5.24
.

For any invertible real matrix
Y
,
\sigma_{\min}(Y)\geq(\lVert Y^{-1}\rVert_{Fr})^{-1}
, where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{Fr}

is the Frobenius norm and
\sigma_{\min}(Y)
is the smallest singular value of
Y
.

Proof of Fact.

Letting
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n^{\prime}}
be the singular values of
Y
, the singular values of
Y^{-1}
are
\sigma_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\sigma_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}
so
\lVert Y^{-1}\rVert_{Fr}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}{\sigma_{i}^{-2}}\geq\sigma_{\min}(Y)^{-2}
and thus
\sigma_{min}(Y)\geq\frac{1}{\lVert Y^{-1}\rVert_{Fr}}
.
∎

Using this fact, we get
\sigma_{\min}((\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1})\geq\lVert(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})\rVert_{Fr}^{-1}\geq(6C_{U})^{-D}
. As for the
(F-BB^{\top})
factor in (
61
), we bound its smallest singular value by the following simple claim.

Claim 5.25
.

For any real symmetric matrix

Y=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{Id}&U^{\top}\\
U&V\end{pmatrix}
,
\sigma_{\min}(V-UU^{\top})\geq\sigma_{\min}(Y)
.

Proof.

Let
Z:=V-UU^{\top}
and let
z
be a unit vector such that
\left|z^{\top}Zz\right|=\sigma_{\min}(Z)
. Taking
y=-U^{\top}z
,
\sigma_{\min}(Z)=\left|z^{\top}Zz\right|=\left|(y^{\top},z^{\top})Y\begin{pmatrix}y\\
z\end{pmatrix}\right|\geq\sigma_{\min}(Y)\cdot\lVert\begin{pmatrix}y\\
z\end{pmatrix}\rVert\geq\sigma_{\min}(Y)\left\|z\right\|_{2}=\sigma_{\min}(Y)
.
∎

Thus we have:

\sigma_{\min}\Big((\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}})^{-1}\left(F-BB^{\top}\right)(\mathrm{Id}+F_{\text{low}}^{\top})^{-1}\Big)\geq{\sigma_{\min}(F-BB^{\top})\over{(6C_{U})^{2D}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{claim:elem}}}}{{\geq}}\frac{\sigma_{\min}(\rho_{0}(\hat{Q}))}{(6C_{U})^{2D}}.

(62)

Continuing, by (
49
) and (
50
),
v^{\top}\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})v=\E_{A}\ [p_{v}(x)^{2}]
where
p_{v}(x):=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}v_{i}{h_{i}(x)\over\sqrt{i!}}
has
l_{2}
-norm
\lVert v\rVert_{2}
under
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, so
v^{\top}\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})v\geq C_{L}^{-D}\lVert v\rVert_{2}^{2}
by the definition of
C_{L}
.
This implies that

\sigma_{\min}(\rho_{0}(\hat{Q}))\geq C_{L}^{-D}.

(63)

So by (
60
), (
62
), the consistency of
X
, and the fact that
\rho_{0}(X)
is PSD (this follows from the PSDness of
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
(
Lemma

5.21
), (
55
), and (
60
)), we have:

\rho(X)\succeq\frac{\mathrm{Id}}{(6C_{U})^{2D}C_{L}^{D}},\ \text{in particular,}\ \rho(X)=WW^{\top}\text{ for some $W\in\text{Im}(\rho)$.}

(64)

Applying
\rho^{-1}(-)
to (
64
), we get
X=\alpha_{0}\star\alpha_{0}^{\top}\ \text{for $\alpha_{0}:=\rho^{-1}(W)\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}$}
.

To show that we can choose
\alpha_{0}
to be left, good and consistent, it suffices to construct a
W
satisfying (
64
) that is lower-triangular, supported on the image of good simple spiders, and which satisfies the leading principal submatrix condition (lpc) (
Proposition

5.18
,
Item

2
). Since
X
is good,
\rho(X)
is supported on the images of good simple spiders, i.e., the entry positions are in
\left([0,D]\backslash[0,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor]\right)\times\left([0,D]\backslash[0,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor]\right)
and the diagonal, so we can let
W
be supported on them too. For every PSD matrix, the Cholesky decomposition gives a factorization of the form
ZZ^{\top}
where
Z
is lower-triangular so we can also assume that the
\rho_{0}
component of
W
is lower-triangular and we can choose its top-left entry to be
1
(as opposed to
-1
) in this decomposition. Using
Fact

5.23
, we can then take the other components of
W
to be the leading principal submatrices of the
\rho_{0}
component of
W
and this will satisfy
WW^{\top}=\rho(X)
. The matrix
W
now satisfies all the three conditions.

3. Proof of
Item

3
, Inequality (
52
).

We first bound the magnitudes of entries in
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
,
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})
,
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}
by

\displaystyle\left|{\vphantom{\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})}\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})(i,j)}\right|,\ \left|\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(i,j)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{i}\cdot C_{U}^{i+j},

(65)

\displaystyle\left|\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}(i,j)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{i(i+1)}\cdot C_{U}^{2i^{2}},\ \ \forall j\leq i\leq D.

(66)

To see (
65
), observe that the coefficients in
\hat{Q}
satisfy
\left|c_{\hat{Q}}(a,b;u)\right|=\left|\E\limits_{A}[h_{a+b}(x)]\right|\leq C_{U}^{a+b}
, so by (
38
),
\left|\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})(i,j)\right|\leq\sum_{u=0}^{j}{\binom{i}{u}C_{U}^{i+j-2u}}\leq 2^{i}C_{U}^{i+j}
. The same is true for
\left|\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})(i,j)\right|
.

To see (
66
), observe that since
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})
is lower-triangular, all of its diagonal entries are
1
(
56
), and
\left|\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i}C_{U}^{i+j}
, applying (
42
) to
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})
gives the bound

\left|\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i-j}\left(2^{i}C_{U}^{2i}\right)^{i-j}\leq 2^{i(i+1)}C_{U}^{2i^{2}}.

Next, we prove (
52
) using (
65
) and (
66
). Since
\rho_{0}(X)=\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\cdot\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})\cdot(\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1})^{\top}
, it holds that

\left|\rho_{0}(X)(i,j)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rho0Qhatentrybound}\text{ and }\eqref{eq:rho0LSSinventrybound}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}:\ k_{1}\leq i,\ k_{2}\leq j\end{subarray}}{2^{i(i+1)+i+j(j+1)}{C_{U}}^{2i^{2}+2i+2j^{2}}}\leq\underbrace{2^{2i^{2}+5i}{C_{U}}^{4i^{2}+2i}}_{:=r(i,j)}.

(67)

In other words, on the support of
\text{Im}(\rho_{0})
,
\left|(WW^{\top})(i,j)\right|\leq r(i,j)
for all
i,j
. This implies that
\lVert(WW^{\top})\upharpoonright_{[0,i]\times[0,i]}\rVert\leq(i+1)r(i,i)
so
\lVert W\upharpoonright_{[0,i]\times[0,i]}\rVert\leq\sqrt{(i+1)r(i,i)}\leq 2^{i(i+3)}{C_{U}}^{2i^{2}+i}
by the lower-triangularity of
W
. It follows that entry-wise,
\left|W(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i(i+3)}{C_{U}}^{2i^{2}+i}
. We now use the matrix
W
to analyze
\alpha_{0}
: for each
t\leq D
we apply the matrix
H_{t}^{-1}
in
Item

3
of
Proposition

5.18
to the vector
v_{t}(i):=W(i+t,i)
,
i\in[0,D-t]
, where again we focus only on the part of
W
supported on
\text{Im}(\rho_{0})
. As a result,

\left|c_{\alpha_{0}}(i+t,i;0)\right|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{i}2^{i-j}2^{(i+t)(i+t+3)}\cdot{C_{U}}^{2(i+t)^{2}+(i+t)}\leq 2^{(i+t)(i+t+4)}\cdot{C_{U}}^{2(i+t)^{2}+(i+t)}.

(68)

This proves the inequality (
52
).

4. Proof of
Item

3
, Inequality (
53
).

For
0\leq{t^{\prime}}\leq D
, denote by
W_{t^{\prime}}
the part of
W
on the support of
\text{Im}(\rho_{D-t^{\prime}})
. Then
W_{t^{\prime}}=W_{0}\upharpoonright_{[0,t^{\prime}]\times[0,t^{\prime}]}
since
W
satisfies lpc. Also,
W_{t^{\prime}}W_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}=\rho_{0}(X)\upharpoonright_{[0,{D-t^{\prime}}]\times[0,{D-t^{\prime}}]}
since
\rho_{0}(X)=W_{0}W_{0}^{\top}
and
W_{0}
is lower-triangular. Using this and (
58
), we get:

W_{t^{\prime}}W_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}=\left(\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\cdot\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})\cdot(\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1})^{\top}\right)\upharpoonright_{[0,{t^{\prime}}]\times[0,{t^{\prime}}]}

(69)

Since
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}
is lower-triangular and
(\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1})^{\top}
is upper-triangular, the operation of taking leading principal submatrices on the RHS of (
69
) can be applied to each term in the product. This gives:

W_{t^{\prime}}W_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}=\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}\cdot\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}(\hat{Q})\cdot(\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1})^{\top},

(70)

where we have used that
\rho({L}_{\text{SS}})^{-1}
and
\rho(\hat{Q})
both satisfy lpc. Taking the matrix inverse, we get:

(W_{t^{\prime}}^{\top})^{-1}(W_{t^{\prime}}^{-1})=\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}({L}_{\text{SS}})^{\top}\cdot\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}(\hat{Q})\cdot\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}({L}_{\text{SS}}).

(71)

We now upper bound the norms of
\rho_{D-t^{\prime}}({L}_{\text{SS}})
and
\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}(\hat{Q})
. They are the
[0,t^{\prime}]
-by-
[0,t^{\prime}]
submatrices of
\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})
and
\rho_{0}(\hat{Q})
, respectively. The intuition for the upper bounds is to let the SoS degree be
t^{\prime}
and then use the corresponding version of (
65
), (
63
). Formally, since
\left|\rho_{0}({L}_{\text{SS}})(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i}C_{U}^{i+j}
for all
0\leq j\leq i\leq D
by (
65
), we have
\left\lVert\rho_{D-t^{\prime}}({L}_{\text{SS}})\right\rVert\leq(t^{\prime}+1)2^{t^{\prime}}C_{U}^{2t^{\prime}}\leq(2C_{U})^{2t^{\prime}}
.
As for
\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}(\hat{Q})
, by setting
D\leftarrow t^{\prime}
in the derivation of (
63
), we get
\sigma_{\min}(\rho(\hat{Q}\upharpoonright_{[0,t^{\prime}]\times[0,t^{\prime}]}))\geq C_{L}^{-t^{\prime}}
and so
\left\lVert\rho_{D-{t^{\prime}}}(\hat{Q})\right\rVert\leq C_{L}^{t^{\prime}}
.

Combining these bounds and (
71
), we get
\lVert W_{0}^{-1}\upharpoonright_{[0,t^{\prime}]\times[0,t^{\prime}]}\rVert\leq(4C_{U}^{2}\sqrt{C_{L}})^{t^{\prime}}
, which implies that for all
0\leq j\leq i\leq D
,
\left|W_{0}^{-1}(i,j)\right|\leq(4C_{U}^{2}\sqrt{C_{L}})^{i}
.

By
Item

3
of
Proposition

5.18
, if we take
w_{t}
to be the vector with coordinates
w_{t}(i)={c_{\alpha_{0}^{\star\text{-inv}}}(i+t,i;0)}
, then
w_{t}=H_{t}^{-1}v_{t}
where
v_{t}
is the vector with coordinates
v_{t}(i)=W^{-1}(i+t,i)
,
H_{t}^{-1}
is lower triangular, and
\left|H_{t}^{-1}(i,j)\right|\leq 2^{i-j}
. Thus,

\left|c_{\alpha_{0}^{\star\text{-inv}}}(i+t,i;0)\right|=\left|w_{t}(i)\right|=\left|\sum_{j=0}^{i}{H_{t}^{-1}(i,j)W^{-1}(j+t,j)}\right|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{i}{2^{i-j}(4C_{U}^{2}\sqrt{C_{L}})^{i+t}}\leq(8C_{U}^{2}\sqrt{C_{L}})^{i+t}.

The inequality (
53
) follows.
∎

6
PSDness of
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
via Parallel Multiplication

In this section, we show that
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
—recall that it denotes
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}
—is positive-definite. The main result of this section is the following.

Lemma 6.1

(PSDness of
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
)
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{100D,20D^{2},2k\log n\}
and

(4C_{U})^{4D^{2}}C_{L}^{2D}D_{trunc}^{32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}.

(72)

Then

[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\succeq n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}\mathrm{Id}.

(73)

The intuition is that roughly speaking, the multiplication of two good simple spider disjoint unions (SSD) amounts to component-wise simple spider multiplications. A formal statement and proof require caution and we need the following definition.

Recall that by
Definition

3.8
, an SSD
\alpha
can be viewed as a multi-set of simple spiders, where different ways of splitting the vertices in
U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
result in the same SSD.

Definition 6.2

(
D
-combination)
.

For a consistent simple spider linear combination
\alpha
, we define its
D
-combination
, denoted as
[\alpha]^{D}
, to be a linear combination of SSDs with the following coefficients: if an SSD
\{\!\!\{S_{1},\dots,S_{t}\}\!\!\}
has both left and right index size no more than
{D}
, then the coefficient of this scaled SSD shape is
\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}c_{\alpha}(S_{i})
, otherwise the coefficient is 0. Here,
c_{\alpha}(\cdot)
means the coefficient of the scaled shapes in
\alpha
.

It is clear from the definition that if
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is consistent, then
[\alpha^{\top}]^{D}=\left([\alpha]^{D}\right)^{\top}
.

Our plan is as follows. We show that
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}=[{Q}_{\text{SS}}]^{D}
, and that if
{Q}_{\text{SS}}\approx AA^{\top}
then
[{Q}_{\text{SSD}}]^{D}\approx[A]^{D}\left([A]^{D}\right)^{\top}
. We then show that
[A]^{D}\left([A]^{D}\right)^{\top}
is sufficiently positive-definite using
Lemma

6.7
.
Lemma

6.1
then follows.

We now establish some properties of the
D
-combination operator. We will then use them to show that
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}=[{Q}_{\text{SS}}]^{D}
and is positive-definite.

Notation.

We denote the left- and right-index size of a simple spider
S(a,b;u)
by
l(S(a,b;u)):=a+u
,
r(S(a,b;u)):=b+u
, respectively, and the left- and right-index size of an SSD
x=\{\!\!\{S_{1},\dots,S_{t}\}\!\!\}
by
l(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}l(S_{i})
,
r(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{t}r(S_{i})
, respectively.

We extend the
|\cdot|_{\infty}
norm on
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
to SSD linear combinations in the natural way.
Namely, if
Y=\sum_{i}c_{i}S_{i}
is a formal linear combination of scaled SSD shapes, we let
|Y|_{\infty}:=\max_{i}{|c_{i}|}
.

Observation 6.1
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
,
\alpha
is left (or right) iff
\rho(\alpha)
is lower- (or upper-) triangular.

Proof.

The definition of
{\rho}
(
5.12
) implies that
\alpha
is left if and only if
\rho(\alpha)
is block-lower-triangular. Since all matrices in
\text{Im}(\rho)
are diagonal on the diagonal blocks, this holds if and only if
\rho(\alpha)
is lower-triangular. The case for
\alpha
being right is similar.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
are consistent and either
\alpha
is left or
\beta
is right. Then
\alpha\star\beta
is consistent.

Proof.

We assume
\alpha
is left; the other case is similar.
One way to see the proposition is by using the representation
\rho
. Namely,
Item

2
of
Proposition

5.18
says
\alpha
is consistent if and only if
\{\rho_{i}(\alpha)\mid i=0,\dots,{D}\}
satisfies the leading principal submatrix condition (lpc). Recall
\rho(\alpha)\rho(\beta)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}\rho_{i}(\alpha)\rho_{i}(\beta)
. If in addition
\alpha
is left, then every
\rho_{i}(\alpha)
is lower-triangular, so by
Fact

5.23
we have that
\{\rho_{i}(\alpha)\rho_{i}(\beta)\}
satisfies lpc too.
∎

The next lemma shows that the
[\cdot]^{D}
operator interacts nicely with
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
and
\star
.

Lemma 6.4

(Commutative diagram)
.

For all consistent
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
where either
\alpha
is left or
\beta
is right,

[\alpha]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[\beta]^{D}=[\alpha\star\beta]^{D}.

(74)

Proof.

Let
\gamma:=\alpha\star\beta
. By
Proposition

6.3
,
\gamma
is also consistent so
[\gamma]^{D}
is defined. We will use the following claim:

Claim 6.5
.

Suppose
z=\{\!\!\{S_{1},\dots,S_{t}\}\!\!\}
is a simple spider disjoint union shape where
l(z),r(z)\leq{D}
. Then the coefficient of the scaled graph matrix
z
in
[\alpha]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[\beta]^{D}
is equal to the product of
S_{i}
’s coefficient in
\alpha\star\beta
(over
i=1,\dots,t
).

If we have this claim, then we can prove
Lemma

6.4
as follows. The product of any two graph matrices can be expressed as a sum of graph matrices, i.e., the coefficients of every ribbon realizing the same shape is the same, so we only need to analyze one ribbon for each shape. For scaled SSD shapes,
Claim

6.5
says their coefficients in
[\gamma]^{D}
are the same as in
[\alpha]^{D}\star[\beta]^{D}
.
Lemma

6.4
follows.
∎

Proof of
Claim

6.5
.

Fix a scaled ribbon
R
that realizes
z
. Then
R
has
t
disjoint components. The well-behaved product configurations for
[\alpha]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[\beta]^{D}
that result in
R
can be characterized by all sets
A=\{(R_{0},R^{\prime}_{0}),\ (R_{1},R_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,\ (R_{t},R_{t}^{\prime})\}
that satisfy the following:

1.

R_{0}=R^{\prime}_{0}
is the ribbon such that
V(R_{0})=U_{R_{0}}=V_{R_{0}}=U_{R}\cap V_{R}
and
E(R_{0})=\emptyset
.

2.

For all
i\in[t]
,
(R_{i},R^{\prime}_{i})
is a composable pair of scaled simple spider ribbons such that

(a)

Either
R_{i}
and
R^{\prime}_{i}
have the same circle vertex or exactly one of
R_{i}
and
R^{\prime}_{i}
is trivial.

(b)

U_{R_{i}}\cap V_{R_{i}}\cap U_{R_{i}}\cap V_{R_{i}}=\emptyset
.

(c)

If
\alpha
is left, then
|V_{R_{i}}|=|U_{R^{\prime}_{i}}|\leq|U_{R_{i}}|
. If
\beta
is right, then
|V_{R_{i}}|=|U_{R^{\prime}_{i}}|\leq|V_{R^{\prime}_{i}}|
.

3.

There is no “cross-pair” vertex intersections, i.e.,
\big(V(R_{i})\cup V(R_{i}^{\prime})\big)\cap\big(V(R_{j})\cup V(R_{j}^{\prime})\big)=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
.

Observe that the contribution of each pair
(R_{i},R^{\prime}_{i})
to the
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
-product is equal to its contribution to the
\star
-product. In particular, the difference between
\star
and
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
mentioned in
Remark

4.17
will not cause any issues. This holds by the definitions of
\star
and
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
when both
R_{i}
and
R^{\prime}_{i}
have circle vertices. In the special case where
R_{i}
or
R^{\prime}_{i}
has a trivial shape, it follows from the fact that all trivial shapes have coefficient 1 in
\alpha,\beta
, due to their consistency.

If we count all sets of the form
\{(R_{1},R_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,(R_{t},R_{t}^{\prime})\}
satisfying the above conditions with coefficient
\prod_{i=1}^{t}c_{\alpha}(R_{i})c_{\beta}(R_{i}^{\prime})
, then we get the coefficient of
R
in
[\gamma]^{D}
. This follows by simply expanding the definition of
[\gamma]^{D}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Assuming
D_{trunc}\geq 20D^{2}
,
Q_{SSD}=[Q_{SS}]^{D}
.

Proof.

Since
L_{SSD}=[L_{SS}]^{D}
,
L^{\top}_{SSD}=[L^{\top}_{SS}]^{D}
, and
L_{SS}\star Q_{SS}\star L^{\top}_{SS}=(L+Q_{0}+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})_{SS}
(
Lemma

4.23
), by applying Lemma
6.4
we have:

L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[Q_{SS}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}=[(L+Q_{0}+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})_{SS}]^{D}.

(75)

From the definition of
L,Q_{0}
and
M
,
[(L+Q_{0}+L^{\top}-2\mathrm{Id})_{SS}]^{D}={M}_{\text{SSD}}
. Putting these pieces together, we have that
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[Q_{SS}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}={M}_{\text{SSD}}
.

Now consider the following linear equation in
X
in the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of simple spider disjoint unions with multiplication
*_{\mathrm{wb}}
:

L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}X*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}={M}_{\text{SSD}}.

(76)

By the paragraph above,
[{Q}_{\text{SS}}]^{D}
satisfies this equation. By
Lemma

4.25
,
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
also satisfies it when
D_{trunc}\geq 20D^{2}
. However, the kernel of this linear form is zero. To see this, assume
Y\neq 0
and consider the smallest nonzero SSD shape
\alpha
in
Y
(under any total order on SSD shapes that respects the number of edges). The only term in
L_{SSD}*_{\mathrm{wb}}Y*_{\mathrm{wb}}L^{\top}_{SSD}
that results in
\alpha
is
\mathrm{Id}*_{\mathrm{wb}}\alpha*_{\mathrm{wb}}\mathrm{Id}
, so the coefficient of
\alpha
in the result is the same as in
Y
, and
Y
cannot be in the kernel. Thus, equation (
76
) uniquely determines
X
so
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}=[{Q}_{\text{SS}}]^{D}
.
∎

Recall that by Lemma
5.22
,
Q_{SS}=\alpha_{0}\star\alpha_{0}^{\top}
for some
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
that is left, good and consistent. By Lemma
6.4
and Lemma
6.6
, we have that

Q_{SSD}=[Q_{SS}]^{D}=[\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}.

(77)

Using this, we can write

\displaystyle Q_{SSD}

\displaystyle=[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}+\left([\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}-[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}\right).

(78)

To prove that the expression (
78
) is positive-definite, we show:

1.

[\alpha_{0}]^{D}
is not too close to being singular; and

2.

\lVert([\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}-[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}\rVert
is small.

We prove item
2
in Section
Section

7
(
Lemma

7.59
). Here, we prove item
1
by the following lemma where we recall that
c_{\alpha}(i,j;0)
denotes the coefficient of
S(i,j;0)
in
\alpha\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
.

Lemma 6.7

(Preserving well-conditionedness)
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
satisfies two conditions:

(i)

\gamma
is left, good, and consistent.

(ii)

\sigma_{\min}\left(\rho(\gamma)\right)>0
.

Then
\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}
exists, and it is left, good, and consistent.
If additionally there are positive numbers

A_{0},\ldots,A_{D},\ B_{0},\ldots,B_{D}

(79)

such that
A_{0}=B_{0}=1
,
A_{i}\geq\max\limits_{j\leq i}\big\{\left|c_{\gamma}(i,j;0)\right|\}
,
B_{i}\geq\max\limits_{j\leq i}\big\{\left|c_{\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}}(i,j;0)\right|\}
, and

A_{i}A_{j}\leq A_{i+j},\ \ B_{i}B_{j}\leq B_{i+j}\ \ \text{for all $i,j$ where $i+j\leq D$,}

(80)

then assuming
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{100D,\ 2k\log n\}
,
the following hold for
[\gamma]^{D}
,
[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}
as real matrices:

1.

[\gamma]^{D}\cdot[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}=\mathrm{Id}+E
where
\lVert E\rVert\leq A_{D}B_{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}
.

2.

\sigma_{\min}([\gamma]^{D})\geq\left(1-A_{D}B_{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}\right)B_{D}^{-1}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}
.

Proof.

We denote the direct-sum decomposition of
\gamma
by
\gamma=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{D}\gamma_{i}
, that is,
\gamma_{i}:=\rho^{-1}\left(\rho_{i}(\gamma)\right)
.

Note that condition
(ii)
implies
\rho_{0}(\gamma)
is invertible. Since
\gamma
is consistent, each
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
is a leading principal submatrix of
\rho_{0}(\gamma)
and so is also invertible, hence
\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
exists, and so does
\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}
by applying
\rho^{-1}
. The lower-triangularity of
\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
holds since it is the inverse of a lower-triangular matrix. Consequently,
\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}
is left. Moreover,
\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}
is consistent and good by a similar inspection of
\rho(\gamma)
as in the proof for
\alpha_{0}
in
Lemma

5.22
,
Item

2
.

Below, we prove items
1
and
2
of
Lemma

6.7
in three steps.

1. We upper bound the coefficient magnitudes in
[\gamma]^{D}
and
[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}
.

For a simple spider
S(a,b;u)
, define its heavy-side weight to be
{\mathrm{hw}}(S):=\max\{a+u,b+u\}
. For an SSD shape
\alpha
in
[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}
, the number
l
of its simple spider components is at most
D
, and the sum of the heavy-side weights of its components
\sum_{i=1}^{l}{\mathrm{hw}}_{i}
is at most
D
. So by the monotonicity of these numbers, we have
|c_{[\gamma]^{D}}(\alpha)|\leq\prod_{i=1}^{l}A_{{\mathrm{hw}}_{i}}
. By property (
80
) of
\{A_{i}\}
, the RHS as a function on
({\mathrm{hw}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathrm{hw}}_{l})
subject to
\sum_{i=1}^{l}{\mathrm{hw}}_{i}\leq D
is maximized at
(0,0,\ldots,D)
, thus

\left|{[\gamma]^{D}}\right|_{\infty}\leq A_{D}.

(81)

The same argument works for
c_{[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}}
using numbers
\{B_{i}\}
, giving the bound

\left|{[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}}\right|_{\infty}\leq B_{D}.

(82)

2. Proof of
Item

1
.

Since
[\gamma]^{D}
and
[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}
are linear combinations of disjoint unions of good simple spiders, we have

\displaystyle\lVert[\gamma]^{D}\cdot[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}-\mathrm{Id}\rVert

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:disj}}}{{=}}\lVert[\gamma]^{D}\cdot[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}-[\gamma]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\rVert

(83)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:alphazeroerror}}}{{\leq}}\left|{[\gamma]^{D}}\right|_{\infty}\cdot\left|{[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}}\right|_{\infty}\cdot D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}.

Plugging (
81
) and (
82
) into the above, we get
\lVert[\gamma]^{D}\cdot[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}-\mathrm{Id}\rVert\leq A_{D}B_{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}
.
Item

1
follows.

3. Proof of
Item

2
.
We first show a claim:

Claim 6.8
.

There are at most
5^{2D}
many non-equivalent SSDs having left and right index size
\leq D
.

Proof.

If
D\leq 3
, it is not hard to check this statement directly. If
D\geq 4
, We can bound the number of possible SSDs as follows. Each SSD can be represented by a set of nontrivial simple spiders of the form
S(i,j;0)
where
i+j>0
together with a trivial simple spider
S(0,0;u)
for some
u\geq 0
. We first specify the number of non-trivial simple spiders in the SSD. There are at most
D+1
choices for this as there are at most
D
non-trivial simple spiders in the SSD. For each non-trivial simple spider, we go through the square vertices one by one and specify the following data.

1.

Is the square vertex a left leg or a right leg?

2.

Is the square vertex the last square vertex of the current simple spider?

After specifying all of the non-trivial simple spiders, if there are fewer than
2D
square vertices then we specify whether there is another square vertex or not. If so, we increase
u
by
1
and then repeat this process if we still have fewer than
2D
square vertices. If not, we stop.

Since there are at most
2D
square vertices and there are at most
4
possibilities for each square vertex, the total number of possibilities is at most
(D+1)4^{2D}\leq 5^{2D}
.
∎

We now upper bound
\lVert[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\rVert
. Since each good SSD in
[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}
has total size at most
6D
, the norm of each (scaled) good SSD
\alpha
is at most
((2D)^{2}\cdot 2k\log n)^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}
by
Theorem

3.15
and the fact that
\lambda_{\alpha}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\alpha)=1
. Combining this,
Claim

6.8
, and the coefficient bound (
82
), we have:

\lVert[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\rVert\leq B_{D}5^{2D}(8kD^{2}\log n)^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}\leq B_{D}D_{trunc}^{32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}

(84)

where the last step used
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
and
D_{trunc}\geq\{2k\log n,\ 100D\}
.

Finally, recall that
[\gamma]^{D}=(\mathrm{Id}+E)\left([\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\right)^{-1}
by
Item

1
, so by taking
\sigma_{\min}([\gamma]^{D})
and applying the basic properties of
\sigma_{\min}
that
\sigma_{\min}(X^{-1})=1/\lVert X\rVert
,
\sigma_{\min}(XY)\geq\sigma_{\min}(X)\sigma_{\min}(Y)
and
\sigma_{\min}(X+Y)\geq\sigma_{\min}(X)-\lVert Y\rVert
, we have:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}\left([\gamma]^{D}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\lVert E\rVert)\cdot\sigma_{\min}\left(\left([\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=(1-\lVert E\rVert)\cdot\lVert[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\rVert^{-1}.

(85)

By plugging into (
85
) the bounds on
\lVert E\rVert
,
\lVert[\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}]^{D}\rVert
from
Item

1
and (
84
), we get

\sigma([\gamma]^{D})\geq\left(1-A_{D}B_{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}\right)B_{D}^{-1}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}.\qed

We now prove
Lemma

6.1
using
Lemma

6.7
.

See
6.1

Proof.

Recall that
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}=[Q_{SS}]^{D}=[\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}
, where
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
is as in (
77
).
We want to apply
Lemma

6.7
with
\gamma\leftarrow\alpha_{0}
and

A_{i}\leftarrow 2^{i(i+4)}\cdot C_{U}^{2i^{2}+2i},\ \ B_{i}\leftarrow 8^{i}\cdot C_{U}^{2i}C_{L}^{i/2}.

For this, we note that
A_{0}=B_{0}=1
, condition (
80
) holds, and
A_{i}\geq\max\limits_{j\leq i}\big\{\left|c_{\gamma}(i,j;0)\right|\}
,
B_{i}\geq\max\limits_{j\leq i}\big\{\left|c_{\gamma^{\star\text{-inv}}}(i,j;0)\right|\}
by (
52
) and (
53
). Thus,
Lemma

6.7
applies, whose
Item

2
yields:

\displaystyle[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}

\displaystyle\succ\left(1-(4C_{U})^{4D^{2}}C_{L}^{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}\right)(3C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}.

We can simplify this bound using the assumption
(4C_{U})^{4D^{2}}C_{L}^{D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}
(
72
). As a result,

[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}\succ 0.99\cdot(3C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}.

(86)

On the other hand,
[\alpha_{0}]^{D}
is good and
\left|{[\alpha_{0}]^{D}}\right|_{\infty}\leq 2^{D^{2}+4D}C_{U}^{D^{2}+2D}
(
Lemma

5.22
), so by
Lemma

7.59
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert[\alpha_{0}]^{D}\cdot([\alpha_{0}]^{D})^{\top}-[\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq(2C_{U})^{2D^{2}+4D}D_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}=n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}

(87)

where the second inequality used assumption (
72
).

By (
86
) and (
87
),
[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}=[\alpha_{0}]^{D}*_{\mathrm{wb}}([\alpha_{0}]^{D})\succ\left(0.9(3C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}-n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}\right)\mathrm{Id}
. Again, we can simplify this using
n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}<0.4\cdot(3C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}
by (
72
). As a result,

[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\succ\frac{1}{2}(3C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}\mathrm{Id}\succeq(5C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}\mathrm{Id}.

Finally, given (
72
), we have
(5C_{U}C_{L})^{-2D}D_{trunc}^{-32{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}\geq n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}
, so
Lemma

6.1
follows.
∎

7
Error Analysis

In this section, we upper bound the terms that we have omitted as “errors” in the matrix multiplications so far. This analysis does not depend on the results in
Section

5
or
Section

6
. The main conclusions of this section are that
Q
is well-approximated by
{Q}_{\text{SSD}}
up to an additive
n^{-\Omega(\varepsilon)}
error (
Theorem

7.43
) and that the other error terms are small (
Lemma

7.55
,
Lemma

7.56
, and
Lemma

7.59
). We prove these statements using
Theorem

7.43
, which is the main technical result of this section.

We will need to show in various situations that the product of two or more scaled shapes has a small norm. Here, the key idea is to keep track of the exponent over
n
from the product of the coefficients and from the norm bound in
Theorem

3.15
. The full analysis is involved (see
Section

7.3
and
Section

7.4
). To carry out this analysis, we first set up some definitions and properties.

7.1
Properties of Square Separators

We recall some familiar properties of vertex separators and vertex disjoint paths. Unless specified otherwise, all shapes have square-vertex indices and edges go between a circle and a square.

Lemma 7.1

(Menger’s theorem for bipartite graphs)
.

For any shape
\alpha
, the maximum number of square-disjoint paths is equal to the size of the minimum square separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

We will apply Menger’s theorem to a shape
\beta
which is created by replacing each circle vertex
x
in
\alpha
with a clique of
new
vertices
x_{1},...,x_{|V(\alpha)|+1}
and replacing each edge to
x
with
|V(\alpha)|+1
edges, one for each
x_{i}
.
The indices of
\beta
are
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
.
We observe the following two facts.

1.

If
P
is a set of square-disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
V_{\alpha}
in
\alpha
with maximum size and
Q
is a set of vertex disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
V_{\alpha}
in
\beta
with maximum size then
|P|=|Q|
. To see this, note that
|P|\leq|Q|
since from each path
p\in P
, we can get a path in
\beta
by following
p
while using a different new vertex each time when needed; this is possible because
|P|\leq|V(\alpha)|
and any path uses a vertex no more than once. The resulting paths are vertex-disjoint. Conversely, a vertex disjoint path set in
\beta
is automatically a square-disjoint path set in
\alpha
by collapsing the cliques to circle vertices, so
|Q|\leq|P|
. Thus,
|P|=|Q|
.

2.

If
S
is a minimum square separator of
\alpha
and
T
is a minimum vertex separator for
\beta
then
|S|=|T|
. To see this, note that
S
is also a vertex separator for
\beta
, so
|T|\leq|S|\leq|V(\alpha)|
. Conversely, we claim that
T
contains no
new
vertex. To see this, assume
T
contains some new vertex
z_{i}
. Since the clique of
z
has size greater than
|V(\alpha)|
and thus greater than
|T|
, there is some
z_{j}\notin T
so
T\backslash\{z_{i}\}
is already a vertex separator for
\beta
since we can replace
z_{i}
with
z_{j}
on any path connecting
U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}
. This contradicts the minimality of
T
. Thus, the claim holds. In particular,
T
is identified with a square-vertex set in
\alpha
. It follows that
T
is a square separator in
\alpha
, so
|T|\geq|S|
. Putting everything together,
|S|=|T|
.

Now Menger’s theorem says that
|Q|=|T|
in
\beta
(where we view all vertices as one sort).
Thus,
|P|=|Q|=|T|=|S|
and the proposition follows.
∎

Definition 7.2
.

Given a shape
\alpha
and vertex separators
S,T
, we say that
S
is to the left of
T
if
S
separates
U_{\alpha}
and
T
and we say that
S
is to the right of
T
if
S
separates
V_{\alpha}
and
T
.

Lemma 7.3

(Existence of leftmost and rightmost minimum vertex separators)
.

For all shapes
\alpha
, there exist unique leftmost and rightmost minimum weight separators of
\alpha
. Similarly, for all shapes
\alpha
, there exist unique leftmost and rightmost minimum square separators of
\alpha
.

Proof.

We prove this statement for minimum weight separators; the proof for minimum square separators is similar.
Given minimum weight separators
S
and
T
, let
L
be the set of vertices
v
such that
v\in S\cup T
and there is a path from
U_{\alpha}
to
v
which does not pass through any vertex in
(S\cup T)\setminus\{v\}
. Similarly, let
R
be the set of vertices
v
such that
v\in S\cup T
and there is a path from
U_{\alpha}
to
v
which does not pass through any vertex in
(S\cup T)\setminus\{v\}
. Note that both
L
and
R
are vertex separators of
\alpha
.

We now make the following observations:

1.

w(L)+w(R)-w(L\cap R)=w(L\cup R)\leq w(S\cup T)=w(S)+w(T)-w(S\cap T)
.

2.

L\cap R\subseteq S\cap T
. To see this, observe that if
v\in L\cap R
then there is a walk from
U_{\alpha}
to
V_{\alpha}
which passes through
v
and does not pass through any other vertices of
S\cup T
. Since
S
and
T
are vertex separators between
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
, we must have that
v\in S\cap T
.

3.

Since
S
and
T
have minimum weight,
w(L)\geq w(S)=w(T)
and
w(R)\geq w(S)=w(T)
.

Together, these observations imply that
L\cap R=S\cap T
and
w(L)=w(R)=w(S)=w(T)
.
∎

Corollary 7.4

(Expandability of vertex sets reachable by square-disjoint paths)
.

Suppose
\alpha
is a shape with indices
(U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha})
. If
X,Y\subseteq V(\alpha)
are square vertex sets such that
X\subseteq Y
and

1.

There exist
|X|
square-disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
X
;

2.

There exist
|U_{\alpha}|
square-disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
Y
.

Then there is a subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq Y
such that
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
,
|X^{\prime}|=|U_{\alpha}|
and there are
|U_{\alpha}|
square-disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
X^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We build up
X^{\prime}
by starting from
X
and adding one vertex at a time:

Lemma 7.5
.

For all
j\leq|U_{\alpha}|-|X|
, there is a set of vertices
X^{\prime}_{j}
such that
X\subseteq X^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq Y
,
|X^{\prime}_{j}|=|X|+j
and there are
|X^{\prime}_{j}|=|X|+j
square-disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha}
to
X^{\prime}_{j}
.

Proof.

We can prove this by induction. For the base case
j=0
, we take
X^{\prime}_{0}=X
. For the inductive step, assume that
j\in[0,|U_{\alpha}|-|X|-1]
and we have a set of vertices
X^{\prime}_{j}
which satisfies the inductive assumption. Let
S
be the leftmost minimum square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}
.

Observe that for each vertex
v\in Y\setminus X^{\prime}_{j}
, one of the following two cases must hold:

1.

S
is a square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
.

2.

There are
|X^{\prime}_{j}|+1
square-disjoint paths between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
.

To see this, assume that there are only
|X^{\prime}_{j}|
vertex disjoint paths between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
. If so, then by
Lemma

7.1
there is a minimum square vertex separator
S^{\prime}
between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
of size
|X^{\prime}_{j}|
. Since
S^{\prime}
is also a square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}
and
S
is the leftmost minimum square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}
,
S
must be a square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
S^{\prime}
which implies that
S
is a square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
.

We now observe that the first case cannot hold for all vertices
v\in Y\setminus X^{\prime}_{j}
as otherwise
S
would be a square vertex separator between
U_{\alpha}
and
Y
but this is impossible as
|S|\leq|X^{\prime}_{j}|<|U_{\alpha}|
. Thus, the second case must hold for some vertex
v\in X\setminus X^{\prime}_{j}
so we can take
X^{\prime}_{j+1}=X^{\prime}_{j}\cup\{v\}
.
∎

Corollary
7.4
follows from the lemma by taking
X^{\prime}=X^{\prime}_{|U_{\alpha}|-|X|}
.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose
\gamma
is a left shape and
V\subseteq V(\gamma)
is a vertex set such that
U_{\gamma}
is the leftmost minimum square vertex separator between
U_{\gamma}
and
V
. Then we can find square-disjoint paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{|U_{\gamma}|}
such that:

1.

For all
j\in[|V_{\gamma}|]
,
P_{j}
starts at a square vertex in
U_{\gamma}
and ends at a square vertex in
V_{\gamma}
. No other vertices in
P_{j}
are in
U_{\gamma}\cup V_{\gamma}
.

2.

For all
j\in[|V_{\gamma}|+1,|U_{\gamma}|]
,
P_{j}
starts at a square vertex in
U_{\gamma}
and ends at a vertex
v_{j}\in V
. Moreover,
v_{j}
is the only vertex on
P_{j}
which is in
V
and no vertices in
P_{j}
except the starting vertex are in
U_{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
Y
be the set of square vertices which are either in
V
or are adjacent to a vertex in
V
. We apply Corollary
7.4
with
X=V_{\gamma}
and
Y
.

For each of the square-disjoint paths
P
given by Corollary
7.4
, we take the segment of
P
starting from the last vertex in
U
. For the paths ending at a vertex in
V_{\gamma}
, we take the entire path segment. For the other paths, we stop at the next square vertex
v
which is in
V
or adjacent to a vertex in
V
. If
v\notin V
then we extend this path by an edge from
v
to a vertex in
V
.
∎

7.2
Strategy for Analyzing Error Terms

Recall that each shape
\alpha
has a scaling coefficient
\lambda_{\alpha}={n^{-{w(E(\alpha))\over 2}}}
(
Definition

3.4
). Our strategy for analyzing error terms is to show that for each term
\tau^{\prime}
(which could either be a proper middle shape
\tau
or a term
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
which results from an intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
), we can redistribute the factors in
\lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}
times its approximate norm bound (see
Definition

7.7
below) so that the factors are split between the vertices of
\tau^{\prime}
and the factor for each vertex
v\in V(\tau^{\prime})
is at most
n^{-\mathrm{slack}(v)}
for some carefully chosen slack function
\mathrm{slack}(v)
. We will choose
\mathrm{slack}(v)
so that it is always non-negative and whenever
\tau^{\prime}
is an error term, there is at least one vertex
v
such that
\mathrm{slack}(v)
is
-\Omega(\varepsilon)
.

For our analysis, we need a few definitions.

Definition 7.7

(
A
-norm)
.

For each shape
\alpha
, we define its
approximate norm upper bound
to be
{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\alpha):=n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))+w(I_{s}(\alpha))-w(S_{\alpha})}{2}}
, where
I_{s}(\alpha)
is the set of isolated vertices in
\alpha
and
S_{\alpha}
is a minimum weight separator of
\alpha
.

In order to redistribute the factors in
\lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\tau^{\prime})
, it is very useful to consider whether the vertices in
\tau^{\prime}
are connected to
U_{\tau^{\prime}}
or
V_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Definition 7.8

(Left-connected and right-connected vertices)
.

Given a (possibly improper) shape
\tau^{\prime}
and a minimum weight vertex separator
S
for
\tau^{\prime}
, we say that a vertex
v\in V(\tau^{\prime})
is left-connected if there is a path
P
from
U_{\tau^{\prime}}
to
v
which does not contain any vertices in
S\setminus\{v\}
. Similarly, we say that a vertex
v\in V(\tau^{\prime})
is right-connected if there is a path
P
from
v
to
V_{\tau^{\prime}}
which does not contain any vertices in
S\setminus\{v\}
.

In our analysis, we will show that for each shape
\alpha
, we can automatically assign some slack to the square vertices of
\alpha
which are not in
U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
or have a larger degree than expected.

Definition 7.9

(Default slack of a shape)
.

Given a shape
\alpha
, we define the default slack of a square vertex
v
to be:

1.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v):=\frac{\deg(v)}{4}
.

2.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
or
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v):=\frac{\deg(v)-1}{4}
.

3.

If
v\in V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha})
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v):=\frac{(\deg(v)-2)}{4}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\deg(v)}{8}
.

We define
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}(\alpha)=\sum_{v\in V_{\square}(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)}
.
We define the default slack for circle vertices to be
0
.

7.3
Analyzing Middle Shapes

We first show how to use this strategy to analyze proper middle shapes
\tau
.

Definition 7.10

(Extra slack of proper middle shapes)
.

Given a proper middle shape
\tau
and a minimum weight vertex separator
S
for
\tau
, we define the extra slack of a circle vertex
v\in V(\tau)
with respect to
S
to be
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\tau,S}(v)=\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}
if
v\notin S
and
0
if
v\in S
. For square vertices
v\in V(\alpha)
, we take
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\tau,S}(v)=\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
if
v\in S\setminus(U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau})
and
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\tau,S}(v)=0
otherwise. We define
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau,S)
to be

\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau,S)=\sum_{v\in V(\tau)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\tau,S}(v)}=\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}|V_{\bigcirc}(\alpha)\setminus S|+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}|(V_{\square}(\tau)\cap S)\setminus(U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau})|.

We define
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau)
to be the maximum of
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau,S)
over all minimum weight vertex separators
S
of
\tau
.

Definition 7.11

(Slack of proper middle shapes)
.

Given a proper middle shape
\tau
and a minimum weight vertex separator
S
for
\tau
, we define
\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)=\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}(v)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\tau,S}(v)
.

We define the total slack for
\tau
to be
\mathrm{slack}(\tau)=\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}(\tau)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau)
. Equivalently,
\mathrm{slack}(\tau)
is the maximum of
\sum_{v\in V(\tau)}{slack_{\tau,S}(v)}
over all minimum weight vertex separators
S
of
\tau
.

Lemma 7.12

(Error analysis of proper middle shapes)
.

For any proper middle shape
\tau
in which all circle vertices have degree at least
k
and all square vertices have total degree at least 2,
\lambda_{\tau}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\tau)\leq n^{-\mathrm{slack}(\tau)}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a minimum weight vertex separator of
\tau
which maximizes
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}(\tau,S)
. If there are edges with a label
l>1
, for the purposes of this argument we view each such edge
e
as
l
copies of
e
.

We can interpret
\lambda_{\tau}
as giving a factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
to each edge. Similarly, we can interpret
{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\tau)
as giving a factor of
n^{\mathrm{target}(v)}
to each vertex
v
where
\mathrm{target}(v)
is defined below. Our goal is to redistribute the factor for each edge to its two endpoints so that the total factor given to each vertex
v
is at most
n^{-\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)}
.

Definition 7.13

(Target factors for a proper middle shape)
.

Given a square vertex
v\in V(\tau)
, we define
\mathrm{target}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
if
v\notin S
and
\mathrm{target}(v)=0
if
v\in S
. Similarly, given a circle vertex
w\in V(\tau)
, we define
\mathrm{target}(w)=\frac{1}{2}\log_{n}(m)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
if
w\notin S
and
\mathrm{target}(w)=0
if
w\in S
.

Since
\tau
is a proper middle shape, there are
|U_{\tau}|
square-vertex-disjoint paths (possibly of length
0
) from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
and each vertex in
U_{\tau}\cup V_{\tau}
is an endpoint for exactly one of these paths. For each edge
e\in E(\tau)
, we distribute the
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
factor on
e
as follows.

If
e=\{u,v\}
is on one of the paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
and goes from
u
to
v
on this path,

1.

If
u
and
v
are left-connected and
u\notin S
, we have
e
point from
v
to
u
. Note that this is the opposite direction as the path containing
e
.

2.

If
u
and
v
are right-connected and
v\notin S
, we have
e
point from
u
to
v
. Note that these two cases cannot happen simultaneously as this would imply that there is a path from
U_{\alpha}
to
V_{\alpha}
which does not go through any vertices of
S
.

3.

If neither of the above cases holds then we do not assign a direction to
e
.

If
e
is not on one of the paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
then we do not assign a direction to
e
.

u_{1}
u_{2}
w
v_{1}
v_{2}
u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
w_{1}
x
w_{2}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}

Figure 8:
Edge factor assignments in middle shapes. The top shape is a dominant shape, while the bottom one is not. Vertices in red form a minimum weight separator.

Roughly speaking, we assign the
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
factor for each edge to the endpoint which it points to if it has a direction and we split the factor evenly between the endpoints if it does not have a direction. However, we modify this idea slightly in order to ensure each vertex has the desired slack.

Definition 7.14
.

For each edge, we redistribute its factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
between its endpoints as follows.

1.

For each edge pointing from a circle vertex to a square vertex, we assign a factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
to the square vertex and a factor of
1
to the circle vertex.

2.

For each edge pointing from a square vertex to a circle vertex, we assign a factor of
n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}
to the square vertex and a factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}
to the circle vertex.

3.

For edges which don’t point in either direction, we assign a factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}
to the square endpoint of
e
and a factor of
n^{-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}
to the circle endpoint.

For each vertex
v\in V(\tau)
, we define
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
so that the product of the edge factors assigned to
v
is
n^{-{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)}
.

Lemma 7.15
.

For all vertices
v\in V(\tau)
,
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)
.

Proof.

The key observation is that whenever a path passes through a vertex
v
which is not in
S
, either one of the two edges on the path incident to
v
points to
v
or neither edge is given a direction.

Proposition 7.16
.

If
v\notin S
and
\{u,v\}
and
\{v,w\}
are two edges on one of the paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
then one of the following cases holds:

1.

\{u,v\}
points from
u
to
v
.

2.

\{v,w\}
points from
w
to
v
.

3.

Neither
\{u,v\}
nor
\{v,w\}
is given a direction.

Proof.

If
v
is left-connected, then
w
is left-connected as well so the edge
\{v,w\}
points from
w
to
v
. Similarly, if
v
is right-connected, then
u
is right-connected so the edge
\{u,v\}
points from
u
to
v
. If
v
is neither left-connected nor right-connected, then neither
\{u,v\}
nor
\{v,w\}
is given a direction.
∎

We now make the following observations:

1.

If
v
is a circle vertex and
v\notin S
,
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\deg(v)(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\varepsilon}{8})
. To see this, observe that each edge which points away from
v
contributes
0
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
, each edge which points towards
v
contributes
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
, and each edge incident to
v
which does not have a direction contributes
\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. By
Proposition

7.16
, for each edge which points away from
v
, there must be another edge which points towards
v
. Thus, on average, each edge incident to
v
contributes at least
\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
which implies that
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\deg(v)(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\varepsilon}{8})
. Since
\mathrm{target}(v)=\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
and
\deg(v)\geq k
,
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)=\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

If
v
is a circle vertex and
v\in S
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq 0
,
\mathrm{target}(v)=0
, and
\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,P}(v)=0
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)
.

3.

If
v
is a square vertex and
v\notin S
then
\mathrm{target}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
. We observe that if there is a path
P
passing through
v
, the two edges incident to
v
contribute
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. If
v
is an endpoint of this path, the edge incident to
v
will point to
v
and thus contribute
\frac{1}{2}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. Each other edge incident to
v
contributes
\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. We have the following cases:

(a)

If
v\in(U_{\tau}\setminus V_{\tau})\cup(V_{\tau}\setminus U_{\tau})
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\frac{1}{2}+(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})(\deg(v)-1)
so

{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})(\deg(v)-1)\geq\frac{\deg(v)-1}{4}

(b)

If
v\notin U_{\tau}\cup V_{\tau}
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\deg(v)(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})
so

{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\frac{\deg(v)-2}{4}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\deg(v)}{8}.

4.

If
v\in S
then
\mathrm{target}(v)=0
. If there is a path passing through
v
, the two edges of this path which are incident to
v
contribute at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. If
v
is an endpoint of a path then the edge of this path incident to
v
contributes at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. Other edges incident to
v
contribute
\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
to
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)
. We have the following cases:

(a)

If
v\in U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})\deg(v)
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\frac{\deg(v)}{4}
.

(b)

If
v\in(U_{\tau}\setminus V_{\tau})\cup(V_{\tau}\setminus U_{\tau})
, then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})(\deg(v)-1)
so

{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})(\deg(v)-1)\geq\frac{\deg(v)-1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

(c)

If
v\notin U_{\tau}\cup V_{\tau}
, then
{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+(\deg(v)-2)(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8})
so

{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)-\mathrm{target}(v)\geq\frac{\deg(v)-2}{4}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\deg(v)}{8}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Lemma

7.15
is proved.
∎

Putting everything together,

\lambda_{\tau}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\tau)=\prod_{v\in V(\tau)}{n^{\mathrm{target}(v)-{\mathrm{assigned}}(v)}}\leq n^{-\sum_{v\in V(\tau)}{\mathrm{slack}_{\tau,S}(v)}}=n^{-\mathrm{slack}(\tau)}.

Lemma

7.12
is proved.
∎

7.4
Analyzing Intersection Configurations
