In this subsection, we study the quantitative properties of intersection configurations. As described in
Section

7.5.1
, our techniques can also be used to analyze product configurations
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
(see
Definition

4.11
) where both
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are good.

We start by recasting the definition of intersections (
Definition

3.37
), with a slightly expanded scope. We introduce some additional terminology (such as vertex equivalence relations) which will be convenient for our later discussions. One modification from the original
Definition

3.37
is that we will assign the label for each edge specified by
\mathcal{P}
post-linearization to be the minimal possible value. This specification will be adequate for the purpose of upper bounding the norm of the resulting term.

Definition 7.17

(Intersection configuration)
.

An intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
is a sequence of shapes
(\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j})
where
j\geq 1
, together with a vertex equivalence relation
\equiv
such that:

1.

In every shape in the sequence, each square vertex has total degree at least
2
, and each circle vertex has degree at least
k
.

2.

\tau
is a proper middle shape in
Q_{0}
.

3.

For each
i\in[1,j]
,
\gamma_{i}
is a left shape in
L
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
is a right shape in
L^{\top}
. Moreover, for each
i\in[j]
, at least one of
\gamma_{i}
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
is non-trivial.

4.

U_{\gamma_{i}}=V_{\gamma_{i+1}}
and
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}=U_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i+1}}
for all
i\in[0,j-1]
where we take
\gamma_{0}:={\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{0}:=\tau
. There are no other vertex identifications between the shapes.

5.

The equivalence relation
\equiv
on
\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{j}\left(V(\gamma_{i})\cup V({\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}^{\top})\right)\cup V(\tau)
satisfies the following conditions.

(a)

If
v\equiv w
then either
v
and
w
are both circle vertices or
v
and
w
are both square vertices.

(b)

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
, the restriction of
\equiv
to
\alpha
is trivial.

(c)

\equiv
satisfies the following
intersection-separation condition
. For each
i\in[j]
, if we take
Int(\gamma_{i})
to be the set of vertices in
V(\gamma_{i})\setminus V_{\gamma_{i}}
which are equivalent to a vertex in
\left(\cup_{i^{\prime}\in[i-1]}{V(\gamma_{i^{\prime}})}\right)\cup V(\tau)\cup\left(\cup_{i^{\prime\prime}\in[i]}{V({\gamma_{i^{\prime\prime}}}^{\top})}\right)
then
U_{\gamma_{i}}
is a minimum square separator between
U_{\gamma_{i}}
and
Int(\gamma_{i})\cup V_{\gamma_{i}}
. Similarly, if we take
Int({\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top})
to be the set of vertices in
V({\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top})\setminus U_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}}
which are equivalent to a vertex in
\left(\cup_{i^{\prime}\in[i]}{V(\gamma_{i})}\right)\cup V(\tau)\cup\left(\cup_{i^{\prime\prime}\in[i-1]}{V({\gamma^{\prime}_{i^{\prime\prime}}}^{\top})}\right)
then
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}}
is a minimum square separator between
U_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}}\cup Int({\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top})
and
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{i}}^{\top}}
.

We call
j
the length of
\mathcal{P}
. We call
\gamma_{j},\dots,\tau,\dots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}
the shapes in
\mathcal{P}
, which are distinguished from each other. The vertex set of
\mathcal{P}
is
V(\mathcal{P}):=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{j}\left(V(\gamma_{i})\cup V({\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}^{\top})\right)\cup V(\tau)
, the edge set of
\mathcal{P}
is
E(\mathcal{P}):=\bigsqcup\limits_{i=1}^{j}\left(E(\gamma_{i})\sqcup E({\gamma_{i}^{\prime}}^{\top})\right)\sqcup E(\tau)
where
\sqcup
means disjoint union and each edge carries the label from the corresponding shape. We define the total size of
\mathcal{P}
to be
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P}):=|V(\mathcal{P})|+w(E(\mathcal{P}))
.

Finally, we define the
result of
\mathcal{P}
to be the following shape
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. First we take the quotient of the union of shapes in
\mathcal{P}
by
\equiv
, which can have multi-edges. Then we replace all edges between each pair of vertex classes with a single edge, where if the edge labels are
i_{1},...,i_{t}
, then the label of this edge is the minimal
i
such that
h_{i}
appears in the linear expansion of
\prod\limits_{l=1}^{t}h_{i_{l}}
in the basis
\{h_{l}\mid l\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Note that
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
may have isolated vertices but no multi-edges.

Note that vertices in each shape in
\mathcal{P}
always stay distinct under
\equiv
. Also, for any fixed equivalence class, there are at most
2j+1
many vertices in that class.

Notation.

We will call an intersection configuration just a configuration. Given a configuration, we use
[v]
to denote the equivalence class of
v
under
\equiv
, which is also viewed as a vertex in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. We let
[U]:=\cup_{v\in U}[v]
if
U\subseteq V(\mathcal{P})
. We use symbols like
v,w,v^{\prime}
to denote vertices in
V(\mathcal{P})
and use square-bracketed symbols like
[v],[w],[v^{\prime}]
to denote vertex classes, or vertices in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. We write
v\in[w]
to mean that vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
is in class
[w]
under
\equiv
.

For each configuration
\mathcal{P}
, we fix an arbitrary minimum weight separator
S
in the result
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. For
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
, we let
\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v)
be the sum of the degrees of
v
over all shapes in
\mathcal{P}
that contains
v
. For a class
[v]
,
\deg_{\mathcal{P}}([v]):=\sum\limits_{v^{\prime}\in[v]}\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v^{\prime})
.

As in
Definition

3.38
, we let
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
denote the set of all distinct intersection configurations on
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
. We also recall the definitions of the scaling coefficient
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
and the Hermite coefficient
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
. In particular, recall that
\lambda_{P}:=\lambda_{\gamma_{j}}\cdots\lambda_{\tau}\cdots\lambda_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}=n^{-\sum_{e\in E(\mathcal{P})}l(e)/2}
.

Definition 7.18

(Side size of
\mathcal{P}
)
.

The side size of a configuration
\mathcal{P}=\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
is
D_{\mathrm{side}}:=\max\{|U_{\gamma_{j}}|,\dots,|U_{\tau}|,\dots,|U_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}|,|V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}|\}
.

Remark 7.19
.

To visualize a configuration
\mathcal{P}
, we can draw the sequence of shapes
(\gamma_{j},\dots,\tau,\dots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j})
from left to right, one stacked after another, identifying
V_{\gamma_{i}}=U_{\gamma_{i+1}}
,
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}=U_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i+1}}
for all
i\in[0,j-1]
where
\gamma_{0}:={\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{0}:=\tau
.
We then draw dotted lines between different vertices identified under
\equiv
. See
Figure

9
for an example.

u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
w_{1}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
w_{2}
v
\times
v
w_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
w_{4}
v^{\prime}_{1}
v^{\prime}_{2}
v^{\prime}_{3}

Figure 9:
An intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
of length 1. The trivial shape in the middle is omitted.

We extend the notion of a vertex being left-connected (see
Definition

7.8
) to configurations.

Definition 7.20
.

We call a vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
left-connected if there is a path in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
from
[U_{\gamma_{j}}]
to
[v]
which does not intersect any vertex of
S\setminus[v]
. Similarly, we call
v
right-connected if there is a path from
[v]
to
[V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}]
in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
which does not intersect any vertex of
S\setminus[v]
.

7.4.1
Slack for intersection configurations

For this section, we assume that we have an intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
and a minimum weight vertex separator
S
for
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.bFor our analysis, it is very useful to consider the first and last times that each vertex appears non-trivially.

Definition 7.21

(Non-trivial appearances)
.

Given
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
and a vertex
v
such that
v\in V(\alpha)
, we say that
v
appears in
\alpha^{\prime}
if there is a vertex
v^{\prime}\in V(\alpha^{\prime})
in the same equivalence class as
v
. If
v^{\prime}\notin U_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap V_{\alpha^{\prime}}
then we say
v
appears non-trivially in
\alpha^{\prime}
. If
v^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap V_{\alpha^{\prime}}
then we say that
v
appears trivially in
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Similarly, we say a vertex
[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
appears non-trivially in
\alpha
if there is a vertex
v^{\prime}\in V(\alpha)
such that
v^{\prime}\in[v]
. If
v^{\prime}\notin U_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap V_{\alpha^{\prime}}
then we say
[v]
appears non-trivially in
\alpha^{\prime}
. If
v^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap V_{\alpha^{\prime}}
then we say that
[v]
appears trivially in
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Note that if
v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
then we say that
v
appears trivially in
\alpha
even if
v
is incident to edges in
E(\alpha)
.

Fact 7.22
.

If
v
is a circle vertex and
v
appears in
\alpha
then
v
appears non-trivially in
\alpha
.

Fact 7.23
.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
then either
v
appears non-trivially in an earlier shape or there is a vertex
v^{\prime}
in the same equivalence class as
v
such that
v^{\prime}\in U_{\gamma_{j}}
. Similarly, if
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
then either
v
appears non-trivially in a later shape or there is a vertex
v^{\prime}
in the same equivalence class as
v
such that
v^{\prime}\in V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
.

We now define the slack for the vertices in
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
.

Definition 7.24

(Default slack)
.

We define
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)}}
.

Definition 7.25

(Extra slack)
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the extra slack for vertices
v\in V(\alpha)
as follows.

1.

If
v
is a circle vertex, we take
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
if
[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivially in both an earlier and a later shape. Otherwise, we take
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{k{\varepsilon}}{16}
.

2.

If
v
is a square vertex, we take
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
if
v\notin U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
and at least one of the following holds:

(a)

[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivially in both an earlier and a later shape.

(b)

v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
and
v
appears in a later shape (note that at least one such appearance must be non-trivial).

(c)

v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
and
v
appears in an earlier shape (note that at least one such appearance must be non-trivial).

(d)

v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
,
v
appears non-trivially in an earlier shape, and
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

(e)

v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
,
v
appears non-trivially in a later shape, and
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Otherwise, we take
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
.

We define
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{S}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)}}
. Then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
is defined to be the maximum of
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{S}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
over all minimum weight vertex separators
S
of
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Remark 7.26
.

Note that conditions
1
and (
2a
) are similar to the extra slack for proper middle shapes except that the condition “
v\in S
” is replaced by the condition that
[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivially in both an earlier and a later shape and we divide the extra slack by a factor of
2
in order to avoid double counting. This is necessary as there are a few cases where a vertex has slack in two different shapes
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
but only obtains extra edge factor(s) from the edges in one of these shapes.

We include conditions (
2b
), (
2c
), (
2d
), and (
2e
) in order to show that the only configurations with zero slack are well-behaved intersection configurations.

Definition 7.27
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and
v\in V(\alpha)
, we define
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)
.

Definition 7.28

(Slack of
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
)
.

We define
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}):=\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
. Note that
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})=\max\limits_{\text{Minimum weight separators $S$ of ${\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}$}}{\{\sum\limits\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)}}\}}
.

Lemma 7.29

(Key lemma for error analysis)
.

For all intersection configurations
\mathcal{P}
,

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\leq n^{-\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}.

Proof.

We prove this lemma using a similar analysis as we used to prove Lemma
7.12
. In particular, we show that we can assign directions to the edges of
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}
so that if we use the redistribution scheme of Definition
7.14
, the total factor for each vertex
v
is at most
n^{-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}(\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v))}
.
In order to assign directions to the edges of
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}
, we need a few more definitions and observations.

In this proof, we fix an arbitrary minimum weight vertex separator
S
for
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. We fix sets of paths for
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1}
and
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
in
\mathcal{P}
given by Corollary
7.6
as well as
|U_{\tau}|
many square-disjoint paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
. We orient all these paths from left to right. We define
anchor vertices
for these paths as follows.

Definition 7.30

(Anchor vertices)
.

For each chosen path
P
in
\gamma_{i}
, we define the
left anchor vertex

u_{P}
for
P
to be the
last
vertex on the path that appears in
U_{\gamma_{i}}
or in an earlier shape. We then define the
right anchor vertex

v_{P}
for
P
to be the
first
vertex equal to or after
u_{P}
that appears in
V_{\gamma_{i}}
or a later shape.

Similarly, for each path
P
in
\tau
, we define the left anchor vertex
u_{P}
for
P
to be the last vertex on the path that appears in
U_{\tau}
or in an earlier shape. We then define the right anchor vertex
v_{P}
for
P
to be the first vertex equal to or after
u_{P}
that appears in
V_{\tau}
or a later shape.

For each path
P
in
{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}
, we define the right anchor vertex
v_{P}
for
P
to be the
first
vertex on the path (going from left to right) that appears in
V_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}
or in a later shape. We then define the left anchor vertex
u_{P}
for
P
to be the
last
vertex equal to or before
v_{P}
that appears in
U_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}
or an earlier shape.

We now assign directions to the edges of
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}
. For each shape
\alpha\in{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
and each chosen path
P
in
\alpha
(going from left to right):

1.

For each edge
e=(u,v)
to the left of
u_{P}
, have
e
point to
u
.

2.

For each edge
e=(u,v)
to the right of
v_{P}
, have
e
point to
v
.

3.

For each edge
e=(u,v)
between
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
, if one of its endpoints is in
S
, we have
e
point to the other endpoint (if both endpoints are in
S
, the choice is arbitrary). Otherwise, we have
e
point to
u
if both
u
and
v
are left-connected in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
and we have
e
point to
v
if neither
u
nor
v
is left-connected in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Notice that the last item is well-defined. This is because each edge
e=(u,v)
on
P
between
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
gives rise to a single edge in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
(in particular the edge won’t disappear). Thus, if neither
u
nor
v
is in
S
then either both
u
and
v
are left-connected or neither
u
nor
v
are left-connected.

Edges which are not on one of the chosen paths are not given a direction.

u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
w_{1}
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
w_{2}
v
w_{3}
y_{1}
y_{2}
y_{3}
w_{4}
v^{\prime}_{1}
v^{\prime}_{2}
v^{\prime}_{3}

Figure 10:
This figure shows the directions of the edges for the configuration
\mathcal{P}
in
Figure

9
. The vertices in the minimum weight vertex separator
S
of
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
are shown in red.

There are two key features of this scheme.

1.

For each shape
\alpha
, all of the vertices
v\in V(\alpha)
except for the anchor vertices obtain the needed number of edge factors from the edges in
E(\alpha)
.

2.

For each path
P
such that the left anchor vertex
u_{P}
is not equal to the right anchor vertex
v_{P}
, neither
u_{P}
nor
v_{P}
is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
. In fact, none of the vertices between
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
are isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
but we only need this fact for
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
.

Definition 7.31

(Guarded)
.

We call a vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
guarded if it appears between
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
, including
u_{P}
and
v_{P}
, on some chosen path
P
where
u_{P}\neq v_{P}
.

Observation 7.1
.

If
v
is guarded, then
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Definition 7.32

(Satisfied)
.

For each shape
\alpha
in
\mathcal{P}
and each vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
such that
[v]\notin S
, we say that
v
is satisfied in
\alpha
if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

1.

v\notin U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
and there are at least as many edges pointing towards
v
as there are edges pointing away from
v
. Note if we split the factor for each edge using the scheme in Definition
7.14
,
v
is guaranteed to obtain roughly
\frac{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}{2}
edge factors from the edges in
E(\alpha)
as edges which do not have a direction split their factor roughly evenly between their endpoints.

2.

v\in(U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha})\cup(V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha})
and there are more edges pointing towards
v
than there are edges pointing away from
v
.
v
. Note that if we split the factor for each edge using the scheme in Definition
7.14
,
v
is guaranteed to obtain roughly
\frac{\deg_{\alpha}(v)+1}{2}
edge factors from the edges in
E(\alpha)
.

Here,
\deg_{\alpha}(v)
means the degree of
v
in
\alpha
.

We always assume
k\geq 2
so the degree of every circle vertex in every shape is at least
2
.

Lemma 7.33
.

Let
\alpha
be a shape in
\mathcal{P}
and
v\in V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})
. If
[v]\notin S
and
v
is not satisfied in
\alpha
, then one of the following cases holds.

1.

v
is the left anchor vertex for some path and is not left-connected.

2.

v
is the right anchor vertex for some path and is left-connected.

3.

v
is both the left and the right anchor vertex for some path.

Proof.

We have the following cases:

1.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
then one of the paths
P
for
\alpha
will be the degenerate path
P=\{v\}
and we will have that
v=u_{P}=v_{P}
.

2.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
then there will be a path
P
starting from
v
. Unless
v
is the left anchor vertex of
P
and is not left-connected, the first edge of
P
will point to
v
so
v
will be satisfied.

3.

If
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
then there will be a path
P
ending at
v
. Unless
v
is the right anchor vertex of
P
and is left-connected, the last edge of
P
will point to
v
so
v
will be satisfied.

4.

If
v\in V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha})
then for each path
P
containing
v
, letting
u
be the vertex before
v
on
P
and letting
w
be the vertex after
v
on
P
, we observe that

(a)

The edge
(u,v)
will point to
v
if
v
comes after
v_{P}
or
v
comes after
u_{P}
and is not left-connected.

(b)

The edge
(v,w)
will point to
v
if
v
comes before
u_{P}
or
v
comes before
v_{P}
and is left-connected.

This implies that if neither of the edges
(u,v)
and
(v,w)
points to
v
then either
v=u_{P}
and
v
is not left-connected,
v=v_{P}
and
v
is left-connected, or
v=u_{P}=v_{P}
. Thus, if none of the cases of Lemma
7.33
hold for
v
then there will be at least as many edges pointing to
v
as there are edges pointing away from
v
so
v
will be satisfied.

Lemma

7.33
is proved.
∎

We now give the slack analysis for
Lemma

7.29
.
For this analysis, we need to divide the factor for each vertex
v\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
among all of the different shapes
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
such that
v
appears in
\alpha
. We do this as follows.

Definition 7.34

(Target factors for an intersection configuration)
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and
v\in V(\alpha)
,

1.

If
[v]\in S
then we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
such that
v\in V(\alpha)
.

2.

If
[v]\notin S
and
v
only appears non-trivially in one shape
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
then we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{\log_{n}(m)}{2}\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
if
v
is a circle vertex. Note that in this case,
v
cannot be isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

3.

If
[v]\notin S
and
v
appears non-trivially in at least two different shapes in
\mathcal{P}
, let
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
be the first and last shapes that
v
appears in non-trivially. We have the following cases:

(a)

If
v
is the left anchor vertex of a chosen path
P
for
\alpha
and
v
is not left-connected then we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
and we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
if
v
is a square vertex and
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{\log_{n}(m)}{2}\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
if
v
is a circle vertex.

(b)

Similarly, if
v
is the right anchor vertex of a chosen path
P
for
\alpha^{\prime}
and
v
is left-connected then we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=0
and we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
if
v
is a square vertex and
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{\log_{n}(m)}{2}\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
if
v
is a circle vertex.

(c)

If neither of the above cases holds then we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{1}{4}
if
v
is a square vertex which is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
if
v
is a square vertex which is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{1}{4}\log_{n}(m)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{8}
if
v
is a circle vertex which is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, and we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}\log_{n}(m)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)k}{4}
if
v
is a circle vertex which is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

In all of these cases, we set
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},S}(v)=0
for all
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
is not
\alpha
or
\alpha^{\prime}
and
v\in V(\alpha^{\prime\prime})
.

The following proposition follows from a direct inspection of
Definition

7.34
.

Proposition 7.35
.

For all
[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
, letting
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v]):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum_{v\in V(\alpha):v\in[v]}{\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)}}
, we have that
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])=0
if
[v]\in S
,
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])=\frac{1}{2}
if
[v]\notin S
and
[v]
is a square vertex which is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])=1
if
[v]\notin S
and
[v]
is a square vertex which is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])=\frac{\log_{n}(m)}{2}
if
[v]\notin S
and
[v]
is a circle vertex which is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, and
\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])=\log_{n}(m)
if
[v]\notin S
and
[v]
is a circle vertex which is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

We now consider the edge factors assigned to each vertex.

Definition 7.36

(Assigned factors)
.

For each vertex
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and each
v\in V(\alpha)
, we define
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)
so that when we split the factor for each edge
e
based on its direction (if any) using the scheme in Definition
7.14
, the product of the edge factors assigned to
v
from the edges in
E(\alpha)
is
n^{-{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)}
.

Lemma 7.37
.

For each vertex
[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
, we have
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{S}([v])\geq\mathrm{target}_{S}([v])+\mathrm{slack}_{S}([v])
, where
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{S}([v]):=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}\sum\limits_{v\in[v]}{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)
and
\mathrm{slack}([v]):=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}\sum\limits_{v\in[v]}\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma using the same ideas we used to prove Lemma
7.15
. The main modification is that for each shape
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
, we treat the set of vertices
v\in V(\alpha)
such that
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
as in the “separator”
S_{\alpha}
for
\alpha
, defined as below.

Definition 7.38

(
S_{\alpha}
)
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
, we define
S_{\alpha}
to be the set of vertices
v\in V(\alpha)
such that at least one of the following holds:

1.

[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivally in both an earlier and a later shape in
\mathcal{P}
.

2.

v
is the left anchor vertex of some chosen path
P
and
v
is not left-connected.

3.

v
is the right anchor vertex of some chosen path
P
and
v
is left connected.

Remark 7.39
.

While every chosen path for
\alpha
will have at least one vertex in
S_{\alpha}
,
S_{\alpha}
may not be a vertex separator of
\alpha
.

We now make the following observations, which can be shown similarly to the proof of Lemma
7.15
:
For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and each
v\in V(\alpha)
,
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)
. Moreover,

1.

If
v
is a circle vertex and
v\notin S_{\alpha}
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

If
v
is a square vertex and
v\in S_{\alpha}
then
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
.

If
v
is a circle vertex, the only ways that we can have
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)<\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)
are as follows:

1.

v
is a left anchor vertex for some chosen path
P
for
\alpha
,
v
is not left-connected, and
v
does not appear non-trivially in an earlier shape. In this case, letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be the last shape where
v
appears non-trivially,
v\notin S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(v)+\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

v
is a right anchor vertex for some chosen path
P
for
\alpha
,
v
is left-connected, and
v
does not appear non-trivially in a later shape. In this case, letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be the first shape where
v
appears non-trivially,
v\notin S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(v)+\frac{k\varepsilon}{8}
.

If
v
is a square vertex, the only ways that we can have
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha,S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)<\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)+\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)
are as follows:

1.

v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
,
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
\alpha
is the first shape in which
v
appears non-trivially, and
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
. In this case, letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be the last shape in which
v
appears non-trivially, we must have that
v\in U_{\alpha^{\prime}}\setminus V_{\alpha^{\prime}}
,
v
is the left anchor vertex of a chosen path
P
for
\alpha^{\prime}
, and
v
is not left-connected as otherwise we would have had that
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{4}
. This implies that
v\in S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(v)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
.

2.

v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
,
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
\alpha
is the last shape in which
v
appears non-trivially, and
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{2}
. In this case, letting
\alpha^{\prime}
be the first shape in which
v
appears non-trivially, we must have that
v\in V_{\alpha^{\prime}}\setminus U_{\alpha^{\prime}}
,
v
is the right anchor vertex of a chosen path
P
for
\alpha^{\prime}
, and
v
is left-connected as otherwise we would have had that
\mathrm{target}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{1}{4}
. This implies that
v\in S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
so
{\mathrm{assigned}}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)-\mathrm{target}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)\geq\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(v)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
.

Summing up over shapes
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and vertices in the class
[v]
, we get the conclusion of
Lemma

7.37
.
∎

Putting everything together, we get the following which completes the proof of
Lemma

7.29
.

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})=\prod_{[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}{n^{\mathrm{target}([v])-{\mathrm{assigned}}([v])}}\leq n^{-\sum_{[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}{\mathrm{slack}([v])}}=n^{-\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}.\qed

7.4.2
Slack for configurations which are not well-behaved

We now lower bound the slack for configurations which are not well-behaved.

Proposition 7.40
.

If
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
,
v\in V(\alpha)
, and
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
, then one of the following must hold:

1.

v
is a circle vertex and either
[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivially in both an earlier and a later shape.

2.

v
is a square vertex,
v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
, and
\deg_{\alpha}(v)=0
.

3.

v
is a square vertex,
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
,
\deg_{\alpha}(v)=1
,
[v]\notin S
,
v
does not appear in any later shape, and either
[v]\in U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}
or
[v]
is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

4.

v
is a square vertex,
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
,
\deg_{\alpha}(v)=1
,
[v]\notin S
,
v
does not appear in any earlier shape, and either
[v]\in V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}
or
[v]
is isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

If none of these conditions hold, then
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

Proof.

We make the following observations. Given a shape
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and a vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
,

1.

If
v
is a circle vertex then by statement 1 of Definition
7.25
, we only have that
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
if
[v]\in S
or
v
appears non-trivially in both an earlier and a later shape. Otherwise,
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{k\varepsilon}{16}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

If
v
is a square vertex and
v\notin U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)=\frac{(\deg_{\alpha}(v)-2)}{4}+\frac{{\varepsilon}\deg_{\alpha}(v)}{8}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
.

3.

If
v
is a square vertex and
v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)=\frac{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}{4}
so we can only have
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
if
\deg_{\alpha}(v)=0
. If
\deg_{\alpha}(v)>0
then
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\frac{1}{4}>\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

4.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
or
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
then
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v):=\frac{\deg_{\alpha}(v)-1}{4}
so we can only have
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
if
\deg_{\alpha}(v)=1
. If
\deg_{\alpha}(v)>1
then
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

5.

If
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
then by statements 2a, 2b, and 2d of Definition
7.25
, if
[v]\in S
,
v
appears in a later shape, or
v
appears non-trivially in an earlier shape and
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
then
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

6.

If
v\in V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha}
then by statements 2a, 2c, and 2e of Definition
7.25
, if
[v]\in S
,
v
appears in an earlier shape, or
v
appears non-trivially in a later shape and
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
then
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

Putting these observations together, the only cases where
\mathrm{slack}_{\alpha,S}(v)=0
are the cases stated in Proposition
7.40
.
∎

Corollary 7.41
.

If the intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
is not well-behaved, then
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{P}
is well-behaved if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
are all disjoint unions of simple spiders.

2.

\mathcal{P}
has no non-trivial intersections between square vertices.

3.

Whenever there is a square vertex
v
which is not in
U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\cup V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}
, there is an intersection between the two neighbors of
v
which results in the two edges incident to
v
combining into a double edge which vanishes.

We show that if any of these conditions are not satisfied, then
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
:

1.

If there is a shape
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
which is not a disjoint union of simple spiders then there is a square vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
such that
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

2.

If all shapes
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
are disjoint unions of simple spiders and there is a non-trivial intersection between square vertices then there must be a shape
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}
and a square vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
such that
v\in U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha}
and
v
appears in a later shape. By statement 2b of Definition
7.25
,
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
.

3.

If the first two conditions are satisfied then each square vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
such that
v\notin U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\cup V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}
, letting
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
be the first and last shapes that
v
appears in,
v
will be adjacent to a circle vertex
u\in V(\alpha)
and a circle vertex
u^{\prime}\in V(\alpha^{\prime})
. For all shapes
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
between
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
,
v\in U_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\cap V_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}
and
v
is not incident to any edges of
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
. If there is no intersection between
u
and
u^{\prime}
(i.e.,
u
and
u^{\prime}
are not equivalent) then
[v]
is not isolated in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
so by statements 2d and 2e of Definition
7.25
,
\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha^{\prime},S}(v)=\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{extra}}_{\alpha,S}(v)=\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
. ∎

Lemma 7.42
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is an intersection configuration which is not well-behaved such that
|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\leq t
and
|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\leq t
where
t\geq 1
, then
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\max{\{\frac{\varepsilon}{8},\frac{\varepsilon}{12}(\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-12{t}^{2})\}}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is not well-behaved,
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
by
Corollary

7.41
. To show that
\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}(\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-12{t}^{2})
, we observe:

1.

\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)}}\geq\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\frac{\varepsilon}{8}(\deg_{\alpha}(v)-1_{\alpha\in(U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha})\cup(V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha})})}}
.

2.

\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{1_{\alpha\in(U_{\alpha}\setminus V_{\alpha})\cup(V_{\alpha}\setminus U_{\alpha})}}}\leq 2t(2t+1)
.

3.

\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}(|V(\alpha)|+w(E(\alpha)))\leq\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}\left(|U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}|+\frac{3}{2}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}}\right)
, where in the second inequality we used
\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}\deg_{\alpha}(v)=2w(E(\alpha))
and
w(E(\alpha))\geq\frac{1}{2}|V(\alpha)\backslash(U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha})|
. Thus,
\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}}\geq\frac{2}{3}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-\frac{2}{3}t(2t+1)\geq\frac{2}{3}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-2t^{2}
.

Putting these observations together,

\mathrm{slack}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\geq\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}}{\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\mathrm{slack}^{\mathrm{default}}_{\alpha}(v)}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}(\frac{2}{3}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-8t^{2})=\frac{\varepsilon}{12}(\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-12t^{2}).\qed

7.5
Main Error Analysis Results for Intersection and Product Configurations

Combining
Lemma

7.29
and
Lemma

7.42
, we get the following main result of
Section

7
:

Theorem 7.43

(Main result for error analysis for intersection configurations)
.

For any intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
, we have the following dichotomy.

1.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a well-behaved configuration,
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})=1
;

2.

If
\mathcal{P}
is not a well-behaved configuration,

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\leq n^{-\max\big\{\frac{\varepsilon}{8},\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\left(\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-12D_{\mathrm{side}}^{2}\right)\big\}}

where
D_{\mathrm{side}}:=\max\{\left|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\right|,\left|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\right|\}
.

Proof.

Item

2
follows from
Lemma

7.29
and
Lemma

7.42
.
We show
Item

1
below.

Assume
\mathcal{P}
is a well-behaved configuration.
For convenience, we denote it by
\mathcal{P}=(\gamma_{-j},\ldots,\gamma_{j})
where
j\geq 1
.
We first prove two properties about
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
:

(1)

{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
is an SSD shape plus isolated square vertices.

(2)

The (unique) minimum weight separator of
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
consists of
U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\cap V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}
plus all circle vertices.

Property
(1)
follows directly from conditions
1
,
2
and
3
of well-behaved configurations. For property
(2)
, having shown that
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
is an SSD shape plus isolated squares, it suffices to show that every circle vertex
[v]\in V({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
has left- and right- degrees both at least
\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil
in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
, i.e.,
[v]
is good. For this, consider the first shape
\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{P}
in which some circle vertex
v_{1}
of class
[v]
appears. We claim that
j^{\prime}\leq 0
. For otherwise,
\gamma_{j^{\prime}}
is a (proper) right SSD shape so the right degree of
v_{1}
is positive, then by the intersection-separating condition
4
(c) and condition
2
of well-behaved configurations, the circle vertex
v_{1}
must be equivalent to a
v^{\prime}_{1}
in one of
\gamma_{-(j^{\prime}-1)},\ldots,\gamma_{j^{\prime}-1}
, which is a contradiction to the assumption on
j^{\prime}
. Therefore, the left degree of
[v]
in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
is at least
\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil
. The right degree of
[v]
in
{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}
can be lower bounded by
\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil
similarly by considering the last shape in
\mathcal{P}
in which some circle vertex of class
[v]
appears. Property
(2)
follows.

From properties
(1)
and
(2)
, we have
\log_{n}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot A_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}})=-\frac{w(E(\mathcal{P}))}{2}+|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|+\frac{|U_{\gamma_{j}}\backslash V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}|+|V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}\backslash U_{\gamma_{j}}|}{2}
. By conditions
1
and
2
of well-behaved configurations, all vertices in
(U_{\gamma_{j}}\backslash V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}})\cup(V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}\backslash U_{\gamma_{j}})
have degree 1 in
\mathcal{P}
, and all vertices in
U_{\gamma_{j}}\cap V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}
has degree 0 in
\mathcal{P}
. By condition
3
of well-behaved configurations, all other square vertices in
\mathcal{P}
, i.e., those in
V_{\square}(\mathcal{P})\backslash(U_{\gamma_{j}}\cup V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}})
, are singleton classes, have degree
2
in
\mathcal{P}
, and 1-1 correspond to the isolated vertices
I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})
. Thus,
w(E(\mathcal{P}))=\sum_{v\in V_{\square(\mathcal{P})}}\deg_{\mathcal{P}}(v)=2|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|+|U_{\gamma_{j}}\backslash V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}|+|V_{\gamma^{\prime\top}_{j}}\backslash U_{\gamma_{j}}|
.
This means
\log_{n}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\cdot A_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}})=0
.
∎

In the applications of
Theorem

7.43
in
Section

5
and
Section

6
, the length
j
of the configuration is at most
2D
, and the side size
D_{\mathrm{side}}
is at most
D
.

7.5.1
Analyzing Product Configurations

We now describe how to modify our techniques to analyze product configurations and give analogous results for product configurations.

We can think of product configurations
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
as intersection configurations of length
1
where
\tau
is the identity,
\gamma_{1}=\alpha_{1}
, and
{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}^{T}=\alpha_{2}
. Keeping the same definitions for default slack and extra slack, we have

Lemma 7.44

(Key lemma for error analysis for product configurations)
.

For all SSD
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
which are good, for all product configurations
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
,
slack(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\geq 0
and

\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\leq n^{-\mathrm{slack}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})}.

Proof sketch.

This lemma can be shown using the same techniques used to prove
Lemma

7.29
, though a few modifications are needed.

1.

Different choice of paths: Instead of considering paths from
U_{\gamma_{1}}
to the union of the set of intersected vertices and
V_{\gamma_{1}}
, we do the following. Since
\alpha_{1}
is good and is an SSD, for each circle vertex
v\in V_{\bigcirc}(\alpha_{1})
, we can take
\lceil{\frac{k}{2}}\rceil
square-vertex disjoint paths from
U_{\alpha_{1}}
to
V_{\alpha_{1}}
passing through
v
.

2.

Modified scheme for assigning directions: For each edge which is not on a path, instead of not giving it a direction, we have it point to its square endpoint.

As noted in the next point, we can afford to do this because each circle vertex in a shape only needs
\frac{k}{2}
edge factors and the
\lceil{\frac{k}{2}}\rceil
paths through the vertex are sufficient to guarantee this.

3.

Modified definition of being satisfied: Instead of having circle vertices be satisfied if the number of edges pointing towards the vertex is greater than or equal to the number of edges pointing away from the vertex, we say that circle vertices are satisfied in a shape if at least
\lceil{\frac{k}{2}}\rceil
edges in the shape point to the vertex.

With this modification,
Lemma

7.33
holds and can be proved using the same logic as before.

∎

Our main error analysis result for product configurations is as follows.

Theorem 7.45
.

For all SSD
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are good,
|U_{\alpha_{1}}|\leq D
,
|V_{\alpha_{1}}|=|U_{\alpha_{2}}|\leq D
, and
|V_{\alpha_{2}}|\leq D
, for all product configurations
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
, we have the following dichotomy.

1.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a well-behaved configuration,
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})=1
;

2.

If
\mathcal{P}
is not a well-behaved configuration then
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})\leq n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}
.

Proof sketch.

This can be proved by the same logic we used to prove
Proposition

7.40
,
Corollary

7.41
, and
Theorem

7.45
.
∎

7.6
Useful Bounds

Before giving our estimates for the error terms, we show a few useful bounds.

7.6.1
Bound on the contribution from a given shape

Lemma 7.46
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq 2k\log n
. For all shapes
\alpha
such that
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)\leq 3D_{trunc}
,

\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}.

(88)

Proof.

By
Theorem

3.15
,

\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq(2k\cdot\mathrm{total}(\alpha)^{2}\log n)^{{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}\cdot{(C_{U})}^{\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}\cdot n^{-\left(w(E(\alpha))-w(V(\alpha))+w(S)\right)/2}

where
S
is a minimum weight separator of
\alpha
and
C
is an absolute constant. Since
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)\leq 3D_{trunc}
and
D_{trunc}\geq 2k\log n
,
(2k\cdot\mathrm{total}(\alpha)^{2}\log n)^{{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}\leq(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}
.

We estimate the exponent over
n
using the following edge weight assignment. Let each edge in
\alpha
have a factor of
1
which we split between its endpoints as follows (an edge with label
t>1
is viewed as
t
parallel edges for this argument). For each edge
e\in E(\alpha)
, we assign
(1-\varepsilon/2)/2
to the circle and
(1+\varepsilon/2)/2
to the square. With this assignment, each circle vertex receives a factor
(1-\varepsilon/2)/2
times its degree, and each square vertex receives a factor
(1+\varepsilon/2)/2
times its degree. We now make the following observations:

1.

Since each circle vertex has degree at least
k
and each square vertex outside of
U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
has degree at least
2
, each vertex not in
U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
receives a factor of at least its weight plus
\varepsilon/4
times its degree.

2.

Each vertex
v\in U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
receives a factor of
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
times its degree.

Using these observations,

w(E(\alpha))-w(V(\alpha))\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}-|U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}-2D.

(89)

To lower bound
w(E(\alpha))-w(V(\alpha))
in terms of
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)
, we make the following further observations:

1.

\deg(v)\geq 2
for each
v\in V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha})
, so
\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}\geq|V(\alpha)|-|U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}|\geq|V(\alpha)|-2D
.

2.

\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}=2w(E(\alpha))
.

Putting these observations together,

\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\in V(\alpha)}{\deg_{\alpha}(v)}\geq w(E(\alpha))+|V(\alpha)|-2D=\mathrm{total}(\alpha)-2D.

By this and (
89
),

w(E(\alpha))-w(V(\alpha))+w(S)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)-4D

Thus, for each shape
\alpha
such that
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)\leq 3D_{trunc}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}n^{-\left(w(E(\alpha))-w(V(\alpha))+w(S)\right)/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}.\qed

7.6.2
Bounds on the number of shapes and configurations

Lemma 7.47

(Number of configurations)
.

For all
a,b,D,D_{trunc}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, there are at most

(48{D}^{2}D_{trunc})^{a}(36{D}D_{trunc}^{2})

configurations
\mathcal{P}
such that
|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\leq D
,
|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\leq D
,
|V(\mathcal{P})|=a
,
w(E(\mathcal{P}))=b
, and
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 3D_{trunc}
.

Note that
Lemma

7.47
applies to both intersection configurations and product configurations.

Proof.

We first observe that there are at most
2D-1
rounds of intersections for
\mathcal{P}
. To see this, observe that if
\tau_{P_{1}}
is the result after the first round of intersection,
|U_{\tau_{P_{1}}}|\geq 1
and
|V_{\tau_{P_{1}}}|\geq 1
so
|U_{\tau_{P_{1}}}|+|V_{\tau_{P_{1}}}|\geq 2
. Each additional round of intersections increases the size of
U\cup V
by at least one so there can be at most
2D-2
additional rounds of intersection.

We can specify
\mathcal{P}
by describing the vertices and edges of
\mathcal{P}
and how they are connected. For each vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
, we specify the following data:

1.

Is
v
is a circle vertex or a square vertex? There are
2
choices for this.

2.

What are the first and last shapes that
v
appears in (before taking intersections into account)? There are at most
\binom{4D-1}{2}+4D-1\leq 8D^{2}
choices for this.

3.

Is there an intersection between
v
and a previous vertex? If so, what is the most recent vertex we’ve seen which has an intersection with
v
? There are at most
3D_{trunc}
choices for this.

For each edge
e\in E(\mathcal{P})
, we specify the following data:

1.

Which shape
e
appears in.

2.

The two endpoints of
e
. There are at most
\binom{3D_{trunc}}{2}
choices for this.

3.

Whether
e
remains after the edge
e
and edges parallel to it are linearized (if the result is an edge with label
l
then we choose
l
of these edges to remain).

Thus, the total number of choices for each
e\in E(\mathcal{P})
is at most
36DD_{trunc}^{2}
. Note that for this argument, we treat an edge with label
l
as
l
separate edges.

Putting these bounds together, the total number of choices is at most
(48{D}^{2}D_{trunc})^{a}(36{D}D_{trunc}^{2})
.
∎

Remark 7.48
.

Note that for each
v\notin U_{\gamma_{j}}\cap V_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{j}}^{\top}}
, the parity labels for
v
(if any) are determined by the parity of
\deg_{\alpha}(v)
for the shapes
\alpha
such that
v\in V(\alpha)
(before intersections are taken into account). If
v\in U_{\gamma_{j}}\cap V_{{\gamma^{\prime}_{j}}^{\top}}
then there are two choices for the parity labels of
v
. This factor can be absorbed into specifying the first and last shapes
v
appears in. If
j=0
then we take
\gamma_{j}={\gamma^{\prime}_{j}}^{\top}=\tau
.

Corollary 7.49
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq 100D
. For all
s\in[3D_{trunc}]
, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
configurations
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})=s
. As a special case, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
proper shapes
\alpha
such that
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)=s
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})=|V(\mathcal{P})|+w(E(\mathcal{P}))
and
D_{trunc}\geq 100D
, by
Lemma

7.47
, there are at most

\sum_{a=0}^{s}{(48{D}^{2}D_{trunc})^{a}(36{D}D_{trunc}^{2})^{s-a}}=\left(48{D}^{2}D_{trunc}+36{D}D_{trunc}^{2}\right)^{s}\leq(D_{trunc})^{3s}

configurations with total size
s
.
∎

7.6.3
Bounds on
\lVert L_{\leq l}\rVert
and
\lVert L-L_{\leq l}\rVert

Corollary 7.50
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq 2k\log n
and
C_{U}(3D_{trunc})^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}
. Then for all
l\leq D_{trunc}
,

\lVert L_{\leq l}\rVert\leq n^{2D}.

Note that this bound holds for
L
since
L=L_{\leq D_{trunc}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
L_{\leq l}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma\text{ is a left shape},\mathrm{total}(\sigma)\leq l}{\eta_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}M_{\sigma}}
. By
Corollary

7.49
, for all
s\leq 3D_{trunc}
, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
many proper shapes of total size
s
. Using this,
Lemma

7.46
and the assumptions on parameters, we have:

\lVert L_{\leq l}\rVert\leq 1+\sum_{s=1}^{D_{trunc}}{(D_{trunc})^{3s}}{C_{U}^{s}}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}s}\leq n^{2D}.\qed

Corollary 7.51
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{\frac{100}{\varepsilon}D,\ 2k\log n\}
and
C_{U}(3D_{trunc})^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}\leq n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}
.Then for all
l\in[3D_{trunc}]
,

\lVert L-L_{\leq l}\rVert\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}l}.

Proof.

Recall that
L-L_{\leq l}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma\text{ is a left shape, }l<\mathrm{total}(\sigma)\leq D_{trunc}}{\eta_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}M_{\sigma}}
. By
Corollary

7.49
, for all
s\leq 3D_{trunc}
, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
many proper shapes of total size
s
.
Using this,
Lemma

7.46
, the fact that
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
and the assumptions on parameters, we have that

\lVert L-L_{\leq l}\rVert\leq\sum_{s=l+1}^{D_{trunc}}{(D_{trunc})^{3s}}C_{U}^{s}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}s}\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}l}.\qed

7.6.4
Bounds on the contributions from intersection and product configurations

Lemma 7.52

(Combinatorial factor
N(\mathcal{P})
in configurations)
.

For every intersection or product configuration
\mathcal{P}
,

N(\mathcal{P})\leq(2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P}))^{2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

(90)

Proof.

Fix a ribbon
R
whose shape is the shape resulting from
\mathcal{P}
(
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
if
\mathcal{P}
is an intersection configuration and
\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}
if
\mathcal{P}
is a product configuration). To bound
N(\mathcal{P})
, we only need to specify:

•

A set of isolated vertices in
V(\mathcal{P})
. This costs at most
|V(\mathcal{P})|
many bits.

•

For each non-isolated vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{P})
, which vertex in
V(R)
it goes to. This costs at most
|V(\mathcal{P})|\cdot\log(|V(R)|)
many bits. Notice that this induces a complete specification for each edge
e\in E(\mathcal{P})
as of where it goes in
E(R)
.

•

The coefficient of
R
in the linearization process of the improper shape resulting from
\mathcal{P}
. By
Proposition

3.17
, this can be bounded by
(w\left(E(\mathcal{P})\right)\cdot|V(\mathcal{P})|)^{w\left(E(\mathcal{P})\right)}
in absolute value, where the bound on the exponent is from the fact that there are at most that many steps of expanding multi-edges.

Together, we have
|N(\mathcal{P})|\leq 2^{|V(\mathcal{P})|}|V(R)|^{V(\mathcal{P})}\left(w\left(E(\mathcal{P})\right)\cdot|V(\mathcal{P})|\right)^{w\left(E(\mathcal{P})\right)}\leq(2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P}))^{2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}
.
∎

Lemma 7.53
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq 2k\log n
. For every intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 3D_{trunc}
,

\left|\eta_{\mathcal{P}}\right|N(\mathcal{P})\frac{\lVert M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

(91)

Proof.

By definition
3.38
,

\eta_{\mathcal{P}}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{\gamma_{i}}}\right)\eta_{\tau}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}}\right)\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})}{(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)}.

(92)

We now make the following observations:

1.

We have that
\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{\gamma_{i}}}\right)\eta_{\tau}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j}{\eta_{{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{i}}}\right)\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}
.

2.

To handle the factor
\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})}{(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)}
in (
92
), we use
Corollary

3.19
, which in particular implies that this factor can be absorbed into the norm bound of
Theorem

3.15
. Following similar reasoning as in
Lemma

7.46
,

\displaystyle\frac{\lVert M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})}{\left|(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)\right|}

\displaystyle\leq(2k\cdot\mathrm{total}(\alpha)^{2}\log n)^{{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leq(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

3.

By
Lemma

7.52
,
N(\mathcal{P})\leq(2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P}))^{2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}
.

Multiplying the three bounds, we get (
91
).
∎

We have a similar bound for product configurations.

Lemma 7.54
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq 2k\log n
. For every product configuration
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2D_{trunc}
,

\left|\eta_{\mathcal{P}}\right|N(\mathcal{P})\frac{\lVert M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\leq(4D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

(93)

Proof.

Following the same reasoning we used to prove
Lemma

7.53
, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\eta_{\mathcal{P}}\right|\frac{\lVert M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lVert M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\prod_{\text{multi-edges }e\in E(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})}{\left|(\text{coefficient of }l_{e}\text{ when linearizing }e)\right|}

(94)

\displaystyle\leq(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}

and

N(\mathcal{P})\leq(2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P}))^{2\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

(95)

Multiplying these two bounds, we get (
93
).
∎

7.7
Bounding Error Terms

We now bound all the error terms.

Lemma 7.55

(Truncation error in canonical product)
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{\frac{100}{\varepsilon}D,\ 2k\log n\}
and
C_{U}(3D_{trunc})^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}\leq\frac{1}{3}n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}
. Then

\lVert M-[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\rVert\leq{n}^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{50}D_{trunc}}.

(96)

Proof.

By definition,
M
is
[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}
minus the terms
\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
where
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)>D_{trunc}
. Note that
\mathrm{total}(\alpha)\leq 3D_{trunc}
for all such terms. By
Lemma

7.46
, for each such
\alpha
,

\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\mathrm{total}(\alpha)}.

By
Corollary

7.49
, for all
s\leq 3D_{trunc}
, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
many proper shapes having total size
s
. Combining these bounds and using the assumptions on the parameters, similarly to the proof of
Corollary

7.51
, we have:

\lVert M-[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}\rVert\leq\sum_{s=D_{trunc}+1}^{3D_{trunc}}{C_{U}^{s}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s+3s}n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}s}}\leq n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{50}D_{trunc}}.\qed

Lemma 7.56

(Truncation error in factorization)
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{50{D}^{2},\frac{500}{\varepsilon}D,2k\log n\}
and
C_{U}(6D_{trunc})^{8{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{24}}
. Then

\lVert[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}\rVert\leq 2n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}D_{trunc}}.

(97)

Proof.

Recall that

\displaystyle[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2D}{(-1)^{j}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\textrm{ $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})\left(L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}-{L}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}L^{\top}\right)}}

where

L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}-{L}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}L^{\top}=(L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}-L)M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}+LM_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}(L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}-L^{\top}).

For these terms, we have that

1.

By
Lemma

7.53
and
Theorem

7.43
, noting that if
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\geq 12D^{2}
then
\mathcal{P}
cannot be well-behaved,

\displaystyle\lVert\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\rVert

\displaystyle=\left(|\eta_{\mathcal{P}}|N(\mathcal{P})\frac{\lVert M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\right)\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})

(98)

\displaystyle\leq C_{U}^{\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\cdot\min\{0,\ -\left(\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})-12D^{2}\right)\}}.

Moreover, by
Lemma

7.47
, for all
s\in[3D_{trunc}]
, there are at most
(D_{trunc})^{3s}
intersection configurations
\mathcal{P}
of total size
s
.

2.

By
Corollary

7.50
,
\lVert L\rVert\leq n^{2D}
and
\lVert L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert\leq n^{2D}
.

3.

By
Corollary

7.51
,
\lVert L_{\leq l_{\mathcal{P}}}-L\rVert\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}l_{\mathcal{P}}}\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}l_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\lVert L^{\top}_{\leq r_{\mathcal{P}}}-L^{\top}\rVert\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}r_{\mathcal{P}}}\leq n^{2D-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}r_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

4.

By the definitions of
l_{\mathcal{P}}
and
r_{\mathcal{P}}
,
l_{\mathcal{P}}+\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\geq D_{trunc}
and
r_{\mathcal{P}}+\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\geq D_{trunc}
.

Combining these bounds,

\displaystyle[L,Q_{0},L^{\top}]_{\mathrm{can}}-LQL^{\top}

\displaystyle\leq 2n^{4D}\sum_{s=1}^{3D_{trunc}}{(D_{trunc})^{3s}}C_{U}^{s}(6D_{trunc})^{5{{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}s}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}(D_{trunc}-s)-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}(s-12D^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq 2n^{4D+{\varepsilon}{D}^{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}D_{trunc}}\leq 2n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}D_{trunc}}.\qed

We now analyze the errors in our approximation of
Q
. For this, the following bound is useful.

Lemma 7.57
.

Assume
8D_{trunc}^{2}\leq n
. Given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and a well-behaved
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
such that
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 3D_{trunc}
,

\lVert{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}-\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}\rVert\leq\frac{10D_{trunc}^{2}}{n}\lVert{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert,

where
I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})
is the number of isolated vertices in
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
. Similarly, for all good SSDs
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
which having both left and right indices at most
D
, and all product configurations
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
,

\lVert{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}-\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}\frac{M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\alpha_{\mathcal{P}})|!}\rVert\leq\frac{10D_{trunc}^{2}}{n}\lVert{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert.

Proof.

We only prove the first statement as the second statement can be proved in the same way. By
Proposition

4.4
, given
\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}
and a well-behaved
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{j},\ldots,\gamma_{1},\tau,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\top}_{j}}
, letting
t:=|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|
,

{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}={\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\left(\frac{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{t-1}{(n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|-i)}}{n^{t}}\right)\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{t!}

which implies

\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}(\tau_{\mathcal{P}})|!}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}=\left(\frac{n^{t}}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{t-1}{(n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|-i)}}-1\right){\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}.

Since
|V_{\square}(\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|+t\leq\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 3D_{trunc}
, it holds
\prod\limits_{i=0}^{t-1}(\frac{n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|-i}{n})\geq 1-\frac{4D_{trunc}^{2}}{n}
and so

\frac{n^{t}}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{t-1}{n-|V_{\square}(\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}})|-i}}-1\leq(1-\frac{4D_{trunc}^{2}}{n})^{-1}-1\leq\frac{10D_{trunc}^{2}}{n}.\qed

Lemma 7.58

(Approximation of
Q
by well-behaved part)
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{100D,\ 2k\log n\}
and

C_{U}^{18D^{2}}(10D_{trunc})^{200{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D^{2}}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}.

(99)

Then

\lVert Q-[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\rVert<n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}.

(100)

Proof.

Recall that

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=Q_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\text{ $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}}

(101)

\displaystyle[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\\
\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved, $l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$, $r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|$}\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}N(\mathcal{P})\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|!}}

(102)

Then
\lVert Q-[Q]_{{\mathrm{well-behaved}}}\rVert\leq A+B
where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\Big\|{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\\
\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is not well-behaved,}\\
l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|,\ r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}{\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}N(\mathcal{P})M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}}}\Big\|,

(103)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\Big\|{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2D}(-1)^{j}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{length}={j}}:\\
\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved,}\\
l_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|U_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|,\ r_{\mathcal{P}}\geq|V_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}|\end{subarray}}{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\left({\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|!}\right)}}\Big\|.

(104)

Below, we show that
A<\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}
and
B<\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}
, and the lemma follows by adding the two.

To upper bound
A
, we observe that for each
\mathcal{P}
that appears in
A
, denoting
s=\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})
:

1.

s\leq(2D+1)D_{trunc}\leq 3DD_{trunc}
.

2.

\left|\eta_{\mathcal{P}}\right|N(\mathcal{P})\frac{\lVert M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}\rVert}{{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})}\leq{C_{U}^{s}}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}
by
Lemma

7.53
.

3.

\|\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})\|\leq n^{-\max\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{8},\ \frac{\varepsilon}{12}(s-D_{\mathrm{side}}^{2})\right\}}
by
Theorem

7.43
as
\mathcal{P}
is not well-behaved.

Let
s_{0}:=12D^{2}
. Using the above observations and the fact that there are at most
s^{3s}
intersection configurations with total size
s
(
Corollary

7.49
), we have:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\mathcal{P}$ appearing in \eqref{eq:wellbehaved,A}},\\
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq s_{0}\end{subarray}}\lVert\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\rVert+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\mathcal{P}$ appearing in \eqref{eq:wellbehaved,A},}\\
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})>s_{0}\end{subarray}}\lVert\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\rVert

(105)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{s_{0}}s^{3s}C_{U}^{s}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}+\sum\limits_{s=s_{0}+1}^{3DD_{trunc}}s^{3s}{C_{U}^{s}}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}n^{-\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-s_{0}}{12}}

\displaystyle\leq 2{s_{0}}^{3s_{0}}C_{U}^{s_{0}}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s_{0}}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}}+2\left((3{D}D_{trunc})^{3}{C_{U}}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}\right)^{s_{0}+1}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}

where in the last step we used
D<\frac{D_{trunc}}{100}
and
C_{U}^{18D^{2}}(10D_{trunc})^{200{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D^{2}}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}
by (
99
).

To upper bound
B
, given
\mathcal{P}
in (
104
), denote again
s:=\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})
. By
Lemma

7.57
and the three observations above (where we note that
{A_{\mathrm{norm}}}(\mathcal{P})=1
by
Theorem

7.43
),

\left\lVert{\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\left({\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|!}\right)}\right\rVert\leq\frac{10D_{trunc}^{2}}{n}C_{U}^{s}(6D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s}.

(106)

Note that in every well-behaved intersection configuration
\mathcal{P}
appearing in
B
, each SSD shape is good and so has total size at most
6D
, so
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq(2D+1)\cdot 6D\leq 18D^{2}
. Summing over all
\mathcal{P}
in (
104
) and using
Corollary

7.49
, we have:

B\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{s=1}^{18D^{2}}s^{3s}\Big)C_{U}^{18D^{2}}(10D_{trunc})^{92{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D^{2}}n^{-1}<C_{U}^{18D^{2}}(10D_{trunc})^{200{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D^{2}}n^{-1}<\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}

(107)

where we used
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
and
D_{trunc}\geq 100D
for the second to last step and (
99
) for the last step.
∎

Lemma 7.59

(Approximation of good SSD products)
.

Assume
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{100D,\ 2k\log n\}
. If
\alpha,\beta
are linear combinations of (scaled) good SSD shapes having both left and right indices of size at most
D
, and
\left|{\alpha}\right|_{\infty}\leq A
and
\left|{\beta}\right|_{\infty}\leq B
for some
A,B>0
, then

\lVert\alpha\cdot\beta-\alpha*_{\mathrm{wb}}\beta\rVert\leq ABD_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}.

(108)

Similarly, if
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{SA}_{D}
are good and
\left|{\alpha}\right|_{\infty}\leq A
and
\left|{\beta}\right|_{\infty}\leq B
, then

\lVert{(\alpha\cdot\beta)}_{\text{SS}}-\alpha\star\beta\rVert\leq ABD_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}.

(109)

Proof.

We only prove (
108
) as the proof of (
109
) is similar. Denote by
c_{\alpha}(\cdot)
,
c_{\beta}(\cdot)
the coefficient of SSD shapes in
\alpha
,
\beta
respectively. Then

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\beta-\alpha*_{\mathrm{wb}}\beta

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:\ \\
\gamma_{1}\in\alpha,\ \gamma_{2}\in\beta\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha}(\gamma_{1})\cdot c_{\beta}(\gamma_{2})\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}:\ \\
\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is not well-behaved}\end{subarray}}\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}

(110)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:\ \\
\gamma_{1}\in\alpha,\ \gamma_{2}\in\beta\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha}(\gamma_{1})\cdot c_{\beta}(\gamma_{2})\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}:\ \\
\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is well-behaved}\end{subarray}}{\left(\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\left({\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|!}\right)\right)}.

Since all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are good, by
Theorem

7.45
,
Lemma

7.54
, and
Lemma

7.57
we have that

1.

For each
\mathcal{P}
in the above sum which is not well-behaved,
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2D_{trunc}
and

\lVert\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}\rVert\leq(4D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}.

2.

For each
\mathcal{P}
in the above sum which is well-behaved,
\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})\leq 2D_{trunc}
and

\lVert\eta_{\mathcal{P}}N(\mathcal{P})\left({\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}}M_{{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}}}-{\lambda_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}\frac{M_{\tau_{\mathcal{P},{\mathrm{\hskip 1.0ptred}}}}}{|I_{s}({\tau_{\mathcal{P}}})|!}\right)\rVert\leq\frac{10{D_{trunc}}^{2}}{n}(4D_{trunc})^{5{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\mathrm{total}(\mathcal{P})}.

The number of pairs
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is at most
5^{4D}
as there can be at most
5^{2D}
many SSD shapes in
\alpha,\beta
(
Claim

6.8
). By
Corollary

7.49
, for each pair
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
, The size of the set
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}
is at most
(2D)^{6D}
. Plugging these bounds and
|c_{\alpha}(\gamma_{1})\cdot c_{\beta}(\gamma_{2})|\leq AB
into (
110
), we have that

\lVert\alpha\cdot\beta-\alpha*_{\mathrm{wb}}\beta\rVert\leq 2AB(5^{4D})(2D)^{6D}(4D_{trunc})^{10{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}}<ABD_{trunc}^{20{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}D}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{12}},

where we used that
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
and
D_{trunc}\geq 100D
.
∎

8
Putting Things Together: Proof of Theorem
2.12

The last ingredient for proving
Theorem

2.12
is that
LL^{\top}
is not too close to being singular.

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose
D_{trunc}\geq\log n
and
C_{U}(3D)^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}
. Then

LL^{\top}\succ(9D_{trunc}^{2}n)^{-D}\mathrm{Id}.

(111)

Proof.

Define a scaled matrix
L^{\prime}:=\mathrm{diag}\left(n^{-|I|/2}\right)_{I\in\binom{[n]}{\leq D}}\cdot L\cdot\mathrm{diag}\left(n^{|I|/2}\right)_{I\in\binom{[n]}{\leq D}}
. By definition,

L^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}L^{\prime}_{0,0}&&&\\
L^{\prime}_{1,0}&L^{\prime}_{1,1}&&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&&\\
&\dots&&L^{\prime}_{D,D}\end{pmatrix}

which is a block-lower-triangular matrix where
L^{\prime}_{i,j}
is supported on
\binom{[n]}{i}\times\binom{[n]}{j}
. Since
L^{\prime}
contains only left shapes,
L_{i,i}=L^{\prime}_{i,i}=\mathrm{Id}_{\binom{[n]}{i}}
for all
i
.

We first upper bound
\lVert L_{i,j}\rVert
and
\lVert L^{\prime}_{i,j}\rVert
for all
0\leq j\leq i\leq D
. For each shape
\alpha
in
L_{i,j}
of total size
s
, since
\alpha
is left and proper, we can use the same edge-factor assignment as for middle shapes (
Definition

7.14
) and
Theorem

3.15
to get:

\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq C_{U}^{s^{\prime}}(3D_{trunc})^{{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\cdot s^{\prime}}n^{\frac{(i-j)}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\cdot w(E(\alpha))},

(112)

where we have used
D_{trunc}\geq\log n
and have taken
s^{\prime}:=\mathrm{total}(\alpha)-|\{v\in U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}:\deg(v)=0\}|
, which we call the
essential size
of
\alpha
. Since
\alpha
has no degree
0
vertices outside of
U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
, it holds that
s^{\prime}\leq w(E(\alpha))+2w(E(\alpha))=3w(E(\alpha))
. This and (
112
) imply that
\lVert\eta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}\rVert\leq C_{U}^{s^{\prime}}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s^{\prime}}n^{\frac{(i-j)}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}s^{\prime}}
. As there are at most
(s^{\prime})^{3s^{\prime}}
shapes in
L
having essential size
s^{\prime}
and total size
s
(by the same proof of
Lemma

7.47
), we have:

\lVert L_{i,j}\rVert\leq\sum\limits_{s=1}^{D_{trunc}}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s}s^{3s^{\prime}}C_{U}^{s^{\prime}}(3D_{trunc})^{3{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}s^{\prime}}n^{\frac{i-j}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{24}s^{\prime}}<n^{\frac{i-j}{2}}\sum\limits_{s=1}^{D_{trunc}}2=2D_{trunc}n^{\frac{i-j}{2}},

(113)

where we used
{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}\geq 1
and
C_{U}(3D)^{6{C_{\mathrm{univ}}}}<n^{\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}
.
Recall that
L^{\prime}_{i,j}=n^{-\frac{i-j}{2}}L_{i,j}
, so by (
113
),

\lVert L^{\prime}_{i,j}\rVert\leq 2D_{trunc}.

(114)

Next, we upper bound
\lVert(L^{\prime})^{-1}\rVert
and
\lVert L^{-1}\rVert
.
Denote
A_{i}:=L^{\prime}\upharpoonright_{\text{blocks $\{(a,b)\mid a,b\leq i\}$}}
.
Then
\lVert A_{0}^{-1}\rVert=1
,
A_{i+1}\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}A_{i}&0\\
L^{\prime}_{i+1,i}&\mathrm{Id}\end{pmatrix}
,

A^{-1}_{i+1}\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}A^{-1}_{i}&0\\
-L^{\prime}_{i+1,i}A_{i}^{-1}&\mathrm{Id}\end{pmatrix}
,
and by (
114
) we have

\lVert A^{-1}_{i+1}\rVert\!\leq\!\lVert A^{-1}_{i}\rVert\cdot(1+\lVert L^{\prime}_{i+1,i}\rVert)+1\!<2D_{trunc}\lVert A^{-1}_{i}\rVert+1.

(115)

By (
115
) and induction, we have the bound
\lVert(L^{\prime})^{-1}\rVert=\lVert A_{D}^{-1}\rVert<(3D_{trunc})^{D}
. We now recall that
L^{-1}=\mathrm{diag}\left(n^{\frac{|I|}{2}}\right)_{I\in\binom{[n]}{\leq D}}(L^{\prime})^{-1}\mathrm{diag}\left(n^{-\frac{|I|}{2}}\right)_{I\in\binom{[n]}{\leq D}}
, so
\lVert L^{-1}\rVert\leq n^{\frac{D}{2}}\cdot\lVert(L^{\prime})^{-1}\rVert\cdot 1<n^{\frac{D}{2}}(3D_{trunc})^{D}
. This means
\sigma_{\min}(L)>n^{-\frac{D}{2}}(3D_{trunc})^{-D}
and thus
LL^{\top}\succ(9D_{trunc}^{2}n)^{-D}\mathrm{Id}
.
∎

We can now prove
Theorem

2.12
. For convenience, we restate
Theorem

2.12
below.

See
2.12

Proof.

For any
\delta>0
, if
n
is large enough then with probability
>1-\delta
, the norm bounds in
Theorem

3.15
,
Corollary

3.19
hold for all relevant shapes. Below we assume this is the case.

By assumption (
8
), we have:

•

M=LQL^{\top}+E
where
\|E\|\leq 3n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{100}D_{trunc}}
by
Lemma

7.55
and
Lemma

7.56
. Since
D_{trunc}\geq\frac{500}{\varepsilon}D
, it follows that
\lVert E\rVert\leq 3n^{-5D}
.

•

Q\succ\left(n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}-n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{20}}\right)\mathrm{Id}\succ\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}\mathrm{Id}
by
Lemma

6.1
and
Lemma

7.58
. Here, we note that the conditions required in these two lemmas follow from condition (
8
).

•

LQL^{\top}\succ(9D_{trunc}^{2}n)^{-D}\left(\frac{1}{2}n^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}}\right)\mathrm{Id}\succ n^{-1.1D}\mathrm{Id}
by the item above and
Lemma

8.1
, where for the last step we use
9D_{trunc}^{2}<n^{\frac{1}{300}}
from (
8
).

Therefore,
M\succeq LQL^{\top}-\lVert E\rVert\mathrm{Id}\succ\left(n^{-1.1D}-3n^{-5D}\right)\mathrm{Id}\succ n^{-2D}\mathrm{Id}
for all large
n
.
∎

9
Applications to Learning Mixture Models and Robust Statistics

In this section, we show that
Theorem

2.12
implies strong SoS lower bounds for a range of problems in learning theory and algorithmic robust statistics. The high-level idea is as follows. We will construct specific distributions
A
in
Problem

1.1
such that the resulting NGCA instance is a valid instance of the problem being considered, and then we apply the lower bound
Theorem

2.12
.

For
Theorem

2.12
to apply, we need to check that
A
matches
(k-1)
moments with that of a Gaussian (where
k
depends on the application) and to bound the quantities
C_{U}
and
C_{L}
. It will be simpler to bound the measures
U_{A}(t)
and
L_{A}(t)
for all
t
, where we recall that

\displaystyle U_{A}(t)

\displaystyle:=\max\limits_{i\leq t}\left|\E\limits_{A}\ [h_{i}(x)]\right|,

(116)

\displaystyle L_{A}(t)

\displaystyle:=\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(x):\ \deg(p)\leq t\\
\text{and }\int_{\mathcal{N}(0,1)}p^{2}(x)=1\end{subarray}}\E\limits_{A}\ [p^{2}(x)].

(117)

For this reason, we will use the following corollary of
Theorem

2.12
, which follows by a simple inspection of the definitions of
U_{A}(t),L_{A}(t)
and
C_{U},C_{L}
(
Definition

2.11
).

Corollary 9.1

(SoS lower bounds in terms of
U_{A}(t),L_{A}(t)
)
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
n
is sufficiently large then the following holds.

Suppose
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
k\geq 2
, and
A
is a 1-dimensional distribution (where
\varepsilon
,
k
, and
A
may all depend on
n
) such that:

A
matches the first
k-1
moments with
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

(118)

\displaystyle U_{A}(t)\leq\mathrm{poly}(\log n,k,t)^{t}\text{ and }L_{A}(t)\geq\mathrm{poly}(\log n,k,t)^{-t}\text{ for all }t\geq 1.

(119)

Set
D=o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
and let
D_{trunc}
be a suitably large polynomial in
k
and
\log n
such that
D_{trunc}\geq\max\{50{D}^{2},\frac{500}{\varepsilon}D,2k\log n\}
. Then, if we draw
m<n^{(1-\varepsilon)k/2}
many i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n})
, with probability greater than
1-\delta
, the degree-
D
pseudo-calibration moment matrix (
12
) with truncation threshold
D_{trunc}
is positive-definite.

In
Section

9.1
