, we state and prove lemmas helpful for bounding
U_{A}(t)
and
L_{A}(t)
for various distributions
A
. Since
U_{A}(0)=L_{A}(0)=1
, we will focus on
t\geq 1
.
In
Section

9.2
, we apply
Corollary

9.1
to different problems in learning theory and algorithmic robust statistics.

In this section, we denote
h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x):=h_{i}(x)/\sqrt{i!}
where
h_{i}(x)
is the
i
th Probabilist’s Hermite polynomial. We call a function
f(x)

unit-norm
if
\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}[f^{2}(x)]=1
.

9.1
Technical Toolkit

In this section, we state some technical lemmas required to prove bounds on
U_{A}(t)
and
L_{A}(t)
.

The first lemma is a generic lower bound on the
l_{2}
-norm of polynomials under
A
.

Lemma 9.2

(Generic lower bound)
.

Let
A
be a distribution with pdf
q(x)
, and let
g(x)
denote the pdf of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Let
t
be any natural number. Suppose
\mathbb{R}
can be partitioned into two measurable sets
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
such that: (1)
\int_{I_{1}}p^{2}(x)g(x)dx\leq 1-\delta
for every degree
\leq t
unit-norm polynomial
p
, and (2)
\frac{q(x)}{g(x)}\geq\varepsilon
for all
x\in I_{2}
.
Then

\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(x):\ \deg(p)\leq t\\
\|p(x)\|_{\mathcal{N}(0,1),l_{2}}=1\end{subarray}}\ \E_{x\sim A}[p^{2}(x)]\geq\delta\varepsilon.

Proof.

The lemma follows from a simple calculation:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p^{2}(x)q(x)~dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p^{2}(x)g(x)\frac{q(x)}{g(x)}~dx

(120)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{I_{2}}p^{2}(x)g(x)\frac{q(x)}{g(x)}~dx

(121)

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon\int_{I_{2}}p^{2}(x)g(x)~dx

(122)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left(1-\int_{I_{1}}p^{2}(x)\exp(-x^{2}/2)~dx\right)

(123)

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon\delta.\qed

(124)

The following corollary of
Lemma

9.2
, focuses on the lower bound in terms of the likelihood ratio in a large interval around 0.

Corollary 9.3

(Lower bound by density ratio around
0
)
.

Let
A
be a distribution with pdf
q(x)
and
g(x)
be the pdf of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then for any
t\geq 1
,

L_{A}(t)\geq\min_{|x|\leq 10\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}}\left\{\frac{q(x)}{2g(x)}\right\}.

(125)

We also need the following generic upper bound on
U_{A}(t)
, which we will later specialize to specific distributions
A
.

Corollary 9.4

(Generic upper bound on
U_{A}
)
.

Let
A
be a distribution whose pdf outside the region
[-B,B]
is upper bounded by
\exp(-(|x|-C)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}))
, where
B\geq C\geq 0
and
B>1
. Then:

U_{A}(t)\leq(8t)^{t}\cdot(\sigma C^{t}+2B^{2t}+\sigma^{t+1}t^{t/2})\text{ for all }t\geq 1.

The final corollary provide bounds on
U_{A}
and
L_{A}
for
A
which a mixture of a small number of Gaussians with bounded means and variances.

Corollary 9.5

(Gaussian mixture bounds)
.

Suppose
A=\alpha\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})+(1-\alpha)E
, where
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
E
is a distribution whose pdf outside
[-B,B]
is upper bounded by twice that of
\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},(\sigma^{\prime})^{2})
, where
1\leq B
and
|\mu^{\prime}|\leq B
. Then for any
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle U_{A}(t)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(t\cdot(|\mu|+|\mu^{\prime}|+1)\cdot(\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}+1)\cdot B\Big)^{2t},

(126)

\displaystyle L_{A}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\ \frac{\alpha}{2\sigma}\exp\left(-\ \frac{O(t\log(t+1))+\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

(127)

Here, the constants in the
O
notation are independent of
t,\alpha,\mu,\mu^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},B
.

Proofs of the above corollaries are deferred to
Section

C.1
.

9.2
Applications to Other Learning Tasks

In this section, we will use
Corollary

9.1
to show that distinguishing versions of many fundamental problems in learning theory and robust statistics are hard for SoS programs. For each problem we consider, there are known SQ lower bounds (see, e.g.,
[
DK23
]
) and low-degree polynomial testing lower bounds (via the near-equivalence between the two models
[
BBH+21
]
). Our SoS lower bounds strengthen these prior results.

It is worth noting that with the exception of a single application — learning
k
-GMMs, where reduction-based hardness was recently established (assuming subexponential hardness of LWE) —
the prior SQ and low-degree lower bounds were the only known evidence of hardness.

See
Table

1
for a summary of the problems we examine and the guarantees we obtain. We prove lower bounds for the hypothesis testing version of the problems, which are known to be efficiently reducible to the corresponding learning problem.

Specifically, our SoS lower bounds for robust statistics tasks apply under the “TV-corruption” model or the “Huber contamination” model. In the TV-corruption model, the adversary may draw samples from any other distribution
D^{\prime}
of its choice, as long as the distribution satisfies
d_{TV}(D,D^{\prime})\leq\tau
, for some
\tau>0
which is the proportion of contamination.

Definition 9.6

(TV-Corruption Model)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a set of
distributions. We define
\mathcal{B}_{TV}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
to be the following hypothesis testing problem: Given
m
i.i.d. samples
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
drawn from one of the following two distributions, the goal is to determine which one: (a)
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n})
; and (b)
D^{\prime}
such that
d_{TV}(D^{\prime},D)\leq\tau
for
D
drawn uniformly at random from
\mathcal{D}
.

In the Huber contamination model, the adversary cannot delete samples but may add samples of their choice. In the definition below and throughout the remaining section, we use a convex combination of distributions
c_{1}D_{1}+\ldots+c_{r}D_{r}
to denote the distribution where we first draw
x\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
with probability
c_{i}
for
x=i
, and then draw a sample according to
D_{x}
.

Definition 9.7

(Huber Contamination Model)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a family of distributions. We define
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
to be the following hypothesis testing problem: Given
m
i.i.d. samples
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
drawn from one of the following two distributions, determine which one: (a)
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n})
; (b)
D^{\prime}
, which is
(1-\tau)D+\tau B
, where
D
is drawn uniformly at random from
\mathcal{D}
and
B
is an arbitrary distribution possibly dependent on
D
.

We now demonstrate SoS lower bounds for the problems considered in
Table

1
using
Corollary

9.1
. Our list is by no means exhaustive, and there are likely several other problems for which similar lower bounds may be obtained.

9.2.1
Robust Mean Estimation of Bounded Covariance Gausians

The problem of Gaussian Robust Mean Estimation with bounded covariance is the problem of robust mean estimation, where samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
for some
\Sigma\preceq\mathrm{Id}
, and up to a
\tau
fraction of these samples may be arbitrarily corrupted.

In this context, it is information-theoretically possible to estimate
\mu
to an
\ell_{2}
-error of
O(\tau)
using
O(n/\tau^{2})
samples; see, e.g.,
[
HR09
]
. Lower bounds against SQ algorithms suggest that achieving an error
o(\tau^{1/2})
in polynomial time requires
\Omega(n^{2})
samples. Such an SQ lower bound is implicit in
[
DKS19
]
and can be directly deduced from
[
DKK+22a
]
.

We show a degree
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{\log n})

SoS lower bound against a hypothesis testing problem known to be efficiently reducible to Gaussian RME with bounded covariance, defined below:

Problem 9.8

(Hypothesis-Testing-RME with Bounded Covariance)
.

Hypothesis-Testing-RME with bounded covariance is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.7
), where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
has the form
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{D},\Sigma_{D})
such that
\Sigma_{D}\preceq\mathrm{Id}_{n}
and
\lVert\mu_{D}\rVert\geq\Omega(\sqrt{\tau})
for some constant independent of
\tau,n
.

To prove the lower bound for
Problem

9.8
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to an instance of
Problem

1.1
where the distribution in the hidden direction is the following
A_{\text{RME}}
.

Lemma 9.9

(Lemma F.2. from
[
DKS19
]
)
.

For any constant
0<\tau<1/2
and
\mu=\sqrt{\tau/{10001}}
, there is a distribution
A_{\text{RME}}=(1-\tau)\mathcal{N}(\mu,2/3)+\tau\tau_{1}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1})+\tau(1-\tau_{1})\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2})
which matches its first 3 moments with
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, where
|\mu_{1}|,|\mu_{2}|<\frac{2}{\sqrt{\tau}}
,
0.9<\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}<1.1
and
\tau_{1}=\Theta(1)
.

Setting
A=A_{\text{RME}}
in
Problem

1.1
, we get an instance of
Problem

9.8
where the set of distributions is
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}

\displaystyle=(1-\tau)\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times\mathcal{N}(\mu,2/3)_{v}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau(1-\tau_{1})\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1})_{v}+\tau\tau_{1}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2})_{v}

\displaystyle=(1-\tau)\mathcal{N}(\mu v,\mathrm{Id}_{n}-\frac{1}{3}vv^{T})

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau\cdot\left((1-\tau_{1})\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1})_{v}+\tau_{1}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1})_{v^{\perp}}\times\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2})_{v}\right).

In other words, each distribution in
\mathcal{D}
has the form
(1-\tau)\cdot\mathcal{N}(\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{10001}}~v,\mathrm{Id}_{n}\!-\!\frac{1}{3}vv^{T})+\tau\cdot B
for some
B
, thus satisfying the requirement in the model
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
.

Corollary 9.10

(SoS Lower Bound for Bounded Covariance Gaussian Robust Mean Estimation)
.

Fix
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{RME}}
with
k:=4
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{RME}}
.

Proof.

Given that
Lemma

9.9
shows that
A
matches the first 3 moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, our lemma directly follows by applying
Corollary

9.1
after verifying that the hypotheses hold. Since
A
consists of a three-component Gaussian mixture with bounded means and variances, satisfying the prerequisites for
Corollary

9.5
,
U_{A}
and
L_{A}
are appropriately bounded, and the conclusion of
Corollary

9.1
follows.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

A natural modification to the SoS program would be to add indicator variables for whether samples are corrupted. Such indicator variables have been used in several SoS algorithms for robust estimation (see, for e.g.
[
KS17
,
KKK19
,
DKK+22a
]
). While we expect that adding these indicator variables does not give SoS much additional power in solving this problem, this does not technically follow from our lower bound as these indicator variables are not low-degree polynomials in the existing solution variables and input variables (though they are often approximated by such low-degree polynomials).

9.2.2
Robust Mean Estimation for Identity Covariance Gaussians

For the problem of Gaussian robust mean estimation, even if we further assume that the covariance of the clean Gaussian distribution is the identity, SQ lower bounds suggest that it is not possible to efficiently achieve the information-theoretic optimal error
O(\tau)
without using significantly more samples
[
DKS17
]
. We show lower bounds for
Problem

9.12
, a hypothesis testing problem known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of Gaussian robust mean estimation with identity covariance (see, e.g.,
[
DKS17
]
and Lemma 8.5 of
[
DK23
]
).

Problem 9.12

(Hypothesis-Testing-RME with Identity Covariance)
.

Let
\tau>0
and
B=O(\log^{1/2}(1/\tau))
be a parameter. Hypothesis-Testing-RME with identity covariance
\mathcal{B}_{TV}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.6
), where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
is of the form
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{D},\mathrm{Id}_{n})
and
\lVert\mu_{D}\rVert\geq\Omega(\tau\log(1/\tau)^{1/2})/B^{2})
.

To prove a lower bound against
Problem

9.12
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
where the distribution
A
is the following
A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
.

Lemma 9.13

(
A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
in Proof of Proposition 5.2 in
[
DKS17
]
)
.

Let
\tau>0
be smaller than some absolute constant, let
B:=\sqrt{\log(1/\tau)}-\tau>1
,
\xi=c\tau\log^{1/2}(1/\tau)/B^{2}
for a large enough constant
c>0
and let
g(x)
be the density of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then the following holds. Define
A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
to have density function

A(x):=g(x-\xi)+q(x)\mathbf{1}(x\in[-B,B])

where
q(x)
is the degree-
k
polynomial uniquely determined by
\int_{-B}^{B}q(x)~dx=0
and
\int_{-B}^{B}q(x)x^{i}~dx=\int_{-B}^{B}(g(x)-g(x-\xi))x^{i}~dx
for

1\leq i\leq\lfloor\sqrt{B}\rfloor
. Then:

1.

A
matches
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
on the first
k
moments.

2.

TV(A,\mathcal{N}(\xi,1))\leq O(\xi~k^{2}/\log(1/\xi))
.

3.

For all
x\in[-B,B]
, it holds that
|q(x)|\leq\tau B^{-3/4}
and
g(x-\xi)+q(x)\geq\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2\pi}}-3\xi\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\xi}}
.

Setting
A:=A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
in
Problem

1.1
, we get an instance of
Problem

9.12
with the set of distributions
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}(\xi v,\mathrm{Id}_{n})\mid v\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies the total variance condition in
Problem

9.12
as shown in
[
DKS17
]
. We have the following bounds on
U_{A}(\cdot)
and
L_{A}(\cdot)
, whose proof is deferred to
Section

C.2
.

Lemma 9.14

(Bounds on
U_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}
and
L_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}
)
.

For any
0<\xi<\frac{1}{26}
,

U_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)\leq(32t\log(1/\tau))^{t}\ \ \text{and}\ \ L_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)\geq\xi\cdot\exp\left(-10\xi\sqrt{t\log(t+1)}\right),\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

Since
\tau
and
\xi
are both constants, this yields the following lower bound.

Corollary 9.15

(Gaussian Robust Mean Estimation with Identity Covariance)
.

Fix
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{26})
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{\frac{k+1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
with
k:=\lfloor\sqrt{\log(1/\tau)}\rfloor
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, then the SoS program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}
.

Proof.

The conditions for
Corollary

9.1
to apply are verified by
Lemma

9.13
and
Lemma

9.14
. The conclusion follows.
∎

9.2.3
Mean Estimation with Bounded
t
-Moments

If we relax the assumption in
Problem

9.8
to include subgaussian distributions rather than strictly Gaussian ones then SQ lower bounds suggest that it is not possible to efficiently recover the mean to an error of less than
O(\tau^{1-1/t})
where
t
is the number of moments that are bounded
[
DKK+22a
]
. This can be shown via a lower bound for
Problem

9.16
defined below, which is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of robust mean estimation for subgaussian distributions
[
DKK+22a
]
.

Problem 9.16

(Hypothesis-Testing-RME-Bounded-
t
-Moments)
.

Let
t
be a positive integer and
\tau\in(0,1)
. Hypothesis-Testing-RME-Bounded-
t
-Moments is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{TV}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.6
) where each
D\in\mathcal{D}
satisfies the following: (1) the mean vector
\mu
satisfies
\|\mu\|\geq\Omega(\frac{1}{t}\tau^{1-1/t})
; (2)
D
has subgaussian tails, i.e., for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
1\leq i\leq t
,
\E_{x\sim D}[\left|v^{T}(x-\mu)\right|^{i}]^{1/i}\leq O(\sqrt{i})
. Here, the constants in the
O
and
\Omega
notations are independent of
\tau,t,n
.

Problem

9.16
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of robustly estimating the mean of a distribution with bounded moments (see Section 6 and the discussion therin from
[
DKK+22a
]
).
To prove an SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.16
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with
A:=A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}
defined as below.

Lemma 9.17

(Lemma 6.14 from
[
DKK+22a
]
)
.

Assume
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
(c(k-1))^{-(k-1)}\leq\tau\leq\frac{1}{2}
for some positive constant
c
independent of
k
.
Then there exists a distribution
A
over
\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the following.

1.

A=(1-\tau)Q_{1}+\tau Q_{2}
where
Q_{1}(x)=g(x-\delta)+\frac{1}{1-\tau}p(x)\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(x)
,
Q_{2}(x)=g(x-\delta^{\prime})
, and
p(\cdot)
is a degree
k-1
polynomial (chosen below).

2.

\delta:=\frac{1}{2000(k-1)}\tau^{1-\frac{1}{k-1}}
,
\delta^{\prime}:=-\frac{(1-\tau)}{\tau}\delta
.

3.

p(\cdot)
is a univariate polynomial satisfying

(a)

\int_{-1}^{1}p(x)\,dx=0,

(b)

\int_{-1}^{1}p(x)x\,dx=0,

(c)

\max\limits_{x\in[-1,1]}|p(x)|\leq 0.1
.

4.

A
matches the first
k-1
moments with
N(0,1)
.

5.

For all
i\geq 1
,
\E\limits_{x\sim Q_{1}}[\left|v^{T}(x-\mu)\right|^{i}]^{1/i}\leq O(\sqrt{i})
.

We denote this distribution by
A_{\text{RME-$(k-1)$-Mom}}
.

Setting
A=A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}
in
Problem

1.1
gives us an instance of
Problem

9.16
with the set of distributions
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}({\frac{1}{2000t}\tau^{1-1/t}}\cdot v,\mathrm{Id})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies the conditions required. We have the following bounds on
U_{A}(\cdot)
and
L_{A}(\cdot)
for
A=A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}
, whose proof is deferred to
Section

C.2
.

Lemma 9.18

(Bounds on
U_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}
and
L_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}
)
.

For any
i\geq 1
,

U_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}(i)\leq O(i)^{i},\ \ L_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}(i)\geq\exp\left(-O(\sqrt{i\log(i+1)})\right).

By
Lemma

9.17
,
A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}
matches the first
k-1
moments with that of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Additionally,
Lemma

9.18
verifies the conditions for
Corollary

9.1
to apply. By
Corollary

9.1
, we get the following.

Corollary 9.19

(SoS Lower Bound for Mean Estimation with Bounded
t
-Moments)
.

Fix
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and a positive integer
k\geq 2
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{k(1-\varepsilon)/2}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{RME-$(k-1)$-Mom}}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}
.

9.2.4
Gaussian List-decodable Mean Estimation

In the problem of Gaussian list-decodable mean estimation (LDME), the algorithm is given a set of samples a majority of which consist of arbitrary (and possibly adversarial) outliers, while a
\tau<\frac{1}{2}
fraction are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathrm{Id}_{n})
with an unknown
\mu
. The goal is to return a set of
O(\frac{1}{\tau})
candidates at least one of which is close to the true mean,
\mu
.

Information-theoretically, under mild conditions, it is possible to achieve this goal within error
O(\log\frac{1}{\tau})
using
O_{\tau}(n)
many samples (see, e.g., Section 5.2 of
[
DK23
]
). Algorithmically, however,
[
DKS18
]
proved SQ lower bounds which suggest that it is impossible to efficiently achieve a smaller error than
O(\tau^{-1/k})
using fewer than
n^{\Omega(k)}
samples (see
[
DK23
]
for a different exposition).

We prove an
n^{k/2}
sample lower bound for SoS (
Corollary

9.22
),
for the natural hypothesis testing version of the problem as below.

Problem 9.20

(Hypothesis-Testing-LDME)
.

Given
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and positive integer
k\geq 2
, the hypothesis-testing-LDME is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(1-\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.7
), where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
has the form
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{D},\mathrm{Id}_{n})
for some
\mu_{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose
l_{2}
-norm is at least
\Omega(\tau^{-1/k})
. Here, the constant in the
\Omega
notation is independent of
\tau,k,n
.

Problem

9.20
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of Gaussian LDME
[
DKS18
]
. To prove an SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.20
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with
A:=A_{\text{LDME}}
, defined below.

Lemma 9.21

(
[
DKS18
]
; see also Lemma 8.21 in
[
DK23
]
)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there exists a univariate distribution
A_{\text{LDME}}=\tau\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)+(1-\tau)E
for some distribution
E
and
\mu=10c_{k}\tau^{-1/k}
where
c_{k}
depends only on
k
, such that
A_{\text{LDME}}
matches the first
k
moments with
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Moreover, the pdf of
E
is upper bounded by two times the pdf of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
pointwise.

Setting
A=A_{\text{LDME}}
in
Problem

1.1
gives an instance of
Problem

9.20
with
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}(\mu v,\mathrm{Id}_{n})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies the conditions on
\mathcal{D}
required by the problem definition. We now state and prove our lower bound.

Corollary 9.22

(SoS Lower Bound for Gaussian List-decodable Mean Estimation)
.

Fix
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and positive integer
k\geq 2
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{\frac{k+1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{LDME}}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{LDME}}
.

Proof.

The conditions for
Corollary

9.1
to apply are verified by
Lemma

9.21
and
Corollary

9.5
, where we set
\mu=10c_{k}\tau^{-1/k}
,
\mu^{\prime}=0
,
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=1
and recall that
k
is a fixed constant beforehand. The conclusion follows.
∎

9.2.5
Gaussian Robust Covariance Estimation in Spectral Norm

We now consider the problem of robustly estimating the covariance (RCE) of a Gaussian up to a constant multiplicative factor in spectral norm, given that a
\tau
fraction of the samples may be arbitrarily corrupted.
It is information-theoretically possible to solve this problem of with
O_{\tau}(n)
samples. The SQ lower bound shown in
[
DKS17
]
suggests that any algorithm using
o(n^{2})
samples requires super-polynomial runtime.

Problem 9.23

(Hypothesis-Testing-RCE in Spectral Norm)
.

Let
0<\tau<\frac{1}{2}
.
Hypothesis-Testing-RCE is the problem

\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.7
),
where each
D\in\mathcal{D}
has the form
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{D})
=where either
\Sigma_{D}\prec\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Id}_{n}
or
\Sigma_{D}\succ 2\mathrm{Id}_{n}
.

Problem

9.23
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of Gaussian RCE in spectral norm
[
DKS17
]
.
To prove a SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.23
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with the distribution in the hidden direction set to
A_{\text{COV}}
, defined below. Given
c\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
and
n>1
, let
\tau:=\frac{c}{\log n}
, and
A_{\text{COV}}
is the following three-component mixture.

A_{\text{COV}}:=(1-\tau)\cdot\mathcal{N}(0,1-\frac{4/5}{1-\tau})+\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot\mathcal{N}(\sqrt{4/(5\tau)},1)+\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot\mathcal{N}(-\sqrt{4/(5\tau)},1).

Setting
A=A_{\text{COV}}
in
Problem

1.1
provides an instance of
Problem

9.23
with the set of distributions
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}-\frac{4/5}{1-\tau}~vv^{T})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
. Here, note that
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}-\frac{4/5}{1-\tau}~vv^{T})
is derived from the first component of
A_{\text{COV}}
, and the condition on
\mathcal{D}
is satisfied. We now state and prove our lower bound.

Corollary 9.24

(SoS Lower Bound for Robust Covariance Estimation, Multiplicative)
.

Fix
c\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{COV}}
with
k:=4
and
\tau:=\frac{c}{\log n}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot show that there is no hidden direction
v
such that the input has distribution
A_{\text{COV}}
in this direction.

Proof.

By a direct calculation, we see that
A_{\text{COV}}
matches the first 3 moments with
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
(cf. Theorem 6.1 in
[
DKS17
]
). By applying
Corollary

9.5
to
A_{\text{COV}}
where we set
\alpha:=1-\tau\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
\mu:=0
,
\sigma:=1
,
\mu^{\prime}:=\sqrt{4/(5\tau)}=O(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{c}})
,
\sigma^{\prime}:=1
,
B:=1
, we get

U_{A_{\text{COV}}}(t)\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{c}\right)^{t}\ \ \text{and}\ \ L_{A}(t)\geq(t+1)^{-O(t)},\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

Thus,
A_{\text{COV}}
satisfies the conditions in
Corollary

9.1
, from which the conclusion follows.
∎

9.2.6
Gaussian Robust Covariance Estimation to Small Additive Error

Here, we want to estimate the Gaussian covariance up to an additive error, given the guarantee that the covariance matrix
\Sigma
is bounded by
\mathrm{Id}\preceq\Sigma\preceq 2\mathrm{Id}
. This is information theoretically possible
up to an error of
O(\tau)
using
O_{\tau}(n)
samples. However, it is unclear how to achieve this with an efficient algorithm. SQ lower bounds in
[
DKS17
]
suggest that polynomial time algorithms cannot recover the unknown covariance to an error smaller than
o(\tau\log\frac{1}{\tau})
without using
n^{\Omega\left((\log\frac{1}{\tau})^{c}\right)}
samples, for some positive constant
c
.

We demonstrate an SoS lower bound against the following hypothesis testing version of the problem of Gaussian RCE, where the goal is to recover the mean up to an error of
o(\tau\log\frac{1}{\tau})
.

Problem 9.25

(Hypothesis-Testing-RCE-Additive
\tau\log\frac{1}{\tau}
)
.

Given
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, Hypothesis-Testing-RCE-Additive is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{TV}(\tau,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.6
), where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
has the form
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
,
\|\Sigma-\mathrm{Id}\|_{2}\geq\Omega(\tau\log\frac{1}{\tau})
, and the constant in the
\Omega
notation is independent of
\tau,n
.

Problem

9.25
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of Gaussian RCE in spectral norm
[
DKS17
]
. To prove an SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.25
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with the distribution in the hidden direction set to
A_{\text{COV-close}}
, defined in
Lemma

9.26
.

Lemma 9.26

(
A_{\text{COV-close}}
from Proposition 5.13. in
[
DKS17
]
)
.

Suppose
0<\delta<\frac{1}{3}
,
1\ll k\ll(\log\frac{1}{\delta})^{\frac{1}{4}}
and
k\ll B\ll(\log\frac{1}{\delta})^{1/2}
, where
Y\ll X
means that
X>cY
for some absolute positive constant
c
. Let
g(x)
denote the density of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Define

A_{\text{COV-close}}:=\frac{g(x/(1-\delta))}{1-\delta}-q(x)\mathbf{1}(x\in[-B,B]),

where
q(x)
is the unique degree-
k
polynomial for
A_{\text{COV-close}}
to match the first
k
moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then,
A_{\text{COV-close}}
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

A_{\text{COV-close}}
and
N(0,1)
agree on the first
k
moments.

(ii)

d_{\text{TV}}\Big(A_{\text{COV-close}},\ N\left(0,(1-\delta)^{2}\right)\Big)\leq O(\delta k^{4}/\log(1/\delta))\leq O(\delta)
.

(iii)

|q(x)|\leq 10\delta k^{4}B^{-3}
in the interval
[-B,B]
.

Setting
A=A_{\text{COV-close}}
in
Problem

1.1
gives us an instance of
Problem

9.25
with the set of distributions
\mathcal{D}:=\{\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}-(1-(1-\delta)^{2})vv^{T})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies the conditions in
Problem

9.25
with respect to the parameter
\tau
where
\delta=2\tau(\log\frac{1}{\tau})/k^{4}
. We now state bounds on
U_{A}(\cdot)
and
L_{A}(\cdot)
, whose proof is deferred to
Section

C.2
.

Lemma 9.27

(Bounds on
U_{A_{\text{COV-close}}}
and
L_{A_{\text{COV-close}}}
)
.

Assume
0<\delta<\frac{1}{4}
and let the absolute constants be sufficiently large in the construction of
A_{\text{COV-close}}
in
Lemma

9.26
. Then:

U_{A_{\text{COV-close}}}(t)\leq O(t^{2}\log\frac{1}{\delta})^{t}\ \ \text{and}\ \ L_{A_{\text{COV-close}}}(t)\geq\exp\left(-O(\delta t\log(t+1)+\delta\log\frac{1}{\delta})\right),\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

An application of
Corollary

9.1
now gives us the following.

Corollary 9.28

(SoS Lower Bound for Covariance Estimation, Additive)
.

Fix
\tau\in(0,\frac{1}{20})
and a positive integer
k\geq 2
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o\left(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}}\right)
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{\frac{k+1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{COV-close}}
with
\delta:=2\tau(\log\frac{1}{\tau})/k^{4}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{COV-close}}
.

9.2.7
Learning Mixtures of
k
Gaussians

We now discuss the problem of learning a mixture of
k
Gaussians (
k
-GMM): given samples drawn from an unknown mixture of
k
Gaussians, the goal is to recover a mixture that is close in total variation to the original. This problem can be information-theoretically solved in
\mathrm{poly}(k,n)
samples. The SQ lower bound in
[
DKS17
]
suggests that any algorithm that takes less than
n^{O(k)}
samples runs in time
2^{\Omega(n^{c})}
for some constant
c
. Building on the SQ-hard instances,
[
GVV22
]
(see also
[
BRST21
]
) gave a reduction-based cryptographic lower bound assuming sub-exponential hardness of LWE, which shows that if
k=\log(n)
and the number of samples is
\mathrm{poly}(n)
then the runtime has to be quasi-polynomial in
n
.

Here we prove an unconditional information-computation tradeoff for SoS algorithms, which is somewhat stronger compared to the cryptographic lower bound mentioned above. Specifically, we show an SoS lower bound against a hypothesis testing version of the problem of
k
-GMM, defined in
Problem

9.29
. In particular, setting
k=\log(n)
or even
k=\omega(1)
, our lower bound suggests that both the runtime and the sample complexity are quasi-polynomial in
n
.

Problem 9.29

(Hypothesis-Testing-
k
-GMM)
.

Let
0<\gamma<1
. Hypothesis-Testing-
k
-GMM is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(0,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

9.7
), where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
is a mixture of
k
Gaussians such that each pair of the Gaussians are
1-\gamma
apart in total variation and
d_{TV}(D,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}))\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Problem

9.29
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of learning a
k
-GMM
[
DKS17
]
. To prove an SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.29
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with the distribution in the hidden direction set to the “parallel pancakes” distribution
A_{\text{GMM}}
, introduced in
[
DKS17
]
,
defined below:

A_{\text{GMM}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\sqrt{1-\delta}~\mu_{i},\delta).

Here,
\mu_{i}
s and
w_{i}
s are from the Gaussian quadrature, i.e.,
\mu_{i}=\sqrt{2}x_{i}
for
x_{1}<\cdots<x_{k}
zeros of the
k
th Probabilist’s Hermite polynomial and
w_{i}=\frac{k!}{k^{2}He_{i-1}(x_{i})^{2}}
, and
\delta=\Theta((k^{2}\log^{2}(k+1/\gamma))^{-1})
.

Setting
A=A_{\text{GMM}}
in
Problem

1.1
gives an instance of
Problem

9.29
with the set of distributions
\mathcal{D}:=\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(v\mu_{i},\mathrm{Id}_{n}-(1-\delta)vv^{T})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies the conditions on
\mathcal{D}
required by the problem definition (see
[
DKS17
]
). We now state and prove our lower bound.

Corollary 9.30

(SoS Lower Bound for learning GMMs)
.

Fix
\gamma>0
and a positive integer
k\geq 2
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{k(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{GMM}}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{GMM}}
.

Proof.

[
DKS17
, Proposition 4.2]

shows that
A
matches the first
2k-1
moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
|\mu_{i}|\leq\sqrt{k}
. What remains to be shown is that
U_{A}
and
L_{A}
are appropriately bounded for these distributions. Since
\delta<1
and
\gamma
are constants,
Corollary

9.5
verifies the conditions for
Corollary

9.1
, an application of which gives the conclusion.
∎

9.2.8
Learning Mixtures of Two Separated Gaussians With Common Covariance

Here we study the task of learning
2
-GMMs with an unknown common covariance whose components are separated. This is a classical special case of the GMM learning problem: a simple (computationally efficient) spectral algorithm succeeds using
O(n^{2})
samples. On the other hand,
O(n)
samples suffice information-theoretically.
[
DDW21
]
gave evidence that this gap is inherent by establishing an
\Omega(n^{2})
sample lower bound for low-degree tests. Moreover, by leveraging the SoS lower bound technology of
[
GJJ+20
]
,
[
DDW21
]
also showed an SoS lower bound of
\Omega(n^{3/2})
on the sample size.

It turns out that the hard family of instances of
[
DDW21
]
is an instance of NGCA where the univariate distribution
A
is discrete. While such instances (with discrete
A
) can be solved efficiently via lattice-basis reduction with
O(n)
samples
[
DK22b
,
ZSWB22
]
, one can appropriately add “noise” to the instance so that the lower bound still applies while LLL-type algorithms fail. We construct such a modified instance and establish an SoS lower bound against a hypothesis testing version of the problem (defined in
Problem

9.31
), suggesting more than
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
samples are required.

Problem 9.31

(Hypothesis-Testing-2-GMM (Common Covariance))
.

Let
\delta>0
. Hypothesis-Testing-2-GMM with a common covariance is the problem
\mathcal{B}_{\text{huber}}(0,\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n}),\mathcal{D})
where every
D\in\mathcal{D}
is of the form
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-\mu_{D},\Sigma_{D})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{D},\Sigma_{D})
, where
0\preceq\Sigma_{D}
and
\mu_{D}^{T}\Sigma_{D}^{-1}\mu_{D}>1/\delta^{2}
.

Problem

9.31
is known to be efficiently reducible to the problem of estimating a
2
-GMM with unknown common covariance (see Lemma 8.5 of
[
DK23
]
). To prove an SoS lower bound against
Problem

9.31
, we will apply
Corollary

9.1
to the instance of
Problem

1.1
with the distribution in the hidden direction set to
A_{\text{MIX}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-\mu,\delta^{2}\mu^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(\mu,\delta^{2}\mu^{2})
, where
\delta,\mu>0
and
\delta\mu\neq 1
.

This gives us an instance of
Problem

9.31
with

\mathcal{D}:=\{(1/2)\mathcal{N}(-\mu v,\mathrm{Id}_{n}+(\delta^{2}\mu^{2}-1)vv^{T})+(1/2)\mathcal{N}(\mu v,\mathrm{Id}_{n}+(\delta^{2}\mu^{2}-1)vv^{T})\mid v\in\{\pm 1/\sqrt{n}\}^{n}\}\;.

This satisfies the conditions on
\mathcal{D}
required by the problem definition, since
(1)
0\preceq\mathrm{Id}_{n}+(\delta^{2}\mu^{2}-1)vv^{T}
and (2)
\mu^{2}(v^{T}(\mathrm{Id}_{n}+(\delta^{2}\mu^{2}-1)vv^{T})^{-1}v)=\mu^{2}\|v\|^{2}(1+\delta^{2}\mu^{2}-1)^{-1}=1/\delta^{2}
.

We now state and prove our lower bound.

Corollary 9.32

(SoS Lower Bound for
Problem

9.31
)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and let
\mu=1/\sqrt{1+\delta^{2}}
. For any
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)\in(0,1)
, as the dimension
n
increases, the SoS program described in
Section

2.2
with degree
o(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon\log n}{\log\log n}})
cannot solve
Problem

1.1
given fewer than
n^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
samples, where
A:=A_{\text{MIX}}
.

In particular, if the samples are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id})
, the program cannot rule out the existence of a hidden direction
v
along which the input distribution is
A_{\text{MIX}}
.

Proof.

A_{\text{MIX}}
matches the first 3 moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, which can be verified by noting that the variance is
1
and the odd moments are
0
. Also,
A_{\text{MIX}}
is a mixture of
2
Gaussians with means and variances bounded by a constant so
Corollary

9.5
applies. This verifies the conditions for
Corollary

9.1
to apply, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 9.33
.

A natural question is whether our SoS lower bound for approximate parallel pancakes can be used to derive an SoS lower bound for the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick problem. We give a sketch of how this can be done in
Appendix

D
.

Appendix

Appendix A
Pseudo-Calibration Calculation

In this section we compute the pseudo-calibration when the planted vector
v
is drawn uniformly at random from
\{\pm{1\over\sqrt{n}}\}^{n}
. We let the planted distribution
\mathrm{pl}
be as follows: first choose
v\sim\{\pm{1\over\sqrt{n}}\}^{n}
uniformly at random, then choose i.i.d. samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}

x_{i}=\big((x_{i})_{v^{\perp}},\ (x_{i})_{v})\sim N(0,\mathrm{Id}_{n-1}\big)_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}

where
A
is the one-dimensional distribution of interest in direction
v
.

For
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\in(\mathbb{N}^{n})^{m}
and
I\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, recall that the pseudo-calibration is then given by

\forall I\in\binom{[n]}{\leq D}\quad\widetilde{E}(\mathrm{v}^{I}):=\sum\limits_{a\in(\mathbb{N}^{n})^{m}:\ \mathrm{total}^{I}(a)\leq D_{trunc}}\ \E_{(x,v)\sim\mathcal{D}_{pl}}\left[v^{I}{h_{a}\over\sqrt{a!}}\right]\cdot{h_{a}\over\sqrt{a!}},

(128)

where
\mathrm{total}^{I}(a):=\lVert a\rVert_{1}+\left|I\cup\{i\in[n]:\ (\exists u\in[m])a_{u}(i)>0\}\right|+{(1-\varepsilon)k\over 2}\cdot\left|\{u\in[m]:\ (\exists i\in[n])a_{u}(i)>0\}\right|
. Note that
\widetilde{E}(\mathrm{v}^{I})
is a function on the inputs
(x_{1},...,x_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{m}
.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
I\subseteq[n]
, the pseudo-calibration value is:

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(\mathrm{v}^{I})=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in(\mathbb{N}^{n})^{m}:\ \mathrm{total}^{I}(a)\leq D_{trunc},\vskip 2.0pt\\
\text{and }(\forall i\in[n])\ I(i)+\sum_{u}a_{u}(i)\text{ is even}\end{subarray}}n^{-{|I|+|a|\over 2}}\prod\limits_{u=1}^{m}\E_{A}[h_{|a_{u}|}]{h_{a_{u}}\over a_{u}!}

(129)

Proof.

We calculate

\mathop{E}_{(x,v)\sim\mathcal{D}_{pl}}[v^{I}h_{a}]=\E_{v}\left[v^{I}\cdot\E_{y\sim(N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v})^{m}}[h_{a}(y)]\right]
in (
128
) fixing
a\in(\mathbb{N}^{n})^{m}
. By independence,
\E_{y\sim(N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v})^{m}}[h_{a}(y)]=\prod\limits_{u=1}^{m}\E_{y\sim N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}}[h_{a_{u}}(y)]
.

We claim that
\E_{y\sim N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}}[h_{\beta}(y)]=\E_{y\sim A}[h_{|\beta|}(y)]\cdot v^{\beta}
. To see this, consider the generating function for
n
-dimensional Hermite polynomials:
\sum\limits_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}h_{\beta}(y){t^{\beta}\over\beta!}=\exp\left(\langle y,t\rangle-{\|t\|^{2}\over 2}\right)
,
\forall y,t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Fixing a
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, write
y=y_{v^{\perp}}+y_{v}
,
t=t_{v^{\perp}}+t_{v}
, then

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}h_{\beta}(y){t^{\beta}\over\beta!}=\exp\left(\langle y_{v^{\perp}},t_{v^{\perp}}\rangle-{\|t_{v^{\perp}}\|^{2}\over 2}\right)\cdot\exp\left(\langle y_{v},t_{v}\rangle-{\|t_{v}\|^{2}\over 2}\right).

(130)

Taking an expectation with respect to
y
drawn from
N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}
, the left hand side above equals
\sum\limits_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\left(\E_{y\sim N_{v^{\perp}}\times A_{v}}[h_{\beta}(y)]\right){t^{\beta}\over\beta!}
, and the right hand side is given by,

\underbrace{\E_{y_{v^{\perp}}\sim N_{v^{\perp}}}\left[\exp\left(\langle y_{v^{\perp}},t_{v^{\perp}}\rangle-{\|t_{v^{\perp}}\|^{2}\over 2}\right)\right]}_{\text{constant 1}}\E_{y_{v}\sim A}\left[{\exp\left(\langle y_{v},t_{v}\rangle-{\|t_{v}\|^{2}\over 2}\right)}\right]=\sum\limits_{k\geq 0}\E_{y\sim A}[h_{k}(y_{v})]{t_{v}^{k}\over k!}\;,

where
y_{v}=\langle v,y\rangle
,
t_{v}=\langle v,t\rangle
. Taking
{\partial\over\partial t^{\beta}}\mid_{t=0}
on both sides gives the claim.

The above claim implies

\mathop{E}_{(x,v)\sim\mathcal{D}_{pl}}[v^{I}h_{a}]=\E_{v}\left[v^{I+\sum\limits_{u}a_{u}}\right]\prod\limits_{u=1}^{m}\E_{A}[h_{|a_{u}|}]=1_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall i\in[n]\left(\sum\limits_{u}a_{u}(i)+I(i)\text{ even}\right)\end{subarray}}\cdot n^{-{|I|+|a|\over 2}}\prod\limits_{u=1}^{m}\E_{A}[h_{|a_{u}|}].

(131)

Plugging this to (
128
) we get (
129
), where recall that
a!
means
\prod\limits_{u=1}^{m}a_{u}!
.
∎

Appendix B
Omitted Proofs from
Section

5

We prove the map
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
constructed in
Definition

5.8
is a representation of
\mathrm{SA}_{D}
; in fact, it is an isomorphism.

Lemma B.1
.

For any fixed
D
,
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}:\ \mathrm{SA}_{D}\to\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathrm{SA}_{D})
is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism.

Proof.

We show that
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
is a homomorphism and is injective. Then by the dimension counting under
Definition

5.5
,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{SA}_{D})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{D+1}i^{2}
which is the same as
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\left(M_{\mathbb{R}}(1)\oplus\dots\oplus M_{\mathbb{R}}(D)\right)
, showing that
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
is an isomorphism.

\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
is a homomorphism.

Fix any
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)
and
S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
, we need to show:

\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\star S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)\right)=\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{2},k_{3};v\right).

(132)

Here, recall
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},u,v)
satisfies the conditions

0\leq k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq D,\ u\leq\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\},\ v\leq\min\{k_{2},k_{3}\}.

(133)

By the definition of
\star
,
S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\star S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)
is a sum of simple spiders each having left-, right-set size
k_{1}
,
k_{3}
, so the LHS of (
132
) is supported on block
B(k_{1},k_{3})
by definition of
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
. The RHS of (
132
) is a product of two matrices supported on
B(k_{1},k_{2})
and
B(k_{2},k_{3})
, so it is also supported on block
B(k_{1},k_{3})
. Moreover both sides are diagonal from bottom-right up on this block, so we can use
LHS(a)
[
RHS(a)
] to denote the
(a,a+k_{3}-k_{1})
-entry in this block for the LHS [RHS] of (
132
), where the parameter
a
ranges in
[0,k_{1}]
.

For the LHS of (
132
), from the definition of
\star
-product (
23
) and linearity of
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(S(k_{1}-u,k_{2}-u;u)\star S(k_{2}-v,k_{3}-v;v)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max\{0,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{3};i\right)

so

LHS(a)=\sum_{i=\max\{0,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{3};i\right).

(134)

To expand this expression, note that if
a<k_{1}-u
then
a<k_{1}-i
for all
i\leq u
, meaning that row
a
is 0 for all
\rho\left(k_{1},k_{3};i\right)
in (
134
). If
a\geq k_{1}-u
, then for row
a
to be nonzero in
\rho\left(k_{1},k_{3};i\right)
we need
a\geq k_{1}-i
, or equivalently,
i\geq k_{1}-a
(
\geq 0
). So we have

\displaystyle LHS(a)=\sum_{i=\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{1}-i}{k_{1}-u}\binom{k_{3}-i}{k_{3}-v}/(k_{2}+i-u-v)!\cdot\binom{a}{k_{1}-i}/(k_{3}-i)!

\displaystyle={a!\over(k_{1}-u)!(k_{3}-v)!}\sum_{i=\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}{1\over(u-i)!(v-i)!(i+k_{2}-u-v)!(a+i-k_{1})!}.

(135)

Now we look at the RHS of (
132
),
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{2},k_{3};v\right)
. Again, if
a<k_{1}-u
then row
a
is zero in
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}\left(k_{1},k_{2};u\right)
and hence zero in this product. Otherwise,
a\geq k_{1}-u
, in which case by the definition of
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
(
29
) we have:

RHS(a)={\binom{a}{k_{1}-u}\over(k_{2}-u)!}\cdot{\binom{a+k_{2}-k_{1}}{k_{2}-v}\over(k_{3}-v)!}.

(136)

Our task is to show that (
135
) and (
136
) are equal. First, let’s look at the range of the index in summation (
135
). If
\min\{u,v\}<\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}
then (
135
) is 0, while at the same time we have
v<k_{1}-a
(this is because
u,v\geq u+v-k_{2}
always holds by (
133
) and we already assumed
a\geq k_{1}-u
), which implies
\binom{a+k_{2}-k_{1}}{k_{2}-v}=0
and so (
136
) is also 0. Therefore, we only need to prove (
135
) equals (
136
) in the case where
u,v,k_{1},k_{2}
additionally satisfies:

\min\{u,v\}\geq\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}.

(137)

Expanding the binomials in (
136
) and canceling out terms with (
135
), we’re tasked to show

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}{1\over(u-i)!(v-i)!(i+k_{2}-u-v)!(a+i-k_{1})!}=

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt{(a+k_{2}-k_{1})!\over(a+u-k_{1})!(k_{2}-v)!(a+v-k_{1})!(k_{2}-u)!}.

Rearranging terms, this is equivalent to showing

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{2}-v}{u-i}\binom{a+v-k_{1}}{i+k_{2}-u-v}=

\displaystyle\binom{a+k_{2}-k_{1}}{k_{2}-u}.

(138)

To see (
138
), consider choosing a size
(k_{2}-u)
subset from a size
(a+k_{2}-k_{1})
set
Z
. There are
\binom{a+k_{2}-k_{1}}{k_{2}-u}
many ways. Counting it differently, we can first fix a partition
Z=Z_{1}\coprod Z_{2}
with
|Z_{1}|=k_{2}-v
,
|Z_{2}|=a+v-k_{1}
and then choose subsets
Y_{\theta}\subseteq Z_{\theta}
(
\theta=1,2
) such that
|Y_{1}|+|Y_{2}|=k_{2}-u
. For
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
to exist,
|Y_{1}|
must be at least
\max\{(k_{2}-u)-|T_{2}|,0\}=\max\{(k_{1}-a)+k_{2}-u-v,0\}
and at most
\min\{|T_{1}|,k_{2}-u\}=\min\{k_{2}-v,k_{2}-u\}
. So if we let
i:=|U_{1}|-(k_{2}-u-v)
, then
i
ranges from
\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}
to
\min\{u,v\}
, giving the counting
\sum_{i=\max\{k_{1}-a,u+v-k_{2}\}}^{\min\{u,v\}}\binom{k_{2}-v}{u-i}\binom{a+v-k_{1}}{i+k_{2}-u-v}
. Comparing the two counts gives (
138
), and thus (
132
).

Proof that
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
is injective.

Order all simple spiders
\{S(i,j;u)\}
by the lexicographical order on tuples
\{(i+u,j+u,i)\}
. Given a nontrivial linear combination
\sum_{i}c_{i}\cdot S_{i}
where
S_{i}
s are distinct simple spiders and all appearing
c_{i}
s are nonzero, consider the minimum simple spider in it, which we assume is
S_{1}
and has form
S(a,b;u)
. Then the
(a,b)
th entry in block
B(a+u,b+u)
of
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(x)
comes only from
c_{1}\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}(S_{1})
, so it is nonzero. This shows that
\rho_{\mathrm{pre}}
is injective.
∎

Appendix C
Omitted Proofs from
Section

9

C.1
Technical Toolkit

In this section, we establish some technical lemmas required to prove bounds on
U_{A}(t)
and
L_{A}(t)
. We use the convention that
0!=0^{0}=1
. Also, recall that
U_{A}(0)=L_{A}(0)=1
from the definition.

We will need the following upper bound on the normal expectation of
x^{2t}
on a neighborhood around
\infty
.

Fact C.1

(See e.g. Lemma A.4 from
[
KKK19
]
)
.

For any
L\geq 0
and positive integer
t
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-L,L]}x^{2t}\exp\left(-x^{2}/2\right)dx\leq 2\exp\left(-L^{2}/2\right)\left(L^{4t}+(16t)^{t}\right).

We have a pointwise upper bound on
p(x)^{2}
and
h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)
as follows.

Lemma C.2
.

For any
t\geq 0
, we have:

1.

For all
i\leq t
and all
x
,
h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)^{2}\leq 2t!\max\{1,x^{2t}\}
.

2.

If
p(x)
is a degree
t
unit-norm polynomial, then
p^{2}(x)\leq 2(t+1)!\max\{1,x^{2t}\}
for all
x
.

Proof.

Recall that
h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)=\sqrt{i!}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{2^{j}j!(i-2j)!}x^{i-2j}
, so we have:

\displaystyle h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)^{2}

\displaystyle=i!\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{2^{j}j!(i-2j)!}x^{i-2j}\right)^{2}

(139)

\displaystyle\leq i!\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}(2^{-j})^{2}\cdot x^{2(i-2j)}\right)\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}\frac{1}{j!^{2}(i-2j)!^{2}}\right)

(140)

\displaystyle\leq i!\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}2^{-2j}\max\{1,x^{2i}\}

(141)

\displaystyle\leq i!\cdot 2\max\{1,x^{2i}\}

(142)

\displaystyle\leq 2t!\max\{1,x^{2t}\}

(143)

where (
140
) is again by Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\frac{1}{j!(i-2j)!}\leq\lfloor 2/i\rfloor
.
Item

1
follows.

For
Item

2
,
p(x)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{t}c_{i}h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)
where
\sum\limits_{i}c_{i}^{2}=1
, as
\{h^{\text{normal}}_{i}\}
is a
l_{2}
-orthonormal basis. By Cauchy-Schwarz,
p^{2}(x)=\left(\sum\limits_{i}c_{i}h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\leq(\sum\limits_{i}c_{i}^{2})(\sum\limits_{i}{h^{\text{normal}}_{i}}(x)^{2})\leq(t+1)~\max\limits_{0\leq i\leq t}h^{\text{normal}}_{i}(x)^{2}
. The conclusion follows from
Item

1
.
∎

Below, we use
Fact

C.1
,
Lemma

C.2
and
Lemma

9.2
to prove bounds on
L_{A}(t)
and
U_{A}(t)
. The first one is a lower bound on
L_{A}(t)
in terms of the likelihood ratio on a large interval around 0.

See
9.3

Proof.

By
Lemma

C.2
and
Fact

C.1
, for any
L\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\backslash[-L,L]}p^{2}(x)g(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq 2(t+1)!~\int_{\mathbb{R}\backslash[-L,L]}\max\{1,x^{2t}\}g(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq 8t^{t}~\exp(-L^{2}/2)(L^{4t}+(16t)^{t}),

where the last inequality used
(t+1)!\leq 2t^{t}
for all
t\geq 1
. Then we can apply
Lemma

9.2
with
I_{1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid|x|>L\}
and
I_{2}:=\mathbb{R}\backslash I_{1}
, to obtain that for any
L\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim A}[p^{2}(x)]

\displaystyle\geq\min_{|x|\leq L}\left\{\frac{q(x)}{g(x)}\right\}\Big(1-8\exp\left(-L^{2}/2\right)\cdot\left(L^{4t}t^{t}+16^{t}t^{2t}\right)\Big).

(144)

Now set

L:=10\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}.

(145)

Since
L>\sqrt{t}
and
L>1
, we have
L^{4t}t^{t}+16^{t}t^{2t}\leq L^{6t}+16^{t}L^{4t}\leq 17^{t}L^{6t}
, so in (
144
),

\exp\left(-L^{2}/2\right)\cdot\left(L^{4t}t^{t}+16^{t}t^{2t}\right)\leq\exp\big(\!-\!L^{2}/2+(6\ln L+\ln 17)t\big)<\exp(-4),

(146)

where the last inequality follows by plugging in
L=10\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}
.
Since
\exp(-4)<1/16
, plug the bound (
146
) into (
144
) and we get the conclusion.
∎

The next one is an upper bound on
U_{A}(t)
.

See
9.4

Proof.

Recall that
U_{A}(t):=\max\limits_{i\leq t}\left|\E\limits_{A}\ [h_{i}(x)]\right|
. An application of Cauchy-Schwarz implies

|\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)]|\leq\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)^{2}]^{1/2}.

Using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we can continue the estimate by

|\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)]|\leq\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)^{2}\mathbf{1}(x\in[-B,B])]^{1/2}+\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)^{2}\mathbf{1}(x\notin[-B,B])]^{1/2}.

Applying
Lemma

C.2
to
h_{i}(x)=\sqrt{i!}h^{\text{normal}}_{i}
, we further have

\displaystyle|\E_{A}[h_{i}(x)]|

\displaystyle\leq 2t^{t}~\left(\E_{A}[\max\{1,x^{2t}\}\mathbf{1}(x\in[-B,B])]^{1/2}+\E_{A}[\max\{1,x^{2t}\}\mathbf{1}(x\notin[-B,B])]^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2t^{t}~\left(B^{t}+\E_{A}[x^{2t}\mathbf{1}(x\notin[-B,B])]^{1/2}\right)

(147)

where the last step used
B>1
. We now focus on
\E_{A}[x^{2t}\mathbf{1}(x\notin[-B,B])]^{1/2}
:

\displaystyle\E_{A}[x^{2t}\mathbf{1}(x\notin[-B,B])]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{x\geq B}x^{2t}\exp(-(x-C)^{2}/2\sigma^{2})~dx

(Hypothesis)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sigma\int_{\sigma x+C\geq B}(\sigma x+C)^{2t}\exp(-x^{2}/2)~dx

(Change of variables)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(2C)^{2t}+\sigma\cdot(2\sigma)^{2t}\int_{x\geq(B-C)/\sigma}x^{2t}\exp(-x^{2}/2)~dx

(
(a+b)^{2t}\leq 2^{2t-1}(a^{2t}+b^{2t})
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(2C)^{2t}+\sigma\cdot(2\sigma)^{2t}\exp(-(B-C)^{2}/2\sigma^{2})\left((\frac{B-C}{\sigma})^{4t}+(16t)^{t}\right)

(
Fact

C.1
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16^{t}~(\sigma C^{2t}+(B-C)^{4t}/\sigma^{2t-1}+\sigma^{2t+1}t^{t})

(
\exp(-|x|)\leq 1
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16^{t}~(\sigma C^{2t}+B^{4t}+\sigma^{2t+1}t^{t}).

(
\sigma>1
,
B\geq C>0
)

Taking term-wise square roots, then plugging it into (
147
), we get

U_{A}(t)\leq(8t)^{t}\left(\sigma C^{t}+2B^{2t}+\sigma^{t+1}t^{t/2}\right).\qed

Finally, we record the following bounds on
U_{A}(t)
and
L_{A}(t)
, for distributions
A
that is typically a mixture of a small number of Gaussians with bounded means and variances.

See
9.5

Proof.

For
U_{A}(t)
, by the definition of
A
we have
U_{A}(t)\leq U_{\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}(t)+U_{E}(t)
. To each term, we apply
Corollary

9.4
:
for
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma)
, we let
C\leftarrow|\mu|
and
B\leftarrow\max\{1,|\mu|\}
;
for
E
, we let
C\leftarrow|\mu^{\prime}|
,
B\leftarrow B
, and note that its pdf on
\mathbb{R}\backslash[-B,B]
incurs an additional factor of
2
. As a result,

\displaystyle U_{A}(t)

\displaystyle\leq(8t)^{t}\cdot\left(\left(\sigma|\mu|^{t}+2\max\{1,|\mu|\}^{2t}+\sigma^{t+1}t^{t/2}\right)+2\cdot\left(\sigma^{\prime}|\mu^{\prime}|^{t}+2B^{2t}+\sigma^{t+1}t^{t/2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq O\Big(t\cdot(|\mu|+|\mu^{\prime}|+1)\cdot(\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}+1)\cdot B\Big)^{2t},\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

For
L_{A}(t)
, from the assumption we have that
\E\limits_{x\sim A}[p^{2}(x)]\geq\alpha\E\limits_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}[p^{2}(x)]
, so we only need to lower bound the latter. Denote by
q(x)
the pdf of
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
, then for any
L\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\min_{|x|\leq L}\frac{q(x)}{g(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma}\exp\left(-(L+|\mu|)^{2}/2\sigma^{2}+L^{2}/2\right)

(148)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sigma}\exp\left(-(L^{2}+\mu^{2})/\sigma^{2}\right).

We choose
L:=10\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}
in (
148
) and then use
Corollary

9.3
, which gives:

L_{A}(t)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2\sigma}\cdot\exp\left(-\ \frac{O(t\ln(t+1))+\mu^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right),\ \ \forall t\geq 1.\qed

C.2
Bounds on
U_{A}
and
L_{A}
for Specific Distributions

In this section, we prove bounds on
U_{A}
and
L_{A}
for the specific choices of
A
from
Section

9
. We start with
A_{\text{RME}}
.

See
9.14

Proof.

For the upper bound on
U_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)
, we apply
Corollary

9.4
with
C:=\tau
,
B:=\sqrt{\ln(1/\tau)}-\tau
and
\sigma:=1
, which gives that

U_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)\leq(8t)^{t}(B^{t}+2B^{2t}+t^{t/2})\leq(8t^{t})(4B^{2}t)^{t}=(32B^{2}t)^{t}<(32t\ln\frac{1}{\tau})^{t},\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

For the lower bound on
L_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)
where
t\geq 1
, we apply
Corollary

9.3
with
L:=10\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}
. This gives
L_{A_{\text{RME}-\mathrm{Id}}}(t)\geq\min\limits_{|x|\leq L}\frac{A(x)}{2g(x)}
.
We further lower bound
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}
for
x\in[-L,L]
as follows.

1.

If
x\notin[-B,B]
, then
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{g(x-\xi)}{g(x)}=\exp(\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\frac{(x-\xi)^{2}}{2})=\exp(x\xi-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2})
. Since
x\in[-L,L]
, this expression is at least
\exp(-L\xi-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2})\geq\exp\left(-10\tau\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}-1\right)
.

2.

If
x\in[-B,B]
, then
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{g(x-\xi)+q(x)}{g(x)}\geq\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{2\pi}}-3\xi\sqrt{\ln\frac{1}{\xi}}
by property
3
of
q(x)
in
Lemma

9.13
.
Here,
\sqrt{\xi/(2\pi)}\geq 2\xi
if
\xi\leq\frac{1}{8\pi}\approx\frac{1}{25.13}
, so
\xi<\frac{1}{26}\Rightarrow\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}\geq\xi
for all
x\in[-B,B]
.

In both cases, if
\tau<\xi<\frac{1}{26}
then
\min\limits_{|x|\leq L}\frac{A(x)}{2g(x)}\geq\tau\cdot\exp\left(-10\xi\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}\right)
. The conclusion follows.
∎

See
9.18

Proof.

Let
g(x)
be the pdf of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Recall that
A(x)=(1-\tau)g(x-\delta)+\tau g(x-\delta^{\prime})+p(x)\boldsymbol{1}_{[-1,1]}(x)
, where
\tau=O(k-1)^{-(k-1)}\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
\delta=\frac{1}{2000(k-1)}\tau^{1-\frac{1}{k-1}}
and
\delta^{\prime}=-\frac{1-\tau}{\tau}\delta=O(1)
. (Although it will be irrelevant for the proof, we also recall that
k
is a constant fixed beforehand.)

For
U_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}
, we apply
Corollary

9.4
with
B=C=\max\{1,\delta^{\prime}\}=O(1)
,
\sigma=1
. As a result,
U_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}(i)=O(i)^{i}
.

For
L_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}
, by
Corollary

9.3
we have
L_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}\geq\min\limits_{|x|\leq L}\frac{A(x)}{2g(x)}
for
L:=10\sqrt{i\ln(i+1)}\geq 1
. We now lower bound
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}
in the range
[-L,L]
:

1.

If
x\notin[-1,1]
, then
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{(1-\tau)g(x-\delta)+\tau g(x-\delta^{\prime})}{g(x)}=(1-\tau)\exp(x\delta-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2})+\tau\exp(x\delta^{\prime}-\frac{\delta^{\prime 2}}{2})
. Since
x\in[-L,L]
, this is lower bounded by
\exp(-O(L))
.

2.

If
x\in[-1,1]
, then
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{(1-\tau)g(x-\delta)+\tau g(x-\delta^{\prime})+p(x)}{g(x)}\geq(1-\tau)\exp(x\delta-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2})-\frac{0.1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e
. Here,
(1-\tau)\exp(x\delta-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2})\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-2\delta)\geq\exp(-0.001)/2
, so
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}>\frac{1}{3}
.

Together, we have that
L_{A_{\text{RME-$t$-Mom}}}(i)\geq\exp\left(-O(\sqrt{i\ln(i+1)})\right)
.
∎

See
9.27

Proof.

By definition, outside the interval
[-B,B]
it holds
A_{\text{COV-close}}(x)\leq O(1)\exp(-\frac{x^{2}}{2(1-\delta)^{2}})
. So by an application of
Corollary

9.4
and using
1\ll B\ll(\log\frac{1}{\delta})^{1/2}
, we have

U_{A}(t)\leq O\left((8Bt)^{2t}\right)\leq O(t^{2}\ln\frac{1}{\delta})^{t},\ \ \forall t\geq 1.

For the lower bound on
L_{A}(t)
,
Corollary

9.3
implies that
L_{A}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\min_{|x|\leq L}\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}
, where
L:=10\sqrt{t\ln(t+1)}
. We now lower bound
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}
for
x\in[-L,L]
.

1.

If
x\notin[-B,B]
, then the ratio is
\frac{g(x/(1-\delta)}{(1-\delta)g(x)}\geq\exp(\frac{x^{2}}{2}(1-\frac{1}{(1-\delta)^{2}}))=\exp(-O(\delta x^{2}))
. In the range
[-L,L]
this is lower bounded by
\exp\left(-O\big(\delta t\ln(t+1)\big)\right)
.

2.

If
x\in[-B,B]
, then the ratio is
\frac{g(x/(1-\delta))/(1-\delta)-q(x)}{g(x)}
. Recall that
|q(x)|\leq 10\delta k^{4}B^{-3}
for
x\in[-B,B]
and
\delta<1/4
.
By choosing in
k\ll B\ll(\log\frac{1}{\delta})^{1/2}
the absolute constants to be sufficiently large, we have that for
x\in[-B,B]
,
\frac{g(x/(1-\delta))}{(1-\delta)}\geq\frac{1}{(1-\delta)\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(\frac{\log\delta}{2})>20\delta k^{4}B^{-3}
. Consequently,
\frac{A(x)}{g(x)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(x/(1-\delta)}{(1-\delta)g(x)}
for
x\in[-B,B]
, and this is further lower bounded by
\exp(-O(\delta B^{2}))=\exp(-O(\delta\ln\frac{1}{\delta}))
by the same calculus as in the case above.

The lower bound on
L_{A}(t)
then follows by taking the minimum of the two, divided by two.
∎

Appendix D
Sketch for deriving an SoS lower bound for Sherrington-Kirkpatrick from approximate parallel pancakes

A natural question is whether an SoS lower bound for approximate parallel pancakes can be used to derive an SoS lower bound for Sherrington-Kirkpatrick. There are two reasons why this is desirable. The main reason is that even though there is an SoS lower bound, the exact parallel pancakes problem is actually easy. A second reason is that we only have an SoS lower bound for the exact parallel pancakes when
m\leq n^{\frac{3}{2}-\epsilon}
whereas our lower bound for approximate parallel pancakes applies when
m\leq n^{2-\epsilon}
.

While intuitively, the answer is yes, obtaining such a derivation is subtle. In this appendix, we sketch an approach for such a derivation. For this approach, we use the same setup as
[
GJJ+20
]
which in turn was based on the reduction from the Boolean vector in a random subspace problem to Sherrington-Kirkpatrick by
[
MRX20
]
. The setup is as follows.

1.

Observe that the span of the top
d
eigenvectors of
M
is a random
d
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This means that we can represent the span of the top
d
eigenvectors of
M_{SK}
as the rows of an
d\times n
matrix
U
whose entries are independently drawn from
N(0,1)
. For each
i\in[n]
, we take
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the vector with coordinates
(u_{i})_{j}=U_{ij}
.

2.

We take the problem variables to be the coefficients
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
such that
x=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{{v_{i}}u_{i}}
. Equivalently,
x_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{{v_{i}}{U_{ij}}}
.

3.

We observe that if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is in the span of the top
d
eigenvectors of
M
then
x^{T}M_{SK}x\geq\lambda_{d}||x||^{2}
where
\lambda_{d}
is the
d
th largest eigenvalue of
M_{SK}
. Moreover, SoS can prove this fact.

To relate this to NGCA, we observe that if we take
e^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to be the
j
th column of
U
then
x_{j}=v\cdot e^{\prime}_{j}
.

When
d\geq n^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}
where
\epsilon>0
and
n
is sufficiently large, the SoS lower bound for parallel affine planes
[
GJJ+20
]
gave pseudo-expectation values for
v
satisfying the equations that for all
i\in[d]
,
v_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{d}
and for all
j\in[n]
,
x_{j}^{2}=(v\cdot e^{\prime}_{j})^{2}=1
. Thus, this gave pseudo-expectation values for
x
satisfying the equation
x_{j}^{2}=1
for all
j\in[n]
such that
\tilde{E}[{x^{T}}Mx]\geq{\lambda_{d}}n\geq(2-\delta(d,n))n^{\frac{3}{2}}
where
\delta(d,n)
is
o(1)
(as a function of
n
) as long as
d
is
o(n)
.

When
d\geq n^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}
where
\epsilon>0
and
n
is sufficiently large, our SoS lower bound for NGCA gives pseudo-expectation values
\tilde{E}
for
v
satisfying the equations that for all
i\in[d]
,
v_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{d}
and approximately satisfying the equations that for all
j\in[n]
,
x_{j}^{2}=(v\cdot e^{\prime}_{j})^{2}=1
. In particular, if we take
A
to be a mixture of two Gaussian distributions with standard deviation
\delta
and means
-1
and
1
respectively then
\tilde{E}[(x_{j}^{2}-1)^{2k}]
will be
O(\delta^{2k})
where the constant is a function of
k
.

One way to handle this is as follows:

1.

Construct variables
x^{\prime}_{j}
which are polynomials in
v
and the entries of
U
such that
{x^{\prime}}_{j}^{2}
is even closer to
1
than
x_{j}^{2}
. For example, if we take
x^{\prime}_{j}=\frac{3x_{j}-x_{j}^{3}}{2}
then if
x_{j}=\pm{1}+\Delta
then

x^{\prime}_{j}=\frac{3x_{j}-x_{j}^{3}}{2}=\frac{\pm{3}+3\Delta\mp{1}-3\Delta\mp{3}\Delta^{2}-\Delta^{3}}{2}=\pm{1}-\frac{\pm{3}\Delta^{2}+\Delta^{3}}{2}

2.

Observe that
||x^{\prime}-x||^{2}
is small so
|\tilde{E}[{x^{\prime}}^{T}{M_{SK}}x^{\prime}]-\tilde{E}[{x}^{T}{M_{SK}}x]|
is small and thus
\tilde{E}[{x^{\prime}}^{T}{M_{SK}}x^{\prime}]\geq(2-\delta^{\prime}(d,n))n^{\frac{3}{2}}
where
\delta^{\prime}(d,n)
is
o(1)
(as a function of
n
) as long as
d
is
o(n)
and
\delta
is
o(1)
.

3.

Letting
M
be the moment matrix with the variables
\{x^{\prime}_{j}:j\in[n]\}
,
M\succeq 0
as these variables are polynomials of the solution variables
\{v_{i}:i\in[d]\}
and the entries of
U
. That said, we need to divide the SoS degree by the degree of these polynomials.

4.

To satisfy the constraints that
{x^{\prime}_{j}}^{2}=1
exactly, we need to adjust the moment matrix
M
. However, since we are only guaranteed that
M
is PSD rather than positive definite, there is a danger that this adjustment will break the PSDness of
M
. To handle this, we can modify
M
to obtain a moment matrix
M^{\prime}
which is positive definite rather than just positive semidefinite. Intuitively, we obtain this modification by randomizing each coordinate
x^{\prime}_{j}
with probability
\epsilon
. Note that this reduces the objective function by a factor of roughly
(1-\epsilon)^{2}

After obtaining the modified moment matrix
M^{\prime}
, we take our final moment matrix
M^{\prime\prime}
so that
M^{\prime\prime}_{AB}=M^{\prime}_{(A\setminus(A\cap B))(B\setminus(A\cap B))}
so that the equations
{x^{\prime}_{j}}^{2}=1
are satisfied.

We describe and analyze this adjustment in more detail in Section
D.1
below.

Remark D.1
.

If
\delta\leq n^{-CD}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
then we can skip the first step. That said, the first step allows us to take
\delta=\frac{1}{n^{c}}
for any constant
c>0
.

D.1
Adjusting the moment matrix

Definition D.2
.

Given
A,B\subseteq[n]
, the disjoint union
A\Delta B
of
A
and
B
is
A\Delta B=(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus A)
.

Definition D.3
.

Given
S\subseteq[n]
and
k\geq|S|
, we define
\mathrm{Id}_{S,k}
to be the diagonal matrix such that
(\mathrm{Id}_{S,k})_{AA}=1
if
S\subseteq A

and
|A|=k
and
0
otherwise. We define
\mathrm{Id}_{k}=\mathrm{Id}_{\emptyset,k}
.

Lemma D.4
.

If
M
is a matrix indexed by sets
A,B\subseteq[n]
of size at most
D
such that
M\succeq 0
, the diagonal entries of
M
are at least
\frac{1}{2}
, and all entries of
M
have magnitude at most
2
then if we take
M^{\prime}
to be the matrix with entries
M^{\prime}_{AB}=(1-\epsilon)^{|A\Delta B|}M_{AB}
for some
\epsilon>0
(which may depend on
D
and
n
),
M^{\prime}\succeq\frac{\epsilon^{D}}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{D}{\frac{1}{(32{D}n)^{D-k}}\mathrm{Id}_{k}}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=1-\epsilon
. Given
S\subseteq[n]
, let
M_{S}
be the matrix with entries
(M_{S})_{AB}={\alpha}^{|A\setminus S|+|B\setminus S|}M_{AB}
if
S\subseteq A\cap B
and
(M_{S})_{AB}=0
otherwise. Observe that
M^{\prime}=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}M_{S}}
. To see this, observe that for all
S^{\prime}\subseteq[n]
,
1=(\alpha^{2}+(1-\alpha^{2}))^{|S^{\prime}|}=\sum_{S\subseteq S^{\prime}}{{\alpha}^{2(|S^{\prime}|-|S|)}(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}}
. Thus, for all
A,B\subseteq[n]
of size at most
D
,

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}(M_{S})_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq A\cap B}{{\alpha}^{|A\setminus S|+|B\setminus S|}(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}M_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{|A\Delta B|}\sum_{S\subseteq A\cap B}{{\alpha}^{2(|A\cap B|-|S|)}(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}M_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{|A\Delta B|}M_{AB}=M^{\prime}_{AB}

We need to extract a small multiple of the identity from
\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}M_{S}}
. To do this, we use the following lemma.

Lemma D.5
.

For all
S\subseteq[n]
such that
|S|\leq D
,
M_{S}\succeq\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|}-\sum_{j=|S|+1}^{D}{(4n)^{j}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|+j}}
.

Proof.

We use the following trick. Let
M^{\prime}_{S}
be the matrix with entries
(M^{\prime}_{S})_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}(M_{S})_{AB}
if
A=B=S
,
(M^{\prime}_{S})_{AB}=(M_{S})_{AB}
if
S\subseteq A\cap B
and either
A=S
or
B=S
but not both,
(M^{\prime}_{S})_{AB}=2(M_{S})_{AB}
if
S\subseteq A\cap B
,
A\neq S
, and
B\neq S
, and
(M^{\prime}_{S})_{AB}=0
otherwise.

Since
M_{S}\succeq 0
and
M^{\prime}_{S}
is obtained from
M_{S}
by multiplying the rows and columns with index
S
by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
and multiplying the rows and columns with index
A
where
S\subsetneq A
by
\sqrt{2}
,
M^{\prime}_{S}\succeq 0
. Since
(M_{S})_{SS}\geq\frac{1}{2}
and the entries of
M_{S}
all have magnitude at most
2
, it is not hard to show that
M^{\prime}_{S}-M_{S}-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|}\preceq\sum_{j=1}^{D-|S|}{(4n)^{j}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|+j}}
. Rearranging and using the fact that
M^{\prime}_{S}\succeq 0
,
M_{S}\succeq\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|}-\sum_{j=|S|+1}^{D}{(4n)^{j}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|+j}}
, as needed.
∎

We now observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{(1-\alpha^{2})^{|S|}M_{S}}

\displaystyle\succeq\epsilon^{D}\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{\frac{1}{(32{D}n)^{D-|S|}}M_{S}}

\displaystyle\succeq\epsilon^{D}\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{\frac{1}{(32{D}n)^{D-|S|}}\left(\frac{1}{4}-\sum_{S^{\prime}\subsetneq S}{\left(\frac{4n}{32{D}n}\right)^{|S|-|S^{\prime}|}}\right)\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|}}

\displaystyle\succeq\epsilon^{D}\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|\leq D}{\frac{1}{8(32{D}n)^{D-|S|}}\mathrm{Id}_{S,|S|}}=\frac{\epsilon^{D}}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{D}{\frac{1}{(32{D}n)^{D-k}}\mathrm{Id}_{k}}.\qed

Corollary D.6
.

Under the same setup as Lemma
D.4
, if
M^{\prime\prime}
is a matrix indexed by subsets
A,B\subseteq[n]
of size at most
D
such that for all
A,B\subseteq[n]
,
|M^{\prime\prime}_{AB}-M^{\prime}_{AB}|\leq\frac{1}{16}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{32{D}n}\right)^{D}
then
M^{\prime\prime}\succeq 0
.
